Not only the sensory sensations that have constitutive function for the constitution of sense-things have immediately intuitive localization and body-relatedness grounded therein, but also sensations of entirely other groups: the “sensory” feelings, pleasure and pain sensations, the well-being streaming through and filling the whole body, the general malaise of “bodily indisposition,” and the like. Here belong groups of sensations that play an analogous role as stuff for the intentional experiences of the feeling-sphere, or for the constitution of values as their intentional correlates, as the primary sensations do for the intentional experiences of the experience-sphere, or for the constitution of spatial-thingly objects. Further belong here various hard-to-analyze sensations forming the material foundations of the desiring and willing life: sensations of energetic tension and relaxation, of inner inhibition, paralysis, liberation, and so on.

All these sensation-groups have as Empfindnisse an immediate bodily localization: they belong for every human being immediately intuitively to the body as his own body. With this stratum the intentional functions are connected: the stuffs receive spiritual formation, just as above the primary sensations receive apprehension, enter into perceptions, upon which perceptual judgments build, and so on. In this way the entire consciousness of a human being is in a certain way connected with its body through its hyletic foundation. But the intentional experiences themselves are no longer directly and properly localized; they no longer form a stratum at the body. Perception as touching apprehension of shape does not sit in the touching finger; thinking is not really intuitively localized in the head the way tension-Empfindnisse are.

### §40. More on the Localization of Empfindnisse and the Non-thingly Properties of the Body

Husserl asks how the constitution of the body through its Empfindnis-stratum is to be understood and what creates unity here. If an object moves mechanically touching over my skin surface, I have a determinately ordered sequence of Empfindnisse; if it always moves in the same way, the result is always the same. This is all “self-evident” — it lies in the apprehension: under such circumstances this body-body behaves in such a way that it is not merely excitable at all, but excitable in a determinate way under determinate circumstances. The extensional locality corresponds to a place-moment in the sensation, and the stimulus-strengths and stimulus-kinds to determinate moments that make the sensation concrete.

The sensibility of the body thus constitutes itself entirely as a “conditional” or psychophysical property. The body is apprehended not merely with the thing-apprehension but with the co-apprehension of sensation-fields, given as belonging in the manner of localization to the appearing body. The body stands there in concrete perception as a novel apprehension-unity, a physico-aesthesiological unity. It is constituted as its own objectuality fitting the formal-general concept of reality as a thing that preserves its identical properties over against changing external circumstances — but where the dependency-relations in which it stands to external nature are different from those of material things among themselves.

### §41. Constitution of the Body as Material Thing in Contrast to Other Material Things

Husserl has shown how, correlatively to the material world, a subject of bodily-psychical capacities constitutes itself, with the body appearing simultaneously as body and as material thing — but as a thing of a special kind.

#### a) The Body as Orientation-Center

Every ego has its thingly perceptual domain and necessarily perceives things in a certain orientation. Things appear from this or that side, and in this mode of appearance the reference to a Here and its basic directions is irremovably enclosed. All spatial being appears necessarily as nearer or farther, as above or below, as right or left. The body has for its ego the unique distinction that it bears the null-point of all these orientations within itself. One of its spatial points is constantly characterized in the mode of the ultimate central Here — a Here that has no other outside it in relation to which it would be a “There.” All things of the surrounding world have their orientation to the body. Thanks to its capacity for free movability, the subject can bring the system of its appearances and thereby the orientations into flux, but the form of the orientation-order with a center necessarily remains preserved.

#### b) Peculiarity of the Appearance-Manifolds of the Body

While I have the freedom to change my position relative to all other things and thereby to vary the appearance-manifolds arbitrarily, I do not have the possibility of removing myself from my body or it from me. Correspondingly, the appearance-manifolds of the body are restricted in a determinate way: certain body-parts I can see only in a peculiar perspectival foreshortening, and others (such as the head) are altogether invisible to me. The same body that serves me as the means of all perception stands in my way in the perception of itself and is a remarkably incompletely constituted thing.

#### c) The Body as Member of the Causal Context

If we nonetheless apprehend the body as a real thing, this is because we find it integrated into the causal context of material nature. Alongside the free kinaesthetic courses there appear others characterized not as “done” but as “done to,” as passive courses in which spontaneity has no part. In this case we simultaneously have an experience of the mechanical process of bodily movement and a givenness of this process in the “psychical” character of undergoing. Movements of my body are apprehended as mechanical processes like those of external things, the body itself as a thing that acts upon others and upon which others act.

### §42. Characterization of the Solipsistically Constituted Body

Summarizing: (1) Seen from “inside” — in “inner attitude” — the body appears as a freely movable organ (or system of such organs) by means of which the subject experiences the external world; further, as bearer of sensations and, thanks to the interweaving these enter with the rest of psychic life, as forming a concrete unity with the soul. (2) Seen from outside — in “outer attitude” — it stands there as a reality of its own kind: as a material thing of special modes of appearance, “switched in” between the rest of the material world and the “subjective” sphere; as center around which the remaining spatial world groups itself; as standing in causal relations to the real external world, but at the same time as “conversion point” where causal relations are converted into conditional ones between external world and bodily-psychical subject.

But in solipsistic experience we do not reach the givenness of ourselves as a spatial thing like all others, nor the natural object “human being” (animal being). To get there we must go beyond our own subject and turn to the animalia that encounter us in the external world.

## Fourth Chapter: The Constitution of Psychic Reality in Empathy

### §43. Givenness of Other Animalia

In experience, in the sphere of original constitution, what is originally given is the multiplicity of things in space and time in manifold appearances, and among them animals, including human beings (“rational” living beings): not as connections of separately given items, but as double-unities, unities distinguishing two strata within themselves — unities of things and subjects with their psychic life. With the human-apperception is eo ipso also given the possibility of mutual relations, of communication between human and human, and the identity of nature for all human beings and animals.

### §44. Ur-presence and Appresence

Husserl introduces a fundamental distinction between Urpräsenz (original presence) and Appräsenz (appresence). An experience in which a subject has the perceived object given in original presence means that the object stands there “actually,” “in the original,” and is not merely “co-presented.” Appresence points back to original presence. The objects of perception then divide into: (1) those that can be originally present not only for one subject but, when originally present for one, can ideally be originally given for every other subject — the totality of possibly originally present objects forming a domain of common original presence is nature in the first and most originary sense, the spatio-temporal-material nature; (2) the subjective over against the objective — the individually unique, temporal, total stock of what is originally present that can be given as originally present to only one subject. Therein belongs every subject itself with all its acts, states, noematic correlates, and also with the bodiliness and properties that constitute themselves for it in inner attitude.

### §45. Animalia as Originally Present Body-Bodies with Appresent Interiority

The originally experienceable, the being presentable in original presence, is not all being, not even all experienceable being. Realities that cannot be given in original presence for several subjects are the animalia: they contain subjectivities. They are peculiar objectualities whose original givenness presupposes original presences while they themselves cannot be given in original presence. The externally given bodies I experience in original presence as I do other things; the interiority of the psychical, through appresence.

I find in my physical surrounding world bodies — material things of the type of the material thing “my body” constituted in solipsistic experience — and I apprehend them as bodies, that is, I empathize into each an ego-subject with everything belonging to it. Thereupon, the system of appresentations that has its original template in the solipsistic subject in original connections of regular coexistence grows, through continued experience of other humans already constituted through empathy, into a system of ordered indications. To the appearance of the other human being there belongs, beyond what was mentioned, also the psychical act-interiority.

### §46. Significance of Empathy for the Constitution of the Reality “Ego-Human-Being”

Under the title “other human being” we have a body in unity with sense-fields and soul-fields, with a subject of acts. This belonging obtains of course also for myself. Originally given to me in my own case were pieces of these belongingnesses; the rest is then empirical extension, transference in experiential thinking. But in the attitude of “self-experience,” it would not occur to me to seriously stuff all my psychical life into my body, to “introject” it. Only with empathy and with the constant direction of experiential consideration upon the psychic life appresented with the other body and constantly taken objectively together with it does the closed unity “human being” constitute itself, and this I then transfer to myself.

As for the experience of others: every human being stands there bodily in the spatial context, among things, and to every body belongs its entire and determinately empathized psychic life, so that when the body moves and is at ever new places, its soul also “moves along” as it were: it is constantly one with the body. But the soul is nowhere, and its connection with the body is grounded only through functional connections: the body is “organ” of the subject. The “being-somewhere” of the human being has its good sense, but “being regularly coordinated and thereby localized” and “being in space itself” are two different things. That body and soul form a peculiar experiential unity and that the psychical obtains its place in space and time by virtue of this unity — therein consists the legitimate “naturalization” of consciousness.

### §47. Empathy and Nature-Constitution

Empathy leads to the constitution of the intersubjective objectivity of the thing and thereby also of the human being, in that now the physical body is a natural-scientific object. The natural-scientific object is the x of “mathematical” qualities, toward whose substructing the causal analysis and the thinking grounded upon it compel. The seen, actually experienced things with their actually experienced causalities proved themselves, in their intuitive qualities, to be subjectively conditioned. So the human being now has, as natural-scientific object, a peculiar structure: the physical body is mathematical (“theoretical”) substructions pointing back to the “merely subjective” appearances; the higher stratum is the subjectivity localized in the substructed mathematical spatial body — the soul with its consciousness-experiences.

The analysis of nature and naturalistic consideration shows that it requires supplementation, that it harbors presuppositions within itself and thus points beyond itself to another domain of being and research: the field of subjectivity that is no longer nature.

## Third Section: The Constitution of the Spiritual World

Four confusions run through the reception of this section, and its argument is built to prevent them. The personalistic attitude is not a philosophical or artificial standpoint; it is the attitude of ordinary life, and the naturalistic attitude is the acquired one. "Surrounding world" (Umwelt) never means the physical environment; it means the world as the person has it in view, and it changes as the person's knowledge changes. Motivation is not a species of causation, and the "stimulus" (Reiz) a thing exerts on a person is not the stimulus a physical excitation exerts on a nerve. And the priority of spirit over nature that closes the section in §64 is a claim about the dependence of sense, not a denial that there is a nature or a demotion of natural science within its limits. A fifth guard concerns two words. "Spirit" (Geist) names the person as subject of a surrounding world; "soul" (Seele) names the same subjectivity as nature apprehends it, a stratum at a body. They are not two entities but two apprehensions, and the section's work is to show which apprehension presupposes which.

### §48. Introduction

Husserl states the section's task in one paragraph (Hua IV 172–173). A group of distinctions — nature against spiritual world, natural science against human science (Geisteswissenschaft), natural-scientific psychology against the theory of personality and the theory of community — all descend from one, the distinction of soul from spirit, and that one has to be clarified, because a whole worldview turns on it. He then places himself in the dispute over the human sciences. The naturalistic reading of those sciences as descriptive natural sciences, self-evident to a scientific age, has provoked reactions for decades, and Dilthey earns first place: he saw the essential differences and saw that a natural science of the psychic cannot ground the concrete human sciences, which need a general science of spirit that is neither psychophysical nor natural-scientific. Husserl's assessment cuts both ways. Dilthey had intuition but not rigorous theory; he saw the problems and the direction of the work, not the formulations and the methodically secured solutions. That Ebbinghaus's elegant critique could dissolve only Dilthey's execution and not stop the development proves the strength of the insight; Windelband, Rickert, Simmel, and Münsterberg took the contrast up from new sides without reaching conclusive clarification. The sentence that closes the paragraph is the section's program: only a radical investigation directed at the phenomenological sources from which the ideas nature, body, soul, and the several ideas of ego and person are constituted can settle the matter and, at the same time, do justice to the motives of all these earlier efforts. The dispute is thereby relocated. It is not a dispute about two regions of what there is but about two ways in which objects are constituted, and only an inquiry into constitution can adjudicate it. The plural "ideas of ego and person" already points to §§57–61, where pure ego, personal ego, and naturalized soul are separated. A footnote refers to an alternative draft of this introduction (Beilage IV), a trace of how often Husserl reworked the entry into this section.

## First Chapter: Contrast Between the Naturalistic and Personalistic World

### §49. The Personalistic Attitude in Contrast to the Naturalistic

Husserl connects the new analyses to the findings of the two preceding sections, carried out in the naturalistic attitude, and explains how such analyses become purely phenomenological at all (Hua IV 174). While one lives in an attitude, the attitude itself is not in one's field; only what is experienced in it is. Phenomenological reflection and reduction make the attitude the theme, relate to it what was investigated within it, and, with eidetic reduction and the purging of all transcending apperceptions, turn the whole inquiry into a pure one. The subject of the naturalistic attitude then shows itself to be the pure ego. The order of discovery matters: reflecting, we first find ourselves as an empirical ego, that is, we perform the reflection as one more naturalistic attitude, which itself belongs in the brackets; the last subject, which no exclusion touches and which is the subject of all eidetic-phenomenological research, is the pure ego. Nothing excluded is lost; the whole world of the naturalistic attitude, nature in the widest sense, is retained in bracketed modification. An attitude cannot see itself, and that is why the naturalistic attitude has been able to take its correlate for the world without noticing that a correlate is what it is.

Retained as pure sense, as the constitutive correlate of the acts that make up the naturalistic attitude, this nature shows a structure Husserl now recapitulates (174). A single founding positing runs through it, the positing of nature in the first sense, physical nature, from which everything else called nature draws its sense as something founded in it. The constituting kinds of apprehension are layered accordingly: physical experience at the base; resting on it and including it, the experience of the body that constitutes human and animal; belonging to the latter as a constitutive stratum, the experience of the soul. The system of naturalistic experiences spans "all-nature," the total domain of the natural sciences in the narrower and the wider sense. This is the architecture of Sections One and Two compressed into a sentence, and it is the architecture the present section inverts: what is founded here, the soul, will turn out in its personal form to be what nature itself presupposes.

Psychology belongs in this system as the natural science of the psychic being of animals, human and non-human, and so ranks under anthropology and zoology (175). Psychic being includes the psychic ego-subject, the ego-subject as nature. Here Husserl names the difficulty the chapter works on: to this ego attach reservations, because a distinction keeps pressing itself on us and keeps failing to establish itself, the distinction between this ego and the ego as person and member of the social world. The ego appears twice, as a natural fact and as a person, and neither ordinary experience nor psychology can hold the two apart. The distinction is to be pursued by phenomenological-constitutive research, not by stipulation.

#### a) Introjection of the Soul as Presupposition Also for the Naturalistic Attitude

Husserl begins from the soul as naturalism takes it (175). Natural-scientifically the soul is nothing for itself, a mere stratum of real occurrences at bodies. In material nature — the objectively true nature, relative to which the nature that appears to me and to everyone, the thing-world with its secondary qualities, is mere appearance — certain things stand out by a stratum of real properties that are not physical, not material or extensive: excitability, sensibility. These properties constitute themselves in the form of localization and are by their sense dependent on physical bodiliness and through it on physical nature as a whole. The specifically psychic, a higher stratum, is experienced in a way similar to the aesthesiological, as a stratum at the body-thing, since it too is in a certain manner localized. The soul animates the body, and the ensouled body is a natural object within the one spatiotemporal world. Everything hangs on the operator "localization" and on the hedge attached to it, "in a certain manner"; the next paragraph pays the hedge out.

The example is a cat at play, seen as the zoologist sees it (176). I see a physical organism, but I also see a sensing and ensouled body, a cat, and I see it in the ordinary sense of seeing. Husserl blocks the objection that animal experience falls short of perception. A stone is seen through an appearance of which very little falls into actual perception; if incompleteness disqualified seeing, the word would lose its sense, since no physical thing is ever seen otherwise — physical perception includes indeterminacies, but as determinabilities. The cat is seen in its own way, with the imperfections proper to animal experience, and it stands there in the flesh as a physical thing with sensing surfaces and sense organs. The sensation-stratum is not there beside the physical thing; one body has physical and aesthesiological properties in one. The body is likewise experienced as the body of a soul, and "soul" names a still more highly founded stratum of properties. That stratum is not spread over the body in genuine localization; it is not a complex of soul-fields, imagined on the analogy of sense-fields, that could be brought point by point into coincidence with extended parts of the body. Yet the psychic is experientially one, really one, with the body, and so is something at or in it, only without a distinguishable locality. Husserl proposes the admittedly misleading word "introjection" for exactly this. The term is Avenarius's name for a fallacy, the projecting of experiences into other bodies; Husserl takes it over as the name of a sense-structure that naturalistic apperception legitimately performs. In objective space, here and now, this objectively real cat appears; it is physical and moves physically like other things, only that beyond its physical properties it persistently has aesthesiological and psychic ones. The surplus of reality over the mere physical thing is nothing separable, nothing beside it but at it; it moves with it and gains its spatial determinacy by being at what is spatial. The psychic has a place by attachment, not by extension, and that is the whole of its naturalistic spatiality.

We can turn the analyzing regard onto these psychic properties as onto any others, and they lift off as a stratum, a really inseparable annex of the physical body — inseparable, since the body is thinkable without them only under the change called organic death (177). None of this is invention; it is the pure unfolding of what lies in the sense of the naturalistic apprehension "animal reality," an apprehension that determines a priori the sense of every zoological judgment, since zoological concepts draw their meaning from it. What is enclosed a priori in the sense of zoological experience "makes" zoological science possible in the same way the content enclosed in the essence-type "physical experience" makes physical science possible; strike either type of experience and its concepts, judgments, and sciences vanish with it. The method is sense-explication, and its yield is a criterion: a science is possible only as the elaboration of a type of experience. §64 applies the criterion to the naturalization of spirit.

#### b) Localization of the Psychical

Every stratum is a constituted unity, and the material of a constitution can be considered in its own essence once the forming, realizing apprehension is set aside (178). Direct the regard at the sensation-material and drop what the apprehension of it as a sensing of the body adds; direct it at the unity of the experience-stream and drop the apprehension in which the stream stands as the state of an experiencing animal thing. In the manifold that then presents itself, unities are found that are no longer nature-unities. With this change of attitude, this detaching of what was apprehended as psychic from the physical body, every ordering into the objective world, into world-space and world-time, is lost. The soul is in the body and is where the body now is, and so are its groups of states, presentations, thoughts, judgments; as acts and states of this cat, which has its place through its body, everything really one with the cat has its place, though it has no extension of its own. As soon as consciousness loses the apperceptive apprehension as psychic state, as stratum at the body, as soon as it is posited purely as itself in phenomenological, if not yet eidetic, reduction, its empirical ordering into objective space lapses. Husserl puts the point through the thought-experiment of Ideas I §49: consciousness in itself, this cogitatio in its nexus, is thinkable without a nature; the nature-apperception can be posited in itself as "this here," and yet it is thinkable that the positing of nature it contains receives no confirmation at all, that there is no nature. Then there is no objective space either, consciousness cannot be posited as a natural being, the state of an animal, and it is absolutely unspatial. The argument concerns the dependence of sense: consciousness has a location only through an apperception that lends it one, and the reduction exhibits the loan by suspending it. "Absolutely unspatial" does not relocate consciousness somewhere else; it says that place is not among its own determinations.

#### c) Temporalization of the Psychical (Immanent Time and Space-Time)

Time repeats the analysis with one asymmetry (179). Pure consciousness is its own time-field, phenomenological time, not to be confused with the objective time that constitutes itself together with nature. Through the psychic apprehension, consciousness-experiences acquire the sense of psychophysical states and with it their ordering into objective time, the form of objective nature; to localization corresponds temporalization. But the immanent phenomenological time of the stream is a one-dimensional continuous manifold with properties exactly analogous to the time that presents itself, "appears," in the experiences of physical perception, and it coincides with that time point for point; and since in appearing time the "absolute" world-time announces itself in the final objectivation, the temporalization of consciousness-time goes especially deep — consciousness-time coincides, in a way, perfectly with absolute time. The localization of the tactile field could not achieve this, because a field without three dimensions cannot coincide in the same regulated way with the three-dimensional extension of the body. Hence objective physical time-measurement, which belongs exclusively to the material world, transfers to consciousness: consciousness-states now have a time that can be measured and documented, in accordance with the constitutive sense of the coincidence of their time with the time of physical nature. Husserl adds that this confirmation must be read in exactly the sense the situation prescribes, and that one underlays it with something psychologically false if one does not make that sense fully clear; the full explication is not the present task. Temporalization goes deeper than localization because the two times are isomorphic, and that is why the psychologist's clocks measure something real while the sense of what they measure is borrowed. The analyses of inner time-consciousness stand behind the paragraph; §64 draws on the same isomorphism for the individuation of spirits.

#### d) Methodical Reflection

Husserl pauses to say what the foregoing has used and what it is good for (180). The right of phenomenological reduction has been exercised, and what liberates in this fundamental method of original sense-determination is that it frees us from the sense-limits of the natural attitude and so of every relative attitude. The natural human being, and above all the natural scientist, does not notice these limits, does not notice that all his results carry an index marking their merely relative sense, does not notice that the natural attitude is not the only one possible and leaves open turns of regard through which the absolute, nature-constituting consciousness emerges, relative to which, by the essential correlation of constituting and constituted, all nature must be relative. The reduction's educative value lies also in making us receptive to attitude-changes of equal rank with the natural — or, as Husserl now says more precisely, the naturalistic — attitude, which like it constitute only relative and limited correlates of being and sense. These new correlates likewise point back to absolute consciousness, and their full clarification demands the regress to its original and complete essential nexus; through reduction to that nexus we are always able to set the givennesses of the different attitudes, with their fundamentally different modes of apperception, into the right relation to one another and to evaluate them absolutely. The paragraph fixes the standpoint from which the section's verdict on the two attitudes will be delivered. Neither attitude can adjudicate the other: the naturalistic cannot see its own index, and the personalistic, as §64 will grant, cannot by itself refute naturalization. Only the standpoint outside both can rank them, and the ranking to come is therefore a phenomenological finding, not the human sciences' plea on their own behalf.

The attitude now aimed at is very natural in one sense but not natural in the scientific one — "not natural" meaning that what is experienced in it is not nature in the sense of the natural sciences but, as Husserl puts it, a counterpart of nature (180). The difficulty is not in performing the attitudes. Apart from the admittedly artificial attitude toward pure consciousness, the residue of the reductions, we glide effortlessly from one attitude into the other, from the naturalistic into the personalistic, and in the corresponding sciences from the natural-scientific into the human-scientific. The difficulty lies in reflection and in the phenomenological understanding of the changes of apprehension and experience and of the correlates they constitute. Only within phenomenology, by relating the differences of being among constituted objects to the essential nexuses of the constituting manifolds, can these differences be kept unconfused and free of the misinterpretations whose source is involuntary and, without pure reflection, imperceptible changes of attitude; only by the regress to absolute consciousness are the relativities of the objectualities of the one and the other attitude, and their mutual essential relations, to be understood. This is Husserl's diagnosis of the dispute over method: its confusions are the residue of unnoticed gliding between attitudes. And "counterpart" is exact, because the personal world is not a second region beside nature but nature's mirror-image, constituted from the same absolute consciousness by a different apperception.

#### e) Naturalistic and Natural Attitude

A one-line paragraph opens the investigation proper.

What "human being and human soul as nature" means is now held fixed, the human standing in for the animal in general (180–181). There is no difficulty in the human soul's having, among its psychic states, ego-states, occurrences of the type cogito. Like everything psychic, they are attached to or laid into the physically appearing body in naturalistic experience, localized and temporalized with it, and belong to the nexus of real, substantial-causal nature; and this concerns the empirical ego that lives in those states. This human being there sees and hears and on the basis of his perceptions performs these judgments, valuations, and volitions in manifold succession. That an "I think" surfaces in him is a natural fact, founded in the body and in bodily events, determined by the substantial-causal nexus of a nature that is not merely physical nature, though physical nature grounds and co-determines all other nature. He falls into dreamless sleep or a faint on physical grounds, and the "I think" drops out of the stream of his psychic events. In the course of these experiences, which are his natural states with real relations to determining real circumstances, dispositions, character traits, and knowledge announce themselves, and these are natural-real properties of the real human being, to be investigated by natural-scientific induction as physical properties are. Naturalism is granted its strongest form: even the cogito is a natural fact, sleep and fainting are the test, since the ego drops out as any state does, and character becomes an inductively ascertainable property. This is the account of the ego that §§57–60 set against the person's own self-apperception.

Among these psychic states are also the acts in which the human being is conscious of himself, of his fellows, and of the surrounding real world, sees them before him, takes note of them, relates to them in lower or higher thinking or in the modes of feeling and will (182): the acts in which he does physics, psychology, history, or acts in practical life, takes the things of his surrounding world into use, reshapes them to his ends, values them by aesthetic, ethical, or utilitarian standards, or enters into communicative relation with his fellows, speaks with them, writes letters, reads of them in the newspaper, joins with them in common deeds, gives promises. Countless remarkable relations between the subject and its surrounding world belong here, all grounded in the fact that the human being "knows" of himself, of his fellows, and of a surrounding world common to them all. That world contains not mere things but objects of use — clothes, utensils, weapons, tools — works of art, literary products, the instruments of religious and legal acts, seals, chains of office, insignia, church symbols; and not only individual persons, since persons are members of communities, personal unities of higher order that lead their life as wholes, persist through the entry and departure of individuals, have their communal properties, their moral and legal orders, their ways of functioning with other communities and individuals, their dependence on circumstances, their regulated changeability, their ways of developing or holding constant. The members of marriage and family, estate, association, municipality, state, and church "know" themselves as members, find themselves consciously dependent on the community and possibly acting back on it. The inventory is still being drawn up inside the naturalistic frame, which can list all of this, and the quotation marks around "knows" mark what the frame cannot make sense of: the relation that founds every item on the list is an intentional relation of knowing, not a causal registration, and the higher-order unities are unities of sense, not aggregates of bodies.

As soon as we vividly presentify any of these personal relations and live ourselves into their bearers, and then put the manner of their givenness under the phenomenological lens, we notice that we are in an essentially different attitude from the naturalistic one just exercised (183). In that one the "objective" physical total-nature was and is there for us, with bodies scattered through it founding sensibilities and psychic lives; all the humans and animals we consider in it are, for theoretical interests, anthropological or zoological objects — Husserl proposes "physio-psychic," reversing the usual "psychophysical" so that the order of founding can be heard — and this holds of ourselves as much as of anyone whenever we consider ourselves theoretically in that attitude: ensouled bodies, natural objects, themes of the natural sciences. Wholly different is the personalistic attitude, in which we always are when we live with one another, speak to one another, shake hands in greeting, are related to one another in love and aversion, in disposition and deed, in speech and reply, and in which we likewise are when we regard the things around us as our surroundings and not, as in natural science, as "objective" nature. It is a thoroughly natural attitude, not an artificial one that would first have to be won and secured by special means. In natural ego-life we do not see the world always, or even predominantly, naturalistically, as if we wanted to do physics and zoology, as if our theoretical and extra-theoretical interest had to be directed at the psychic as founded in the body and causally interwoven with it. Even the zoologist and the naturalistic psychologist do not always see it so; they have only acquired the fixed habit, whose limits they can as a rule no longer break, of taking the naturalistic form — equivalently, the form directed at "objective" actuality — as soon as they take any scientific attitude at all. They wear habitual blinkers: as researchers they see only "nature," but as persons they live like everyone else and "know" themselves constantly as subjects of their surrounding world. To live as a person is to posit oneself as a person, to find oneself in conscious relations to a surrounding world and to bring oneself into such relations. The paragraph carries the section's first decisive reversal: the naturalistic attitude is not the default but a habit, and the habit of identifying scientific attitude with naturalistic attitude is what makes the human sciences look like second-rate natural sciences. It also supplies the definition on which the chapter runs — a person is a subject that posits itself as subject of a surrounding world.

On closer examination it will even turn out that the two attitudes are not of equal rank, nor two equally legitimate apperceptions interpenetrating one another (184). The naturalistic attitude subordinates itself to the personalistic and gains a certain independence only through an abstraction, or rather through a kind of self-forgetfulness on the part of the personal ego, and in doing so it illegitimately absolutizes its world, nature. The thesis of the section is stated in advance, and §§62–64 argue it. "Subordinates" is not "is false": the abstraction is legitimate, the absolutizing is the error, and self-forgetfulness names the mechanism by which a legitimate abstraction is mistaken for the whole.

A second one-line paragraph proposes to gain clarity here.

Husserl first restates what naturalism can rightly say about persons (185). We listed the person and its personal modes of behavior earlier as components of human nature, and naturalistically regarded all consciousness, all experiencing whatever, is bodily founded, hence also the entire stock of what, in persons, intentionally constitutes the world with all its properties. The person sees things around her, and all these thing-apprehensions and thing-positings with their stocks of noetic stuff and form are psychic, physio-psychic; she values the thing, apprehends it as beautiful or useful, as a garment or a cup, and as such it stands there for her perceptually; the apperceptions that go back to the sphere of feeling and will and everything belonging to them — sensuous feelings and drives, the modes of valuing and practical consciousness related to things — belong, in the consideration of nature, phenomenally to the human being as ensouled body, and so do all social acts. The whole of sociality concerns the psychologist and the natural scientist, but only as a totality of physical bodies with their psychic foundings, among which, by the mediate route such foundings prescribe, interhuman causalities are also established: interphysical causal relations make possible, through the excitations distributed to the several bodies and the aesthesiological-psychic events functionally tied to them, the real occurrence of psychic experiences of "mutual understanding" and, further, of "social consciousness." All of these are special natural facts, no different from other facts of perception and knowledge that occur in one with the body, with such content that the ego belonging to the body is conscious of things as oriented to it and appearing in such and such qualities. All the facts of personality thus appear, in the naturalistic way of considering them, as natural facts and demand as such their natural-scientific treatment, which finally leads into the domain of physical nature and is psychophysical in the good original sense; the personal psychic data form only a part of the psychic and, more generally, of the natural data, and from nature's standpoint everything personal is subordinate. The concession is complete and deliberate. Naturalism is granted a causal account even of mutual understanding and social consciousness, by way of inter-bodily excitation. The argument to come will not be that naturalism fails to cover the facts; it will be that this coverage has its sense on loan from the attitude it subordinates.

### §50. The Person as Center of a Surrounding World

The counter-move is to bring personal subjectivity itself to intuitive givenness, to live into it wholly (185). Then, as already indicated, it will become noticeable of itself that the attitude in which we live in the personal world, as subjects in a world of subjects, is essentially other than the naturalistic, and that an apperceptive turn is in fact required in order to thematize the personal naturally. The phenomena of personality are to be followed and described.

As a person I am what I am, and every other person is what she is, as subject of a surrounding world (185). The concepts ego and surrounding world are inseparably related: every person has her surrounding world, while several communicating persons have a common one. The surrounding world is the world perceived, remembered, grasped in thought, surmised or inferred by the person in her acts, the world of which this personal ego is conscious, which is there for it, to which it relates in one way or another — thematically experiencing and theorizing with respect to the things that appear to it, or feeling, valuing, acting, shaping technically. The definition sets the term for the whole section. Umwelt is not an environment; it is a correlate, and the person is defined through it and it through the person.

The person "behaves" toward that world in acts on which it can reflect, as it does when it takes notice of itself as a personal ego, and on which every other reflects too, in a correspondingly modified way, in the reflection of empathy, whenever he grasps these acts as acts of this person, for instance whenever he speaks of her with clear understanding as a person (185–186). The person is presenting, feeling, valuing, striving, acting person, and in every such personal act stands in relation to something, to objects of its surrounding world. Two things are secured at once: the person's acts are accessible to reflection, its own and the other's, and every personal act is object-related, so that the surrounding world is coextensive with the person's acts and not with any physical container.

It follows that physical reality is not simply and in general the actual surrounding world of a person, but only so far as she "knows" of it, grasps it by apperception and positing, or has it consciously co-given and ready to be grasped in her horizon of existence — clearly or obscurely, determinately or indeterminately, as her positing consciousness happens to be (186). If she knows nothing of the discoveries of physics, the world does not belong to her actual surrounding world with the sense-content of physics, and the same holds for the psychological sphere and the discoveries of psychology. Quite generally the surrounding world is no world "in itself" but a world "for me," the surrounding world of its ego-subject, experienced or otherwise conscious, posited in its intentional experiences with a respective sense-content; and as such it is in a way always in becoming, constantly producing itself anew through changes of sense and ever new formations of sense with their positings and cancellations. Two consequences are set down that the later Husserl develops at length: the surrounding world has a horizon-structure, the co-given and the ready-to-grasp, and it has a history, since it is the sediment of the person's own positings and cancellations. The same physical reality is therefore a different surrounding world for a physicist and for someone who has never heard of physics, and neither is the world "in itself."

At its core the world is first a sensibly appearing world, characterized as on hand, given in simple experiential intuitions and possibly actually grasped; and to this experiential world the ego finds itself related in new acts, valuing acts, acts of liking and disliking (186–187). In them the object is conscious as valuable, agreeable, beautiful, and in different ways — in original givenness, for instance, where on the basis of mere intuitive presenting a valuing builds itself up which, in the immediacy of its living motivation, plays the role of a value-"perception," a value-taking (Wertnehmung), in which the value-character is originally and intuitively self-given. Hearing the tone of a violin, I have its pleasingness, its beauty, given originarily when the tone moves my heart originally and livingly; the beauty as such is given originally in the medium of this pleasing, and so is the mediate value of the violin as producer of such a tone, insofar as we see it bowed and grasp intuitively the founding causal relation, and so too the beauty of its outward build, its elegant form, where the details and connections that motivate the pleasure really stand out in the unity of the constituting intuition and exercise their motivating force. But value-consciousness can also have the mode of a non-originary pleasing and of an evaluation of the pleasing as such without the heart being "originally" touched — the analogue, in the sphere of feeling, of obscure as against clear presentations. When I find a violin "beautiful" and "a work of art" at first sight, the pleasing is imperfect, if it gives the beauty itself at all; I can see the violin, finding it beautiful, without my heart being "properly" stirred. Value-taking, introduced in §4 of the First Section, receives its full analysis here: the feeling-sphere has its own perception, its own originary and non-originary modes, and its own equivalents of empty and fulfilled intention, and value is given in the medium of feeling, not inferred from it. The word "motivating," used of the details that move the pleasure within the intuition itself, is the intuitive sense of the term that §56 raises into the law of the spiritual world.

The objects of desiring and practical acts follow the same pattern (188). Experienced objects, as objects of this experiential sense, stimulate my desire or satisfy needs relative to consciously constituted circumstances — the recurrent need for food, say — and afterwards become apprehensible as serviceable, by this or that property, to the satisfaction of such needs; they then stand there, in the apprehension itself, as foodstuffs, as use-objects of some kind, fuel, hoes, hammers. I see coal as fuel; I recognize it as serving and suited and destined to produce warmth. Husserl slows down to show how much is layered into that seeing. I see something burn or glow, step nearer, warmth radiates from it; I know this experientially, and the object is "warm" even when I feel no degrees of warmth, because warmth is an objective property that announces itself in sensations of warmth and in apprehensions of the object's radiating warmth — an unchanged, always equal warmth of the object, while I, stepping nearer or farther, have unequal sensations of it. I learn further that the object comes to glow by friction or by being lit at a body already burning: it is "combustible" material, at first without practical relation. Now I can use it as fuel; it is valuable to me as a possible spreader of warmth, or valuable in relation to my producing the warming of a room and thereby pleasant sensations of warmth for myself and others; under this aspect I apprehend it — I "can use it for that," it is useful to me for that; others apprehend it so too, and it acquires an intersubjective use-value, esteemed and estimable in the social association as serviceable, as useful to human beings, and is now immediately "regarded" as such; and then further as a "commodity" held for sale. The paragraph is a genesis. A property of the thing, warmth, is constituted, exactly as Section One constituted objective properties, through the variation of subjective sensations; a capacity, combustibility, is constituted on it; usefulness for me, usefulness for us, and commodity-status are constituted on that, each founded on the last and each becoming a character the object itself wears in perception, so that I see the coal as fuel and the coal as goods. Nothing in the series is inferred; every level is a new apperception. A reader of Heidegger will recognize the analysis of equipment; here it stands as an analysis of the person's surrounding world, and its levels are levels of founded objectivation.

All of these are founded objects, constituted for the ego in apprehension by founded acts in the way described, and constituted in turn either in an originarily giving consciousness by such acts or in one that does not give, in fully developed, proper acts or in improper ones (188). The distinction of proper and improper, originary and non-originary, carries over from value-taking to the whole practical sphere.

Once such founded acts have been carried out in whatever modes, the objects endowed with value-characters, desire-characters, and practical characters are for the ego again objects of its surrounding world, toward which it can behave in new personal acts: it values them as more or less good or bad, fit or unfit for their purpose; it reshapes them, they become its "products," its "makings," and as such enter the ego's surrounding world once more, valued as works, perhaps as beauties, perhaps serving as workpieces for new products, which in turn function in position-takings and found new strata of apprehension, new objectivations (188). The subject thus continually carries out acts of various levels and of every possible kind, from which, by suitable turns, ever new and ever higher objectivations can grow; living in them, it has the objects constituted at each stage as its "objects," which now determine its further behavior and are conscious to it, as it behaves, as determining. The surrounding world is thereby shown to be iterative and historical: the person's own acts sediment into objects that then solicit new acts, and "determining" already names, before the word appears, the relation of motivation.

Husserl now draws the contrast the whole section turns on (190). The relation of the human being as personal subject to the objectualities of its surrounding world has a fundamentally new sense as against the natural relation between the naturally understood human being, the psychophysical reality, and other realities. From the physical natural object, one says, "stimuli" proceed: sense-nerves are stimulated by physical excitations, and then the soul too is called stimulated, the excitations propagating through the nervous system terminating in a final excitation in the central nervous system that functions in turn as "stimulus" for the soul and makes it react in sensation. But on the ground of the intentional subject-object relation, the relation between person and surrounding world, the concept of stimulus takes on a fundamentally new sense. In place of the causal relation between things and human beings as natural realities steps the motivation-relation between persons and things — and these things are not the things in themselves of nature, of exact natural science with the determinations it alone allows as objectively true, but experienced, thought, or otherwise positingly meant things as such, intentional objectualities of personal consciousness. "Stimuli" proceed from the things that "lie in the sense" for the personal ego as really existing. Phenomenologically, the thing-unities, the noematic unities, are starting points of more or less "strong" tendencies: already as conscious but not yet grasped, hovering in the background, they draw the subject toward themselves, and at sufficient "stimulus-strength" the ego "follows" the stimulus, "yields," turns toward, and then exercises on them its explicating, comprehending, theoretically judging, valuing, practical activities. They engage its interest by their being or so-being, their beauty, agreeableness, usefulness; they arouse its desire to enjoy them, play with them, use them as means, reshape them to purposes; at ever new levels they function as stimuli for its doing — or, not to forget the negative, for its suffering. The motivation-subject can yield to stimuli and can also resist them, and all of these are phenomenological relations that are to be found and described only in the purely intentional sphere. In a widest sense the personal or motivational attitude can therefore also be called the practical: it is always a matter of the active or the passive ego, in the proper and inner sense of those words. "Stimulus" is used twice with two senses, and their confusion is the confusion the section exists to prevent: a physical excitation causes a reaction in a nerve; a noematic unity solicits an ego, which yields or resists, and only the second is a relation between a person and its world. The affective pull of the not-yet-grasped, the "yielding" of the ego, and the graded "strength" of tendencies are the first sketch of what the analyses of passive synthesis and Experience and Judgment (§17) treat as affection and ego-turning. That the personal attitude is the practical attitude in the widest sense is the definition that returns in §60's "free ego."

### §51. The Person in the Personal Association

The subject finds in its surrounding world not only things but other subjects, and sees them as persons, active in their own surrounding world, determined by their objects and ever again determinable (190). In this attitude it does not occur to it to lay the spirit into the body — to regard the spirit as something at the body, founded in it, belonging with it to one reality — that is, to perform the natural real apperception. If we do perform it, the human being is himself posited as a thing, and the spirit as person, of equal rank with our own person as a member of the personal association, does not come into its right; it functions only as psychic being in the sense of the consideration of nature, causally dependent on the body onto which it appears grafted. The sentence recalls introjection from §49 a in order to say where it does not happen: the personalistic attitude does not introject, it takes the other as counter-subject, and "grafted" is Husserl's word for what the other becomes when one does.

To "treat humans and animals as mere things" has different senses, a legal and moral one and a scientific one, but they share something (191). Morally and practically I treat a human being as a mere thing when I do not take him as a moral person, as a member of the moral association of persons in which a moral world constitutes itself; I do not treat him as a subject of right when I take him not as a member of the legal community to which we both belong but as rightless, like a mere thing. Analogously, I treat a human being theoretically as a thing when I do not order him into the association of persons in relation to which we are subjects of a common surrounding world, but treat him as a mere annex of natural objects as pure things and hence as himself thingly. This has its right within certain limits, and it becomes a wrong only when I fail to recognize two things: that the naturalization of persons and souls can bring to knowledge only certain relations of dependence in objective existence and succession that hold between the natural world of things and personal spirits insofar as both belong to the unity of the objective spatiotemporal world of realities; and that spirits make possible and demand another and more significant mode of research, with respect to their having their own being as ego-subjects and being required, as such, as counter-members for all thingliness and all research into things, relating in their intentional life to the world in the form of their surrounding world. Whoever sees only nature everywhere, nature in the sense and as it were with the eyes of natural science, is blind to the sphere of spirit, the proper domain of the human sciences; he sees no persons and no objects that receive sense from personal achievements, no "culture"-objects — he sees, strictly, no persons at all, though he busies himself with persons in the attitude of the naturalistic psychologist. Husserl marks the point as needing thorough elaboration. The transposition of the moral "treating as a thing" into a theoretical one is exact, and it cuts both ways. Naturalization is not a theoretical sin; within its limits it yields real knowledge, of dependencies. The wrong is a claim of exhaustiveness, and its symptom is blindness: the naturalistic psychologist handles persons without seeing any.

In the comprehensive experience of the other's existence we understand him without further ado as a personal subject, and as related to objectities to which we too are related: to earth and sky, field and forest, to the room in which "we" are together, to a picture we are looking at (191). We are related to a common surrounding world — we are in a personal association — and these belong together. We could not be persons for others if a common surrounding world did not stand over against us in a communality, an intentional connectedness of our lives; correlatively, the one constitutes itself essentially with the other, and every ego can become a person in the normal sense, a person in the personal association, for itself and for others, only when comprehension establishes the relation to a common surrounding world. Personhood in the normal sense is thereby made social by essence, not by addition: person and common world are co-constituted, and neither is prior. "Comprehension" (Komprehension) is the term Husserl uses here for the understanding apprehension of the other as a subject with a world; it does the work that "empathy" did in the Fourth Chapter of Section Two, now in the personalistic register.

The common surrounding world receives communalities of a new and higher sense through the acts of personal mutual determination that proceed on the basis of reciprocal comprehensions, and with these arise possibilities not only for a parallel and mutually understood behavior toward objects of the common world but for a united behavior of persons toward such objects, a behavior in which they take part jointly, as members of a connected whole (191–192). Persons apprehend one another not only comprehensively in the first and founding way, in which the one understands the other's bodiliness, belonging to his surrounding world, together with its spiritual sense as a body, reading expression, gesture, and spoken word as the manifestation of personal life; they also apprehend one another so that they "determine one another" and are active jointly and not only singly, personally united. Comprehension of the other as a subject is only the first level; the second is being determined by him, and only the second makes a community rather than a set of mutually intelligible individuals.

Husserl derives the second level from the account of motivation in §50 (192–193). Just as mere physical things "motivate" — as appearing, experienced actuality they exert stimuli on the subject of experience and "occasion" it to behave, which is phenomenally an immediate experiential relation between a world-object and a subject — so human beings exert on one another, in the same general sense, "immediate" personal effects, intuitive effects; they have "motivating force" for one another. But they do not act merely in the manner of physical things of experience, in the bare form of stimuli, though occasionally they do that too: I see an insufferable person and get out of his way, as I get out of the way of a disgusting thing. There is another form of persons acting on persons. They direct themselves, in their spiritual doing, at one another; they perform acts with the intention of being understood by their counterpart and of determining him, in his understanding grasp of these acts as uttered with that intention, to certain personal modes of behavior. Conversely the one so determined can go along with this influence willingly or refuse it unwillingly, and by not only acting accordingly but making his willingness or unwillingness intelligible through communication, he determines the one who determined him to reactions in turn. So relations of agreement (Einverständnis) form: to speech follows answer, to the theoretical, evaluative, or practical demand one person makes on another follows the answering turn back, assent or refusal or a counter-proposal. In these relations a conscious reciprocal relation of the persons is established and at the same time a unitary relation of both to the common surrounding world — which may be not only a physical and animal or personal one but an ideal one, the mathematical "world," since a surrounding world spans the totality of the "objectities," ideal ones included, that stand over against the communicating persons as consciously the same. The difference between a stimulus and an address is that an address is made to be understood and answered; and it is in the answering, not in the understanding alone, that a common world is constituted. That the common world can be ideal shows that community is not tied to shared space.

The surrounding world that constitutes itself in experiencing others, in mutual understanding and in agreement, Husserl names the communicative surrounding world (193). It is by its essence relative to persons who find themselves in it and find it as their counterpart, and this holds of it as of the "egoistic surrounding world" of the person thought in isolation — the person thought abstractively in her relation to a surrounding world so that she includes no relations of agreement with other persons, none of the social association. Every person has, ideally speaking, her egoistic surrounding world within her communicative one, insofar as she can "abstract" from all relations of agreement and the apperceptions founded in them, or rather think them apart. In this sense there is a "one-sided detachability" of the one surrounding world in relation to the other, and the egoistic constitutes an essential core of the communicative, such that abstractive processes are needed before the former can stand out from the latter. The order of the analysis is thereby settled against the order of presentation: the egoistic surrounding world is not what a person first has and then enlarges, but a core that is reached by abstraction from the communicative world in which she already lives.

Think an isolated subject, with only its egoistic surrounding world abstracted out; when the abstraction is lifted, that world acquires new intentional strata with the appearance of relations of agreement, the personal association and the communicative world relative to it constitute themselves, and this is the association's own surrounding world, which does not only surround it from outside but includes it (193–194). As persons already belong to an egoistic surrounding world, so persons outside communication — outside the social association in question — can belong to a communicative one. Persons who belong to the social association are given to one another as "fellows" (Genossen), not as objects but as counter-subjects, who live "with" one another, associate, are related actually or potentially in acts of love and love returned, hatred and hatred returned, trust and trust returned. A communicative world contains its own subjects; this is what distinguishes it from an environment, and the word for the mode in which its members are given to one another, counter-subject, is the personalistic attitude's answer to introjection.

The acts of social reciprocal relation are, on the account just given, specially distinguished among the acts of one-sided understanding of another's ego-life (194). It is not enough that others are understood as persons and that the one who understands behaves toward them thus and so, orients himself by them; there need be no agreement with them in that. But agreement is exactly what matters. Sociality constitutes itself through the specifically social, communicative acts, acts in which the ego turns to others, and in which these others are conscious to the ego as those to whom it turns, and who in turn understand this turning, possibly orient their behavior by it, and turn back in concurring or dissenting acts. These acts are what establish, among persons who already "know" of one another, a higher unity of consciousness, into which they draw the surrounding world of things as the common surrounding world of the position-taking persons; and the physical world too, in this apperceptive inclusion, has social character — it is a world that has spiritual significance. The definition of sociality is act-theoretic: a community is not a fact about persons but a structure of address and reply, and the world becomes social by being drawn into that structure.

So far the concept of the surrounding world has been taken very narrowly, as the world over against a personal ego, or an ego standing in a communicative association, comprising all the objectualities constituted for it through its "experiences" — its thingly, axiological, and practical experiences, into which axiological and practical apprehensions therefore also enter (194–195). This surrounding world is, as said at 185f., something constantly changing with the progress of the subject's actual experience and actual activity in natural experience, in theoretical thinking, in valuing, willing, creating, in shaping ever new objects. The recapitulation prepares a widening: a surrounding world defined by actual experience is too small.

For every personal individual, however, a surrounding world with an open horizon constitutes itself, spanning the objectities that could present themselves further in connection with the course of its actual constituting of objects of its surrounding world — things, value-objects, and so on — and would present themselves under given circumstances (195). Actually performed experiences motivate possibilities of new ones; the objects are experienced by the subject as ones that have their existence, their orders of being, their dependences that can be followed up. The subjects communicating with one another belong reciprocally to one another's surrounding world, which is relative to the ego that in each case looks about from itself and constitutes its own surrounding world. And this ego belongs, in virtue of self-consciousness and of the possibility of the manifold behavior directed at itself, to its own surrounding world: the subject is "subject-object." On the other side a single world constitutes itself in the intersubjective association, a world with levels: the communicating subjects constitute personal unities of higher level, whose total compass, as far as actual and possible personal bonds reach, makes up the world of social subjectivities, from which is to be distinguished its correlative and inseparable world for these subjectivities, the world of social objectities. Three results are packed here. The surrounding world is horizonal, comprising what would present itself as well as what does; the person belongs to its own surrounding world as subject-object, so that the world is not merely what stands over against it; and the intersubjective world is one world with two correlated faces, social subjects and social objects.

Different concept-formations are therefore needed (195–196). First a subject and its surrounding world were separated; then a subject-association and its surrounding world had to be distinguished, the subject belonging, as objectifiable and objectified for itself, to its own surrounding world at once. So that division, necessary as it is, does not suffice. For a circle of friends, for an association, its "outer world" is the whole rest of the world — the other spirits, spiritual communities, physical things, cultural objects, sciences, arts, insofar as they are objects of its interests and occupations; and to the surrounding world of such a circle, and of any social subjectivity, a communicatively constituted association of subjects, belongs that subjectivity itself, insofar as it can become an object for itself in the association's relation back to itself, as each single subject of it can. It is therefore useful to distinguish, for the single subject as for a subject-association, between surrounding world in the full sense and surrounding world in the sense of outer world, the latter excluding the objectified subject. The two senses of Umwelt are fixed here for the rest of the section: the full sense includes the subject, the outer world is what remains when the subject is subtracted.

Now all social objectities that are in communication with one another are to be taken together as one, with the ideally possible isolated individual as the limit case (196–197). The idea of communication evidently extends from the singular personal subject to social subject-associations, which are themselves personal unities of higher level; all such unities, as far as their actually established communication reaches, or the communication to be established in accordance with their own indeterminately open horizon, constitute not merely a collection of social subjectivities but close together into a more or less intimately organized social subjectivity that has its common counterpart in a surrounding world, or outer world, in a world that is for it. If it is a surrounding world that contains no further subjects — none, that is, standing within the practically possible range of an actual communication to be established, like the possible inhabitants of Mars, and open like these in their actuality — but only objects, they are still objects for the united persons and higher personal unities, objects that every such subjectivity can find in its surrounding world and, indeed, as ones that every "fellow" could find in his. This ability belongs as an ideal potentiality essentially to the sense of a surrounding world. At the same time the idea of the world as spiritual world constitutes itself, in the form of the total compass of the social subjects of lower and higher level standing in actual, or partly actual and partly potential, communication with one another — the single person included as the limit case zero of social subjectity — in one with the belonging total compass of social objectities. For every subject that is in this way a member of a social total-association, one and the same spiritual world constitutes itself, apprehended and posited from the "standpoint" of that subject with a correspondingly, and from subject to subject variably, changing sense of apprehension: a united plurality of subjects, individual spirits and spiritual communities, related to a world of things, a world of "objects," an actuality that is not spirit but actuality for spirit, and yet, as actuality for spirit, always also bespirited, spiritually significant, carrying spiritual sense in itself and receptive to ever new such sense. A footnote refers to Beilage V for the paragraph that follows. The world of the human sciences is here given its constitutive definition: one spiritual world for all its members, indexed to each member's standpoint, whose objects are objects for spirit and therefore carriers of sense. Nothing in it is a thing without being also a possible meaning.

At its lowest level this thing-world is intersubjective material nature as the common field of the actual and possible experience of individual spirits, singly and in experiential communalization, and all individual bodies belong to it; sensuous feelings and drives range themselves beside sensuous experience (197). At a higher level this nature comes into question as the field of theoretical, axiological, and practical actions, the activities of spirits at their various levels of sociality: nature becomes the domain of the natural sciences, the field of aesthetic valuations or of actual or possible practical work, which may itself have scientific, aesthetic, ethical, or other ends. The ends are set by individual or social subjectivity, social at the respective level; it is subjectivity too that assigns means and ways to the ends, whereupon ends, means, ways, and products in turn are subject to individual and social valuation, release new spiritual reactions, can determine new settings of ends, and so on. Apperceptions of various levels arise, and it must especially be taken into account that the respective objects can be apperceived as objects of possible or presumptive valuations and settings of ends and means. The iterative structure §50 found in the individual's surrounding world is here found in the social one, and with one addition: an object can be apperceived as the object of valuations that others might make, so that the social world contains, as a character of its things, the horizon of what they could be for anyone.

Looking closer, Husserl distinguishes further, in two numbered items (198). First, the surrounding world or outer world of the common spirit: the world of intersubjectively constituted objectualities, spirits, spiritual communities, bespirited matters and mere natural things, objectualities that every subject belonging to the communal association can bring to givenness in its own way, from its own standpoint, and of which it can at the same time recognize, through mutual understanding, that those given to it and to its fellows are one and the same. Second, the merely subjective sphere of the single subject, which has a surrounding that is in an original way merely its own and so can be given originarily to no one else. In the communicative community everyone sees what I see, everyone hears what I hear, or can see and hear the same; we experience the same things and events, we experience the animals and human beings facing us and see the same inner life in them. And yet each has the appearances that are exclusively his own, each the experiences exclusively his own, and these only he experiences in their bodily selfhood, wholly originarily. In a certain way I do experience the other's experiences too, and there lies a self-givenness in it, insofar as the empathy (comprehensio) performed in one with the originary experience of the body is a kind of presentification and yet founds the character of a bodily being-there-with; so far we have experience, perception. But this being-there-with — "appresence," in the sense given earlier at §44 — can in principle not be converted into immediate originary presence (Urpräsenz). What is peculiar to empathy is that it refers to an originary body-spirit consciousness, but to one I cannot myself originarily perform, I who am not the other and function only as an understanding analogue for him. The two spheres are distinguished by mode of givenness, not by content: the same world is given, but each subject's appearances of it are given originarily to that subject alone, and the other's are given to me as appresent, never as present. This is the exact point at which Section Two's apparatus of presence and appresence re-enters the personalistic analysis.

We thereby strike on an ideal distinction that is evidently significant also under the viewpoints of constitutive genesis, the distinction between the "idea" of pre-social subjectivity — the subjectivity that presupposes no empathy yet — and the idea of social subjectivity (198–199). The first knows only inner experience, absolutely originary, containing no elements of presentification, bodily grasp through and through without co-grasping except in the form of "horizons"; and outer experience, which is throughout only the experience of a co-positing — "apprehension" or "appresentation" — that can be redeemed in transitions to experiences of bodily givenness. The second is the subjectivity of the world of the common spirit: here we have experience of other subjects and of their inner life, experience in which their character and properties come to givenness, experience of forms of community, of communal matters, of spiritual objects, and everywhere a moment of presentification through empathy belongs to the experience that can never be redeemed through immediate presentation. To the world of social subjects belong also the subjects as objects, and the acts, the experiences, of these subjects, their appearances, and so on; these come to originary perception, to presentation, only for the subjects to whom they belong, and even then to purely originary perception only as "immanent" data, not as constituents of the intersubjective world with its intersubjective time and intersubjective realization, as belonging to spatial bodiliness and so to the spatiality of nature. The genetic significance is the point: pre-social subjectivity is an idea reached by abstraction, and its outer experience already contains an appresentation that is the model for empathy, so that the social level does not add a new kind of consciousness but extends an existing one to a case where it can never be redeemed.

Still, within the mode of givenness the objective — that is, the intersubjective — divides into two groups (199–200). First, what the personal subject, as member of the communicative world, can experience immediately and originarily according to its own content, even if a form of ordering, or better a form of reality, belonging to its intersubjective objectivity still attaches to it; here belong all the experiences of the subject and the subject itself for itself, as an "inwardly" perceivable and really apperceived object, as subject-object. Second, what the personal subject experiences only mediately, insofar as on the basis of what is actually perceptively experienced, or perceivable in progressing experience, it experiences a co-presence that is not perceivable, not redeemable in its perceptions, according to its own content of being. Myself I can experience "directly," and only my intersubjective form of reality I cannot in principle experience; for that I need the media of empathy. I can experience others, but only through empathy; their own content they alone can experience through originary perception.
