Likewise my experiences are given to me directly, according to their own content, but the experiences of others are experienceable by me only mediately, empathically; and at the same time each of my experiences, as a constituent of the "world," the objective spatiotemporal sphere of reality, is not directly experienceable, since the form of reality, that of intersubjective objectivity, is no immanent form. The asymmetry is complete and symmetrical at once: what is originary to me is only mediately given to you, and what makes my own experiences objective — their place in the one world — is given to me no more directly than yours are.

An addendum (Zusatz), printed in smaller type, asks what social experience is, the experience in which social objectualities are given (200). What a marriage is I can "understand," insofar as I can empathize into one, in full clarity when I have entered one myself, live it through, and grasp it by its essence, which would be "perception"; the "given" I can then intuitively modify, produce intuitive variants of marriage and grasp by essence various differences of "marriage," so obtaining material for comparative valuations. Friendship likewise: I stand in friendly relations to others; the friendship may be no "ideal" one, but I can modify the intuitive presentation, which requires that I can bring to intuitive validity ways of motivation that are not, or not predominantly and regularly, the effective ones, for instance by excluding these or those egoistic motives, or presenting them excluded, and then following the modifications that proceed by essence for the ideal social formation. An association I can make clear to myself by starting from a students' association in which I took living part; a municipality, most fully as a citizen taking active part, living through all the civic activities of the municipal domain, learning its constitution not only verbally, reading up its statute and the customs of its sphere, but making clear to myself the "sense" of all this, making intuitive the laws in their application and by their function for its regulation, and bringing its "essence" to full clarity; if I cannot do this all-sidedly, it suffices to do it in examples. The addendum supplies the method the human sciences need on this account: essential insight into social formations is won by living participation and free variation, not by observation from outside, so that the eidetics of the social is an eidetics performed from within.

### §52. Subjective Appearance-Manifolds and Objective Things

To the second of the two groups just distinguished belongs the whole infinity of the appearances of things, the things of intersubjectively constituted physical nature (201). This nature appears to everyone, but to each, as already touched on, in principle in a different way. Each has his thing-appearances; at best, with a change of the subjective circumstances of experiencing, the one can have the same appearances the other had before, but never can different subjects at the same point of intersubjective, "objective," time have the same appearances. Insofar as the one subject belongs to the surrounding world of the other, the stock of the one's actual appearances does belong to the surrounding world of the other; but only insofar. So it stands with every manifold of constituting phenomena in which an identical reality constitutes itself for every subject — constitutes itself as identical, as intersubjective object of the communal world, thanks to the agreement, actual or potential, that unites the subjects and makes acts of intersubjective identification possible. Here belong all the sensuous schemata, adumbrations, sensuous stuffs of which the constitution of things spoke: they are specifically "subjective." What Section One established for the constitution of the thing is reassigned here to the personal sphere: the adumbrations are mine as no thing is.

One could go straight on and say the same holds, obviously and all the more, for the apperceptive apprehension-characters and finally for the entire stream of consciousness (201–202). But an essential difference must not be overlooked. In the stream of subjective experiences the subject announces itself as a real personality; the experiences are at the same time its states. In the sensuous schemata, in the subjective thing-appearances, on the contrary, not the subject announces itself but the things of its surrounding world; the appearances are of course not states of the things themselves, since states of things too first announce themselves in appearances, and that they are not states of the subject is clear from their being transcendent to the subject's actual states, its experiences. This is not new; it shows the fundamentally different way in which the real subject and the real objects constitute themselves, and that holds for the psychic as for the personal subject, as will become fully plain once the relation of the two has been determined. The appearances belong to me without being states of me: they are the medium in which things announce themselves, and it is the difference between a state and a medium that keeps the constitution of persons and of things apart.

Going back to what was established earlier, at 151f., about the subject and the object-world given to it, we see that the thingly surrounding world of the person, the one intuited or intuitable at any time, coincides with the world as it appeared to the solipsistic subject: things appearing in manifolds of adumbrations and grouping themselves in space around a central Here (202).

Other subjects that face me, and that I apprehend as such in understanding, can have the same things given in the same determinations, but their actual manifolds of appearance are in principle not the same (202). Each has his "Here," and for the same phenomenal Now it is a different one from mine; each has his phenomenal body, his subjective bodily movements. Each can make the same places in the intersubjectively identifiable space, the common surrounding space, his Here, which I have made my Here in succession just now; but for every point of intersubjectively identical time my Here and his are separate. The Here is the index of the subjective: it can be exchanged in succession, never shared at a time.

The talk of "intersubjective space" and "intersubjective time" still needs to be shown to have its right here (202). We saw earlier that the psychic undergoes temporalization and localization and thereby becomes nature in the widened sense. But also as spirit, apprehending and positing myself and others not as nature, I find myself and others in the spatial and temporal world. The claim is stated in emphasis and then unpacked over the next paragraphs: spirits are in space and time without being natural, and the account of how they are must not borrow from §49's introjection.

I am now, and was before, and will be afterwards; simultaneously, in the same time — objective time — are the others; their acts and mine have temporal position toward one another by simultaneity, before and after, and this time is the same as the time of our surrounding world (202).

It is similar with space (202–203). Everything is related to the Here that is my Here; I, the person, am in space at this place; others are there, where their bodies are. They go for a drive, pay a visit, and in doing so their spirits change their place in space with their bodies, in the space of the one objective surrounding world. The local positions are measured and determined like all spatial positions; and as for the temporal, they are measured by clocks, chronometers of every kind, apparatus that has its natural-scientific significance. And all such measurement leads back to certain spatial measurements to be performed in physical nature, attached to periodic spatial processes in the physical world. The paragraph concedes the naturalist's strongest point about spirits: their places and times are measured with instruments that belong to physics.

Accordingly there seems after all to be no essential difference between the natural-scientific and the human-scientific apprehension of the spiritual (203).

The answer is that every person has, as such and by essence, its surrounding world — first its subjective world of appearance, and then, through relation to the nexus of persons, at the same time a relation to the common objective surrounding world, in respect of which the subjective surrounding world is mere appearance (203). To every personal ego belongs the givenness of a physical surrounding world with its determinate orientation; each has its center from which it perceives things, has its body as the central thing around which, insofar as it encloses the center, all the other things appearing to it are ordered and present themselves in their orientation. The world is thereby, body included, the counterpart of the ego, its surroundings, given to it at every time in a mode of appearance specifically belonging to it. "I am here" does not mean: I am a natural object. The reply distinguishes two senses of place. The person's Here is the zero-point of an orientation, and it is by relation to the common world that this Here becomes a place in objective space; the natural object's place is the second, and the person has the first before it, so that measurement of the second does not touch the sense of the first.

I, the human being as natural object, am a body-thing (Leibkörper), and the body-thing is, egoistically and subjectively considered, my surrounding-object in the "here," and objectively considered, at the place of objective space presented in the subjective here (203–204). Further: this body is first of all, in every respect, my surrounding-object, and on the other side, by everything it is in "objective truth," a thing in objective nature, the nature of natural science. This objective body-thing is not yet the human being but only the bearer of an aesthesiological and a body-soul stratum of being, and this whole is now the object of the common surrounding world for all natural-scientifically researching subjects related to it. More directly still, every other human being is apprehended as nature in the natural-scientific attitude that I and all natural-scientific subjects perform, in which nature belongs to my spiritual surroundings — where I am not in the human-scientific, personal attitude, because I do not there have personality and its surrounding world as my thematic sphere. In this attitude the other human being is constituted as founded being, which has its spatiality and temporality through the founding body. The natural scientist's own case is the test: even his objective body is first his surrounding-object, and nature itself is a region of his spiritual surroundings, so that the naturalistic attitude is here located inside the personal one rather than beside it.

But if I am in the human-scientific attitude, in which the other spirit is thematically posited as spirit and not as founded in the physical body — not as the founding reality of the total reality "natural-historical human being," zoological creature — then this body-thing is, like everything that is not spirit, surrounding thing-world: a thing that has spiritual significance, that serves a spiritual being, a person and her spiritual behavior, as expression, as organ, and so on (204). The spirit, though not apprehended here as nature, does therefore have an assignment to the body and through it to an objective part of space. The space of the surrounding world with its things is at any time objectifiable as natural-scientific space, the space of natural-scientific nature, since the possibility of the natural and natural-scientific attitude and of natural research belongs to the essence of the situation; so the spirit too, which is itself naturalizable in a determinately specified way, has "its" place assigned, its distance from other things of the surroundings. The spirit, the human being as a member of the personal human world, does not have a place as things have a place — not even in the secondary sense in which a body founded in thinghood and a natural-historically founded human being have one; it has a place in the sense that it stands in constant functional relation to a body which for its part has its place in the surrounding world and, in that world's scientific determination as physical world, possibly a place determinable with physical instruments. The concept of a functional relation is what makes the spirit locatable without making it a thing: its place is derived from the body's by a relation that is not containment.

The same holds in temporal respect (204–205). The different persons have their different subjective time insofar as the single subjects, so long as no empathy is thematically performed and the personal association is not yet thematically established in which the person as such has constituted itself, have their temporality in the course of their consciousness. Every appearance of the pure ego has its place in this time, every ego-act its temporal extension. But all constituted unities have their time too, and it belongs to the essence of the constitution of unities of appearance that they constitute themselves as temporal in such a way that the constituted, egoistically transcendent time stands, by essence, in relation to the time of the constituting experiences, with relations of simultaneity, before and after running determinately back and forth; the primarily constituting appearance-thing, the sensuously intuitive unity, stands there as simultaneous in its duration with the perceptual continuity and its noetic duration. The analysis §49 c gave of the psychic is here redone for the person: the constituted thing's time is tied to the constituting experiences' time by essence, and this is the seed of intersubjective time.

That transfers to all intersubjectively constituting unities so far that a priori objective time and subjective time — my immanent and my spatio-temporal — are a single temporal order: in the subjective space-times as "appearances" objective time presents itself, "appears," insofar as the appearance is a valid appearance in this respect (205). This temporalization and localization of spirit is something essentially other than that of "introjection," at 190f. The contrast is the section's recurrent one: the spirit's time and place are those of a subject whose appearances are appearances of an objective order, not the time and place of a stratum attached to a body.

In a Now, then, that is identical as the intersubjective present for the different subjects standing in mutual understanding, they cannot have the same "here," the same intersubjective spatial present, and cannot have the same appearances (205–206). The index of this phenomenological state of affairs is the impenetrability of the different simultaneous bodies as such. Two bodies can be objectively joined into one thing, "grown together," but being grown together into one thing does not create one body filling the same duration, one here, one phenomenal orientation-space, and identity of the thing-appearances with respect to the thing-world surrounding both subjects. The appearances of two subjects cannot flow together into identical appearances as the optical appearances of two eyes do. Different subjects therefore have phenomenal objectities that are necessarily separate in phenomenal intersubjective time and in principle not even of the same essential stock. The same things can actually appear to them, but the appearances, these things "in their respective mode of appearance," in their subjective characters of relief, orientation, the colorings presenting themselves just so, are different — only that relations of exchange obtain, according to which the same thing that was given to me now in a certain mode of appearance can afterwards, in the flow of intersubjective time, be given to the other in a wholly equal mode, and conversely. In another respect exchangeability is by essence excluded: the central here to which the modes of appearance are related and which lends them individuation, with their concrete essential content, in the belonging Now, is not exchangeable, and so the individual phenomena are not exchangeable, as everything subjective in its individuality is not. So too the "I move" that is mine, this ego's own, and as such can never be "empathized" into another ego; what is understood in empathy is not my ego but his, not my subjectivities in experiences and "subjective" modes of appearance but his. The argument runs on two levels of sameness. Modes of appearance are exchangeable in kind across time; but the here-and-now that individuates a mode of appearance is not, and that is what makes the subjective the subjective. The two eyes are the exact counter-image: their appearances fuse because they have one here.

Different subjects describe the objects of their surrounding world on the basis of their respective appearances (206). Insofar as subjects standing in possible communication have, if not equal actual appearances, equal groups of appearances — actual and motivatedly possible perceptual appearances — such that with a mere exchange of their positions in space the appearances of the one subject would have to pass into equal appearances of the other, so far the appearing things are the same for the different subjects and describable by them in the same way. We already know that a certain equality of the whole manifolds of appearance is a condition of the possibility of mutual understanding, so that only differences in certain directions are possible. Such differences come out in the exchange of descriptions; on the ground of the intersubjective concordance of experience discordance stands out, and only so can it become known. Discordance is here made parasitic on concordance: without a shared style of appearances there could be no disagreement about the things, only unrelated worlds.

From there the lines run to the possible and familiar distinctions of "normal" human persons against anomalous ones — a normality related to a plurality of persons of one communicating association who on average, by preponderant regularity, agree in their experiences and accordingly in their experiential statements, as against other persons of the same association who make deviating statements about their surrounding world in particular directions of description and are thus apprehended in comprehension as persons who experience the same things in another way, one not empirically realizable for the one who understands them within his own frame of motivation (206–207). Normality is thus a social and statistical notion of concordance, and anomaly is not error but a mode of experiencing the same world that the normal cannot re-enact.

From here the lines run further to the constitution of physical thing-objectivity in intersubjective natural research (207–208). The objectivity that constitutes itself intuitively for the single subjects and comes to expression in their descriptive statements, and also the objectivity that constitutes itself as the correlate of a community of persons rating one another reciprocally as normal, now counts itself as mere "appearance" of a "true" objectivity; the common nature of intuitive experience counts only as "appearance" of an essentially non-intuitive nature in itself, a nature intersubjectively determinable in theory but not directly experienceable, hence not properly describable, not determinable by any immediate experiential concepts. Put in Cartesian turns of phrase: one separates the nature of imagination from the nature of a pure intellection — disregarding that since Kant this nature of natural-scientific theory has itself customarily been taken again as the appearance of an unknowable, problematic, metaphysical "in itself." But all these ways of speaking harbor very dangerous anticipations that must not at first be accepted. Every agreement of the community-subjects with respect to the appearance-predicates of the individually and interindividually appearing objects counts as a contingent fact, whereas their agreement with respect to all the objective, physical, determinations that come out in rational experiential thinking counts as necessary. To this belongs, further, that by means of this objective theoretical knowledge of nature all actual and possible differences and agreements of the subjects in their phenomena, the immediately experienced thingly stocks, are to be "explained" with regard to their likewise objectively investigable bodily and psychic organization, and so are themselves to be recognizable as necessary. The constitution of physical nature is retold from the personal side, with its two marks: the intuitive world is demoted to appearance of a non-intuitive truth, and agreement is reclassified from fact to necessity. Husserl signals the philosophical baggage of "appearance" and "in itself" and declines it; §64 will say what the relativity of nature does and does not mean.

So we have happily landed back at natural science, first physical science, then natural science in general (208). The differences of "world-pictures" that emerge in intersubjective agreement — empirically intuitive thing-worlds which, despite the discrepancy of their content, demonstrate themselves in intersubjective understanding as experiences of the world, the one and same world — and the resulting impossibility of reaching unconditionally valid judgments about this world on the basis of actual experience, compel theoretical research in the form of natural science; it brings "objective" nature to knowledge, which now, in its turn, belongs to the surrounding world of the community-spirit. The circle is closed and named in the same breath: natural science is motivated by the personal world's discrepancies, and its product returns into that world as an object of the common spirit. §53 takes the circle as its problem.

### §53. The Relation of Nature-Consideration and Spirit-Consideration to One Another

It is time to take stock (208–209). We feel an uneasy difficulty, a certain tension between the nature that stood at the beginning and the nature that has now grown for us in the context of community. We started from the naturalistic, natural-scientific attitude, in which nature — physical, bodily, psychic — comes to givenness and theoretical knowledge; but this naturalistically considered world is not the world. Rather, what is pre-given is the world as everyday world, and within it the theoretical interest and the sciences related to the world grow for the human being, among them, under the ideal of truths in themselves, natural science. The pre-given world is first investigated with respect to nature; then come the animals, first of all human beings, and there the first thing is to investigate them as ego-subjects, going back to their life. That is inductive — as individually factual, like the person herself, determinable in its position in the spatial world only psychophysically — and in any case one has a series of psychophysical investigations. But that is not all: it is the one "objective" world, with the one space, the one time, into which "everything" orders itself, the persons too, who lead their personal life in unity with their bodies. To consider, reflect, give an account of the world in this attitude means to perform in advance the theoretical positing of "nature," and in approaching anything given, that thing acquires its inclusion into nature; the person, for instance, is eo ipso apprehended as naturally existing, as a bodily annex. The tension is precisely located: the everyday world is pre-given, natural science grows inside it, and yet the naturalistic attitude, once taken, absorbs everything, persons included, into the nature it posits — which was the "self-forgetfulness" §49 e predicted.

We then immersed ourselves in the essence of personal being as being of persons and for persons (209–210). We performed a piece of actual personal life, or imagined ourselves into one, felt our way into the personal life of another; we described the correlation between the personal subject and its personal surrounding world; we saw persons enter into personal relations, personal associations constitute themselves as objectualities of a higher level; a spiritual world grew up as the world of intersubjective community, and against it a special world for each personal subject, endless manifolds of appearances exclusively its own. Everyone finds himself, in a suitable direction of regard, as a subject, that is, as bearer of a merely subjective world that can be the same for no other subject, or rather as bearer of a subjective mode of appearance of the world, an appearance of the world in which the same world actually appears. In this contrast, with respect to the relation between appearance and the appearing objective, we again hit upon nature and natural science: nature is now an objectivity constituting itself in the context of the personal world, namely as the identically same that is concordantly experienced as existing by all rational members of the community in communal experience and, at a higher level, in experiential-scientific activities, in predicative formations called truth and science. We seem to have fallen here into a vicious circle. For if at the beginning we posited nature simply, as every natural scientist and everyone in the naturalistic attitude otherwise does, and took human beings as realities which have a plus over their physical bodiliness, then the persons were subordinate natural objects, components of nature. But if we pursued the essence of personality, nature presented itself as something constituting itself in the intersubjective association of persons, and so as presupposing that association. The circle is stated at full strength: nature contains persons as parts, and persons constitute nature as correlate. Its resolution will not be a choice between the two but the discovery that the second relation founds the first.

How are we to find our way (210)? I said above that immersion in personality and its surrounding world brings us of itself into a new attitude, one essentially other than the naturalistic attitude of, say, the natural scientist. Let us try to get clear about the opposition between these attitudes.

A change of attitude means nothing else than a thematic transition from one direction of apprehension into another, to which correlatively different objectivities correspond (210–211). What concerns us here are radical changes of that kind, transitions into apprehensions of fundamentally different phenomenological type. So our question is whether such differences of apprehension, and accordingly of "experience," are present here, in correspondence with which the noemata with their noematic objectivities — the experienced "as such" — are fundamentally different; or, with reference to the contexts of possible experiential verification and of the experiential knowledge to be based on them, whether the objects on the two sides belong to fundamentally different "regions." Is it really a matter of two worlds, "nature" on the one side and the spiritual world on the other, separated by cardinal differences of being? That need not mean, and shall not mean, that the two worlds have nothing at all to do with one another, that their senses do not establish essential relations between them. We know other cardinal differences of "worlds" that are nonetheless mediated by relations of sense and essence: the relation between the world of ideas and the world of experience; the relation between the "world" of pure, phenomenologically reduced consciousness and the world of the transcendent unities constituted in it; or between the world of things as appearances, the things of "secondary" qualities, and the world of physical things. All such differences hang together with cardinal differences of "attitude," with fundamentally different kinds of apprehension or experience, and the correlative objectivities, fundamentally different as they are, are still mediated through relations of sense that already show in the designations: things as appearances are appearances of the things of physics, pure consciousness is constituting for these or those constituted unities, and so on. This gives the form the answer to the circle will take. Nature and spirit are correlates of different attitudes, hence different regions, but regions whose senses refer to one another; the question is then only which way the reference runs, and §64 will answer that spirit's sense does not refer to nature as its ground while nature's does refer to spirit.

As for the attitude whose correlate is nature — the world of realities in objective spatio-temporality and of the causality that governs the changes appearing in it — we need only recall it; we have studied it thoroughly enough (211). What is experienced here is, at the lowest level, material, physical nature, and founded in that is the experience of bodily and psychic being. The aesthesiological and the psychic is an "annex" of the physical body, localized in it in an extended sense, gaining through it objective spatial position and ordering into the time of nature. Husserl's note fixes what "annex" means: regulated coexistence, where the regulation of change is "causality," an inductive causality. In this manner we apprehend every human being, possibly, as "nature," positing psychic properties at his body as psychophysically dependent, and likewise ourselves if we so choose — though that has its noticeable difficulties. What those difficulties are will become clear of itself when we now pass over to the personalistic attitude and experience. The self-application of the naturalistic attitude is flagged as the point of strain: apprehending oneself as an annex of one's own body is possible but never quite honest, and §54 will show why by beginning from what the ego finds when it looks at itself.

## Second Chapter: Motivation as Fundamental Law of the Spiritual World

### §54. The Ego in the Inspectio Sui

In this attitude I take myself simply as that which I customarily take myself to be when I say "I," and I say it in any "I think" — I am convinced, I doubt, I hold possible, I love, I rejoice, I will, and so on (211–212). Husserl's note sends the reader to Supplement VI and to §§4–11 of the First Section for this and the following section. There is no question here of my meaning or finding myself and my cogito as something at the body, as founded in it and localized as an annex. Rather the reverse: the body is my body, and it is mine first of all as my counterpart, my object, as the house is my object, my seen or visible, touched and touchable; "mine," but not a component of the ego — given to me, that is, through manifold concordantly synthetic perceptions that I perform as subject, and according to what I have not perceived "co-given" to me through these very perceptions, since possible perceptions are motivated by actual ones in regulated fashion. In the body I do, to be sure, find the stratum of sensation localized, sensuous pleasure and sensuous pain among it; but that shows only that this stratum does not belong to the domain of what is properly egoic. As the body in general, so everything that constitutes it as an object is "non-ego" over against the ego, and belongs to the ego only in the manner of the counterpart, precisely as an existing object of its experiences. Every thingly objectivity of my experience is ego-belonging in this way; each has, to that extent, a character of subjectivity as experienced by the ego and so existing for it, as the target of its attending, the substrate of its theoretical, affective and practical acts. The inspectio sui — the Cartesian-Augustinian phrase for the ego's look at itself — returns a result contrary to the naturalistic apprehension: the body is on the object side, along with the house, and even localized sensings are thereby ruled out of the ego proper.

Now the ego can of course also reflect on its experiences, directions of regard, valuing or willing acts, and then they too are objective and over against it (212). But the difference leaps to the eye: they are not ego-foreign but themselves egoic; they are activities, acts, states of the ego itself; they are not merely ego-belonging as experienced or thought objects, not mere identity-correlates of what is primarily and originally egoic, subjective. The distinction between two ways of being "mine" — being my act and being my object — is the axis of the whole chapter.

The body has, of course, its special virtues over against other things, on account of which it is "subjective" in a distinguished sense: as bearer of the sense-fields, as organ of free movements and so as organ of will, as bearer of the center and of the fundamental directions of spatial orientation (212–213). But all of that is egoicity by the grace of the original egoicities. As the objects are objects for the ego, its worldly surroundings, by virtue of its apprehensions and experiential positings — namely as unities given therein in the mode "existing" — so too this body is my body, and mine even in the felt special sense, because I already am and, as it were, confer its special virtues on it. The center of orientation, for instance, belongs to the noematic content of my perception of the body-thing as such, and in the experiential positing it belongs to the intuitively constituted objectivity body, and so to a level of appearances that is already constituted appearance. It is thus by the grace of the ego, or of egoic originary intuition — which naturally does not mean: from the ego's contingent caprice. Husserl's note refers to Supplement VII. The Second Section's body, with its zero point and its "I move," is not retracted but demoted to a constituted level: what was there the subjective par excellence is here a product of a subjectivity more original than it.

So we find as the original and specifically subjective the ego in the proper sense, the ego of "freedom": the attending, considering, comparing, distinguishing, judging, valuing, attracted, repelled, inclined, disinclined, wishing and willing ego — the ego "active" in every sense, the ego that takes positions (213–214). But that is only one side. Over against the active stands the passive ego, and the ego is always, wherever it is active, at the same time passive, both in the sense of affective and of receptive — which does not exclude that it can also be merely passive; "receptivity" is in its sense an expression that includes a lowest level of activity, if not the proper freedom of active position-taking. Subjective in the original sense is also the "passive" ego, in a second sense, as the ego of "tendencies," which undergoes stimuli from things and appearances, is attracted, and merely yields to the pull. Subjective too are the "states" of the ego: states of sadness, of cheerfulness, passive desiring, renunciation as a state. "Being affected," as by a piece of news, is something subjective from the side of the object; "reacting" against it, rearing up, pulling oneself together is something subjective from the side of the subject. The ego of the personalistic attitude is thus not the pure act-pole alone; passivity, tendency and state belong to it originally, with the one proviso that they are the ego's own undergoing, not something it has as an object. That proviso is what distinguishes this from the naturalistic "psychic state."

From the properly subjective — the ego itself and its behavior, active and passive — we must now separate, on the one hand, the objective over against which the ego behaves actively or passively, and on the other the "material" substrate on which this behavior builds (214). For in every conscious life the stratum of position-takings, of acts in general, builds on understrata; we can connect here with what was said earlier in §§4–10. The objects of the surrounding world, against which the ego is active in its position-takings and by which it is motivated, are all originally constituted in acts of this ego. Goods, works, objects of use refer back to valuing and practical acts in which the "mere things" received this new stratum of being. If we disregard these strata of being, we are led back to "nature" as the domain of mere things, and then further back to the syntheses of various levels in which the ego was active as nature-constituting. Running backwards through the strata of thing-constitution we finally reach the sensation-data as the last, primitive proto-objects, which are no longer constituted by any ego-activity but are, in the most pregnant sense, pre-givennesses for all ego-activity. They are "subjective," but not ego-acts or ego-attitudes; they are ego-possessions (Ichgehabtheiten), the first "subjective having" of the ego. But we saw earlier that everything that originally constitutes itself in spontaneous acts of the ego becomes, as constituted, a "having" of the ego and a pre-givenness for new ego-acts: so the "sense-things" of various levels for the corresponding higher syntheses, the fully constituted intuitive objects of nature for theoretical-scientific activity, for valuing and practical behavior, and so on. The picture of the First Section is reasserted from the personal side: constitution is stratified, and each stratum, once constituted, becomes material the ego "has" for the next; only the sensation-data are had without ever having been made.

We have therefore to distinguish two kinds of subjective being (214–215). First, a subjective being as being and behavior of the ego: the subject and its acts or states, activities and passivities. Second, a subjective being as being for the subject: the having of the ego, consisting of the sensation-material and the totality of objects which constitute themselves for the subject in the course of its genesis. To this belongs also the proper human-ego that appears as a member of nature, but already too the solipsistically constituted body-soul unity presupposed for it, and even somatic bodiliness insofar as it constitutes itself in the inner attitude: although this has — like the sensation-material — a special ego-belongingness, it is not in the same way "over against" the ego as the constituted external world and its appearances are. There remains, then, of what is given in the inner attitude, only the subject of intentionality, of acts, as the subjective in the original and proper sense. The reduction is deliberate and severe: even the human-ego of nature and the solipsistic body-soul unity fall on the side of what the ego has. What the personalistic attitude calls "I" is the subject of intentionality and nothing else, and §55 begins from that.

### §55. The Spiritual Ego in Its Behavior Toward Its Surrounding World

This ego of intentionality is, in the cogito, related to its surrounding world and specifically to its real surrounding world — to things and human beings, say, that it experiences (215). This relation is immediately not a real relation but an intentional relation to something real. So there is a difference, and its first term is the intentional relation: I have the object given, I have it given as appearing thus and so, I have, in a suitable turn of regard, the appearance of the object given; I have the appearance but am attentive to the object, or on the other hand I am attentive to the appearance, and so forth. The distinction between intentional and real relation, fundamental since the Logical Investigations, is here the hinge of the personalistic attitude: the ego's relation to its surroundings is a relation of consciousness, whether or not the object exists.

The second term is the real relation: the object stands in a real-causal relation to me, the ego-human, and so first of all to the body that is called my body (215–216). The real relation drops away if the thing does not exist; the intentional relation remains. That whenever the object does exist a real relation runs "parallel" to the intentional one — that vibrations then spread from the object, the real actuality, through space and strike my sense organs, processes to which my experience attaches — is a psychophysical fact. But it does not lie in the intentional relation itself, which suffers nothing from the non-actuality of the object and at most changes through its consciousness of that non-actuality. The parallelism is granted as a fact and denied as an analysis: what the personalistic attitude describes is invariant under the object's existence or non-existence, and so cannot be the causal chain that requires it.

Let us now consider the subject's relations to its surrounding world as the world posited by it, to which not only realities but, say, ghosts can belong, and let us take the subject first again as a single, solipsistic subject (216–217). Then we find a wealth of relations between the posited objects and the "spiritual" subject, as we now call the subject of intentionality — relations, in the designated sense, between what is posited as reality and the positing ego, but not real relations: subject-object relations. Among them are relations of a subjective-objective "causality," a causality that is not real causality but has a wholly distinctive sense, that of motivational causality. Experienced objects of the surrounding world are sometimes noticed and sometimes not, and if they are, they exert a greater or lesser "stimulus," "awaken" an interest, and by virtue of this interest a tendency to turn toward them; this tendency flows off freely in the turning-toward, or flows off only after counter-tendencies have been weakened and overcome. All of this plays out between the ego and the intentional object. The object exerts a stimulus, perhaps by virtue of a mode of appearance that is pleasing; "the same" object can be given to me in a displeasing mode, and I feel the stimulus to change my position suitably, to move my eyes, and the pleasing appearance is now there, the telos of the movement reached. Here body- and eye-movements again come into consideration not as real processes of nature; a realm of free possibilities of movement is present to me in its own way, and the "I can" is followed, according to the sway of stimuli and tendencies, by an "I do," and correlatively the end has the character of a goal. The object stimulates me by virtue of its experienced properties — not the physical ones, of which I need know nothing, and which, if I do know of them, need not in truth exist. It stimulates me, within the intentionality of experience or of an indirect experiential knowledge and by virtue of the properties correlatively attributed to it, to eat it. It is a good of the class foodstuffs; I reach for it to eat it. That is a new kind of subject-objective "effect." The object has value-characters and is "experienced" together with them, apperceived as a value-object; I occupy myself with it, it exerts stimuli of occupation, I observe how it behaves as such an object, how these new properties, which are no properties of nature, verify themselves and let themselves be determined more closely. But I am not only a valuing but also a willing subject, and I prove myself as such not only as the subject of value-experiences and of the thinking that judges about them; I can also creatively reshape these things, actually use them for the purposes they serve. In doing so they also demonstrate their existence as nature at a certain lowest, intuitive level; in the contrary case they are nothing, they no longer determine or motivate me in this respect — though possibly the illusory objects as such act on me, the noemata in their cancellation, in their own way, as before in another way the noemata in their proto-doxic being-character. This is the first full statement of motivational causality, the section's central concept: a "causality" running from object to subject that goes by way of the object's experienced and valued properties, not its physical ones, and that survives the object's non-existence in the modified form of the cancelled noema. The ghost is the test case: it motivates fear without a real relation.

We have here the relation of reacting to something, of undergoing stimuli from it and thereby being motivated in a determinate sense (217–218). I am motivated to turn toward, to an attentive, pleased turning-toward; I feel the stimulus of beauty. Something reminds me of something else like it, and the likeness stimulates me to compare and distinguish. Something incompletely seen determines me to get up and go over to it. The bad air in the room, which I experience as such, stimulates me to open the window. Always we have the "suffering from something," being passively determined by something, and actively reacting to it, passing over to a doing, and this doing has a goal. Here belongs all making and reshaping of physical objects, but also every "I move" — my hand, my foot — and likewise "I push," I shove, I pull, I resist a thing. Of course the event of the mechanical movement of my hand and its mechanical effect on the "pushed" ball is a physically real process; likewise the object "this human being," "this animal" takes part in this event according to its "soul," and its "I move the hand, the foot" is a psychophysically interwoven process, to be explained real-causally in the context of psychophysical reality. But here it is not this real psychophysical process that is before us but the intentional relation: I, the subject, move the hand — and what that is in the subjective way of considering excludes all recourse to brain processes, nerve processes and the like, and the same holds for "I push the stone." The bodily member hand, appearing to consciousness thus and so, is as such the substrate of the "I move," is object for the subject and, so to speak, the theme of its freedom, of its free doing, and in the pushing the means "whereby" the thing, likewise intentionally given to me, can become and does become the "theme" of the pushing. The same event — the hand moving the stone — is thus described twice, once as a psychophysical process and once as an intentional relation with a theme, a means and a goal, and the second description contains no term that refers to the first.

Evidently, then, what belongs essentially to the intentional relation between the subject and the thematic object is the cogito that defines the thematic object — the object for me, for this ego — or the thematic relation: in it there is an appearing, perceived, remembered, emptily represented, conceptually thought object (218). The being-thesis, of experience or of thinking, can be false; the thing does not exist, which means that in my subsequent critical judgment or in another's, I push nothing in actuality, I do not dance or jump. But the evidence — the evident experience — of "I suffer" or "I do," of "I move myself," is not thereby affected, not cancelled. One can say that the "I push," "I dance" and the like is also a cogito, only one that includes a transcendence-thesis in itself and harbors the "ego sum" within this mixed form. The world is my surrounding world — not, that is, the physical world but the thematic world of my and our intentional life, together with what is extra-thematically conscious, co-affecting, accessible to my thematic positings, my thematic horizon. The Cartesian certainty of the ego is extended, with a qualification, to bodily action: "I push" cannot be wrong as an act, only as a thesis about the world, and the surrounding world is defined as the correlate of exactly such acts.

This surrounding world possibly is, or harbors within it, my theoretical surrounding world; more clearly, it can present theoretical themes for me, the actual subject — not for me, the human being — and I can, theoretically investigating the contexts of reality, come to practice natural science (218–219). Aiming at the real, or working out real actuality, I gain the "true nature" that announces itself in the given things of the surrounding world as appearances. To this belongs also that I make myself and my psychic life an object, pursue the body-soul connections and investigate myself, the human being, as a reality among realities; but then natural science and natural-scientifically true being is a determination belonging to my surrounding world and appearing within its wide circle. The circle of §53 is now traversed in the right direction: natural science, including the naturalizing of myself, is a theme within my surrounding world, and its "true nature" a determination of that world's things, not a ground beneath it.

The surrounding world can then be for me the theme of natural-scientific technique, in general the theme of practical formations with reference to valuations and settings of purpose (219). I then practice technique, art and so on. I can set and accept values and thereupon see and find derived values, means-values; regard purposes as final purposes, weigh in hypothetical-practical consciousness, choose and then take into actual praxis and assign possible means to these purposes. But I can also judge about values, finally pose ultimate questions of value and of purpose, and so practice theory of values, theory of rational praxis, theory of reason. The theoretical, axiological and practical interests of §51 reappear as strata of the one surrounding world of the one ego, and theory of reason is named as the highest of them — the level at which §60 will treat the person as subject of reason-acts.

So we survey the field of the proper ego-facts (219–220). Always the ego is the subject of intentionalities, and therein lies: a noema and a noematic object is immanently constituted — "apperception" — an object made conscious, and in particular an object posited as existing, which, conscious in its how, enters into a relation to the subject that is "intentional" in a new sense: the subject behaves toward the object, and the object stimulates, motivates the subject. The subject is the subject of a suffering or of a being-active, passive or active in relation to the objects noematically before it, and correlatively we have "effects" on the subject that issue from the objects. The object "presses itself upon the subject," exerts stimuli on it — theoretical, aesthetic, practical stimuli — it wants, as it were, to be the object of the turning-toward, knocks at the door of consciousness in a specific sense, that of turning-toward; it attracts, the subject is drawn toward it, until finally the object is what is noticed. Or it attracts practically, wants as it were to be grasped, invites enjoyment. There are countless such relations and countless noematic strata that the object takes on with these turnings-toward, which superimpose themselves over the original noema — with things, the pure thing-noema. Two senses of "intentional" are now distinguished within the one relation: the constitutive sense in which the noema is made conscious, and the new, motivational sense in which the constituted object acts on the subject and the subject reacts. The second presupposes the first and adds noematic strata to it; that is the whole structure of the spiritual world in outline.

### §56. Motivation as Fundamental Lawfulness of Spiritual Life

We see, then, that by the spiritual or personal ego is to be understood the subject of intentionality, and that motivation is the lawfulness of spiritual life (220). What motivation is needs closer investigation.

#### a) Reason-Motivation

Consider first the way in which, for instance, perceptions and the like motivate judgments; how judgments justify and correct themselves through experiences, so that the ascription of a predicate confirms itself in the concordant experiencing of it, and in conflict with experience the cancelling negation is motivated; or how judging is motivated by other judging in inference; but also, in quite another way, how judgments are motivated by affects and affects by judgments, how suppositions or questions, how feelings, desires and willings are motivated (220). In short: the motivation of position-takings by position-takings — for which certain "absolute motivations" are always presupposed: something pleases me in itself, "for its own sake," and the like — indifferently whether reason rules within the motivations or not. The first sense of motivation is act-to-act motivation, and it is defined so as to include the irrational, with the note that every chain of it rests on something valued for its own sake.

Yet reason is not thereby to be excluded, insofar as precisely the distinguished case of reason-motivation, of motivations within the frame of evidence, is present, which, when they rule in purity, produce constitutive unities of consciousness of a higher level, with correlates in the region "true being" in the widest sense (220–221). Here belongs in particular every example of logical grounding. So we have to distinguish, first, motivations of active acts by active acts in the sphere that stands under norms of reason — and here lies the difference between motivation of the ego and motivation of the acts — and second, other motivations. The reference to Ideas I's phenomenology of reason is unmistakable: rational motivation is the case in which motivation and constitution coincide, evidence motivating the positing of true being.

Motivation can here have its most proper sense, in which the ego is what is motivated: I give the conclusion my thesis because I have judged thus and so in the premises, have given them my thesis (221). The same holds in the sphere of valuing something for itself and for the sake of another, of derived willing as deciding for the sake of another. Everywhere here I perform my cogito and am determined by the fact that I have performed another cogito. Evidently the thesis of the conclusion is related to the thesis of the premises. They are ego-theses, but on the other hand they are not themselves the ego, and so we have as motivation also a peculiar relation among the theses. But the theses as theses have their "matter," and this too yields lines of dependence: the full propositions, and correlatively the full experiences, have a "motivational linkage." Three levels of motivation are distinguished in one paragraph — ego motivated by ego, thesis by thesis, matter by matter — and the first is called the most proper, because only there is the "because" a subject's own.

One can speak of pure reason-motivations in a double sense (221–222). It can mean the mere relations and connections of demand between "acts" properly so called; the subject is here the "active" one, and in a certain way it is everywhere here, even in purely logical thinking, "acting." Reason can then be called pure reason when and insofar as it is insightful and thoroughly motivated by insight. But that need not be the case; fallacies too fall under the title reason-motivation. Their "matter" is perhaps the sediment of reason-acts, but it now emerges confusedly and as a unity and so receives the thesis; reason is here a "relative" one. Whoever lets himself be pulled by drives and inclinations that are blind — because they do not issue from the sense of the things functioning as stimuli, do not have their source in it — is irrationally driven. But if I hold something to be true, a demand to be moral, and so to spring from the corresponding values, and I freely follow the supposed truth, the supposed moral goodness, then I am rational, though relatively, since I can be mistaken in it. I sketch a theory in relative reason insofar as I fulfil the intentions prescribed to me by my presuppositions; but I may have overlooked that one of my presuppositions was wrong, and perhaps I then follow a blind tendency. I believed I remembered that the proposition was proved; the tendency is not wholly blind, insofar as memory has its reason. In the end we arrive here at the basic questions of ethics in the widest sense, which has rational subject-behavior as its object. Rationality is thus graded rather than binary: pure where insight motivates throughout, relative where the subject follows what it takes for truth or goodness, and absent only where the pull does not come from the sense of the thing at all. That last clause is the criterion, and it makes rationality a matter of how the motivation runs, not of whether the result is correct.

Spinoza's and Hobbes's doctrine of the affects deals, by and large, with immanent motivations (222). The remark places the classical theory of the passions inside the new discipline as a partial anticipation of it.

#### b) Association as Motivation

Further, the whole realm of associations and habits belongs here (222–223). These are relations established between earlier and later consciousness within one ego-consciousness. But the motivation runs in the "present" consciousness, in the unity of the course of consciousness that is characterized as actual time-consciousness, originary consciousness. Here it is not a matter of the motivation of position-takings by position-takings, of active theses by active theses, but of experiences of any kind — either those that are "sediments" of earlier reason-acts, reason-accomplishments, or occur by "analogy" with such as apperceptive unities without really being formed by an action of reason; or those that are wholly without reason: sensibility, what presses itself upon us, the pre-given, the drive-mechanism in the sphere of passivity. The particular is there motivated in the dark underground, has its "psychic grounds," after which one can ask: how do I come to this, what brought me to it? That one can so ask characterizes all motivation whatever. The "motives" are often deeply hidden but can be brought to light by "psychoanalysis." A thought "reminds" me of other thoughts, calls a past experience back into memory. In some cases that can be perceived; in most cases the motivation is really present in consciousness but does not come to relief, is unnoticed or unnoticeable, "unconscious." Husserl's note registers a difficulty he does not resolve: in the "ego-less" sphere one still speaks of the ego that has been motivated — is the ego there the stream itself? The reference to psychoanalysis is not decorative; it marks that the concept of motivation is wide enough to include the hidden, and that the question "what brought me to it?" is the test for whether something is a motivation at all.

The contrast between associative motivations and motivations in the pregnant sense of ego-motivation, reason-motivation, which is what concerns us here, can be brought to relief as follows (223). What does the general fact "passive motivation" say? If a connection has once appeared in a stream of consciousness, then in the same stream there is the tendency for a newly appearing connection that is similar to a piece of the earlier one to continue in the sense of the similarity, to strive to complete itself into a total connection similar to the earlier total connection. Now we ask: whence do I know that? I reflect on an earlier connection and then on a second, standing to it in the stated relation; so I expect, after the initial piece, a similar piece, in rational motivation, and then really find it. Here, for us who ask, weigh and establish the fact of association, something new appears: the existence of the similar piece demands the existence of the similar complementary piece. That is a law of motivation, and it relates to existential positings; the demand is an "original" one, a demand of reason. So there are reason-motivations for existential positings as for judgments and for belief-position-takings in general, among which the formal-logical ones belong — and, the note adds, the correlative existential motivations in the constituted would have to be treated here too, such as "under similar objective circumstances, in nature, something similar is to be expected." Likewise there are reason-motivations for position-takings of feeling and will. The move is Humean in its material and anti-Humean in its result: the associative tendency, once reflected on, yields not a habit but a rational demand — the expectation of the similar becomes a motivated positing with its own evidence.

Of course belief, and every position-taking, is an occurrence in the stream of consciousness and so stands under the first law, that of "habit" (223–224). If I have once believed M with this sense and in a certain mode of representation, there is the associative tendency to believe M again in a new case. If I consider whether A is and pass over to assent, it is A, then in a new case the assent "it is A′" can attach itself "habitually" to the consideration whether A′ — which we think similar to A in its matter — is. No differently, if I have once apprehended a sensation-content and posited it objectively as A, I can another time apprehend a similar sensation-content, with its similar relations and circumstances, and posit it again as A′. Then we again have forms of course in the stream. But it is clear that I may not throw together the occurrences of habit with the occurrences of motivation in the sphere of position-takings, which in ordinary usage alone is called motivation. Habit covers position-takings too, but as occurrences, not as positions; the same assent can be described as a habitual recurrence and as a rationally motivated act, and the two descriptions are not to be confused.

Admittedly the one and the other kind of motivation interweave — the "causality" in the undergrounds of association and apperception and the "causality" of reason, the passive and the active or free (224). The free is pure and completely free where passivity plays only its role of procuring the proto-material that no longer contains any implied theses. The criterion of pure freedom is stated in constitutive terms: not the absence of passivity, which is impossible, but passivity confined to supplying material innocent of positings.

#### c) Association and Experiential Motivations

We spoke just now of unnoticed, "hidden" motivations that are present in habit, in the occurrences of the stream of consciousness (224). In inner consciousness every experience is itself doxically "characterized as existing." But there lies a great difficulty. Is it really characterized as existing, or does there obtain only, by essence, the possibility of reflection, which necessarily, in objectifying, allots the being-character to the experience? And even that is not clear enough. Reflection on an experience is originally a positing consciousness; but is the experience itself something given or constituted in a positing consciousness? If it were, then with reflection we could go back a step — would we not then arrive at an infinite regress? The difficulty is that of Ideas I §§77–78 and of the time-lectures: whether the pre-reflective experience already carries a doxic character or acquires one only in reflection, and how to avoid the regress in either case. Husserl states it and sets it aside.

But that can be left open here (224–225). Certainly there are hidden motivations; even without our performing the belief-acts, they enter into motivations. Examples are offered by the realm of experience, the endless field of motivations enclosed in every outer perception and memory, and also, in modified form, in every thingly phantasy. The apprehensions of things and of thingly connections are "webs of motivation": they build up through and through from intentional rays which, with their content of sense and of fullness, point forward and back, and they let themselves be explicated as the performing subject enters into these connections. I have the unitary intentionality in which I have a thing given in one glance. Then every manner of running through it yields a series of continuous acts that run in the first-posited sense and in a concordant sense according to every further givenness; everything, that is, is here "motivated" — every new positing, every phase of the unitary total positing, every new partial positing that may stand out but need not. And the same concerns every new content: motivated is precisely the positing together with its content, quality and matter, though each engages in a different way. In every moment I have a "divisible" matter and a unitary quality spreading over the matter, and so also elements and webs of motivation in coexistence, unities of "belonging-together" to "one thing." Against this stands: "the similar under similar circumstances motivates the similar," or better, the givenness of something similar, in positing consciousness, motivates the givenness of the similar. Two structures are set side by side — the motivational web that holds one thing's perception together in coexistence, and the associative law that carries expectation from one case to a similar one — and the question of their relation is put in the next unit.

How do the two hang together (225)? On the one hand I have, in the unity of an experience or of a context of experience, "intentional" connections under the title "motivations," tendencies that run back and forth, saturate themselves or remain open. They raise and carry one another, and here too we have reason-motivations: existence motivates co-existence, and so on — so everywhere that a "unity of consciousness" relates to a transcendent unity of an objectual in grasping something given. It would be worth considering whether in the mere unity of the stream of consciousness there is not also a unity in which each piece motivates the adjacent one.

On the other hand we have motivations that reach beyond the continuity of adjacent and inner connection, and likewise beyond the continuity of what is unitarily given on the constituted side (225–226). There too the performing ego need not live in the motivations. The similar reminds me of the similar, and by analogy with what was given on the one side together with the similar, I expect the similar on the other side. It is associated to it and "remembered" to it, but as an analogue of what is remembered in the ordinary, narrower sense.

Now one could ask whether the one and the other are really different (226). Where I experience the unity of belonging-together, where motivations run continuously back and forth, the true state of affairs, one could say, is that A points to B as belonging to it because earlier, in analogous cases, A appeared in one with B. All thingly apperception, all apperception of unities of connection among several things and thing-processes, would then rest on associative motivations. We come back to an original togetherness and an original sequence where nothing of motivations is yet present. But how far that would be thinkable, how far even the unity of a stream of consciousness could be a unity without any motivation at all — that is the question. The genetic reduction of perceptual motivation to association is entertained as a possibility and checked by a limit: a first togetherness with no motivation in it may be unthinkable. This is the seed of the later doctrine of passive synthesis, where association is itself a form of motivation rather than its motivation-free ground.

Further, we have to separate two things (226). In the unity of a thing-consciousness as a consciousness of belonging-together we have "conditionalities," a "because" and a "so" to be explicated in different directions: if I turn my eyes thus, if I stage the optical series of experience thus, then I must see this and that. That stands there as the unity of one thing with these and those parts; the mode of givenness of one part supports that of the other. If they are objectively to be together as these, and the one appears thus, then the other must appear thus, correlatively, under given circumstances of experience. If on the other hand one takes the case: under the earlier circumstances, in earlier connections Z, A appeared — in the present similar Z′, A′ is to be expected, or is already given in consequence — then we speak here too of "co-givenness," of A's being motivated by Z, of the A-positing by the Z-positing; but the being-motivated surely has a different sense here. The "if–then" of the kinaesthetic conditionalities from the Second Section and the "then–now" of associative expectation are both motivation, but not the same motivation: the first is a structure of one presentation, the second a link between two.

#### d) Motivation on the Noetic and Noematic Side

If we take in view the structure of thing-constituting consciousness, we see that all nature with space, time, causality and the rest is completely dissolved into a web of immanent motivations (226–227). In the unity of the total experience — the consciousness is of a thing existing there and an ego existing here with its body — we find manifold objectualities distinguishable and we find functional dependencies, which are not dependencies of the actual thing on the actual body and the actual ego in the world, in short not natural-scientific psychic and psychophysical dependencies, but also not dependencies of subjective appearances, as had by the experiencing subject, on real objectities that are posited or accepted as real. We can now consider the noetic experiences according to their relations of motivation, of the connection of belonging-together, according to which the advance from positing to positing, positing "in consequence," occurs in this peculiar character; or we consider the thetic correlates, the themata, in their noematic belonging-together, where again on this side the correlative "in consequence" appears. "Dissolved into a web of immanent motivations" is the strongest formulation in the section of the thesis that nature is a constituted unity, and it is guarded at once against two misreadings: the dependencies are neither psychophysical causation nor a dependence of appearances on real things. Motivation has a noetic and a noematic face, and the "in consequence" can be read on either.

We have static and dynamic belonging-togethers, and the static pass over into dynamic with change of the experiences — changes that have the character of "subjective" processes of the "I change," of an "I run through," possibly at will (227–228). But now we face the main difficulty. It is not said that in the unity of my stream of experience every experience is necessary, necessarily conditioned by the preceding and co-experienced experiences. When we say that every act-experience is motivated, stands in interweavings of motivation, that is not to say that every meaning is a meaning "in consequence." When I become aware of a thing, the thesis lying in the perceiving is not always a thesis "in consequence" — as when I see a shooting star flare up in the night sky or, wholly without mediation, hear the crack of a whip. Yet here too a kind of motivation can be shown, one enclosed in the form of inner time-consciousness. This form is something absolutely fixed, the subjective form of the now, the earlier and so on; I can change nothing in it. Nevertheless there is here a unity of belonging-together by virtue of which the judgment-positing "now this is" conditions the future-positing "something will be," or again: now I experience something — beforehand there has been an experience. Here we have one judgment motivated by another, but before any judgment the time-forms themselves motivate one another. In this sense one can say that the pervasive unity of the stream of consciousness is itself a unity of motivation. In the personal attitude that means: every act in the ego is subject to the constant apprehension that characterizes it as an act "of" the ego, as "my" experience. The concession that not every positing is "in consequence" is what keeps motivation from collapsing into determinism, and the recovery — that even the unmotivated whip-crack is motivated in its temporal form — is what lets the whole stream be called a unity of motivation without contradiction. The ego's "mine" is the personal-attitude name for that unity.

#### e) Empathy into Other Persons as Understanding Their Motivations

In empathy this "mine"-apprehension is of course also co-laid in (228–229). It is another ego, a certain one that I at first do not know, but do know according to ego-being in general. I know what "a personality," a human being, is in general, and it is the business of the experience of empathy to instruct me, in its progress, about his character, his knowledge and ability. It is here analogous to the case of physical thing-apperception. I perceive at one stroke a thing that has these visible properties: "one" thing. What further it is, experience must teach. The perceptual apprehension can reach variously far; I can recognize the thing as a spatial thing, in the dark; more closely, in better light, as a tree; then as an oak; then as the more familiar one I looked at more exactly yesterday. And always much remains unknown, imperfectly known. So too in the apperception "human being" very much is already enclosed. We know already from self-experience that a double possible apprehension is implied in it, that as natural object and that as person, and this holds too for the consideration of other subjects. Common to both is the givenness of the fellow human being through comprehension, but comprehension functions differently in the two cases. In the one, what is comprehended is nature, in the other, spirit; in the one, the foreign ego, experience, consciousness is posited introjectively, built on the fundamental apprehension and positing of material nature, apprehended as functionally dependent on it, attached to it; in the other, the ego is posited as person, as "simply" posited, and thereby posited as the subject of its personal and thingly surroundings, as related by understanding and agreement to other personalities, as fellow of a social context to which a unitary social surrounding world corresponds, while at the same time each of the fellows has his own surroundings, bearing the stamp of his subjectivity. The analogy with the oak seen in the dark makes empathy an ordinary apperception with an open horizon, and the two attitudes of §49 are then located inside that apperception as two ways of positing what it presents.

That is to say, other human beings are apprehended, analogously to one's own ego, as subjects of a surrounding world of persons and things toward which they behave in their acts (229). They are "determined" by this surrounding world, or by the spiritual world that surrounds and co-encompasses them, and exercise "determination" upon it: they stand under the lawfulness of motivation. The heading's claim is thus made good in one sentence: to empathize with a person is to understand a motivational subject, not to explain a natural one.

#### f) Nature-Causality and Motivation

The "because–so" of motivation has an entirely different sense from causation in the sense of nature (229). No causal research, however far it reaches, can improve the understanding we have when we have understood a person's motivation. The unity of motivation is a connection founded in the acts themselves, and when we ask for the "because," the ground of a personal behavior, we want to know nothing other than this connection. The causality of nature has, in the natural sciences, its correlate in laws of nature according to which it is to be determined univocally — at least in the domain of physical nature — what must follow under univocally determining circumstances. When by contrast it is said in the human-scientific sphere that the historian, the sociologist, the researcher of culture wants to "explain" human-scientific facts, that means he wants to lay motivations bare, to make intelligible how the human beings in question "came to" behave thus and so, what influences they underwent and exerted, what determined them in and to a commonality of action. When the researcher of spirit speaks of "rules" and "laws" under which such modes of behavior, or modes of forming cultural forms, stand, the "causalities" in question, which find their general expression in these laws, are anything but natural causalities. The historian's question is: what did the members of the society represent, think, value, will in their common life? How did these human beings reciprocally "determine" one another and let themselves be determined by the surrounding world of things, and how did they, in turn, reactively shape it? This is Husserl's answer to the Dilthey–Windelband debate on the human sciences: their "explanation" is understanding of motivation, and their "laws" are generalizations of motivational connections, so that the difference from natural science lies in the kind of causality, not in a preference for the individual over the general.

All spiritual modes of behavior are "causally" linked through relations of motivation (230). I suppose A because I know that B, C and so on are. I hear that a lion has broken loose and know that a lion is a bloodthirsty animal; therefore I am afraid to go into the street. The servant meets the master and, because he recognizes him as his master, greets him with deference. We note on a memorandum what we intend for tomorrow: the consciousness of the intention, combined with the knowledge of our forgetfulness, motivates the noting. In all these examples the because of motivation appears. There is no question here of a direction of judgment upon the real as such; the because expresses anything but a nature-causality, a real causality. I, as subject of the "premises of action," do not grasp myself inductively and really as the cause of the ego as subject of the "conclusion of action"; in other words, I who decide on the basis of these and those motives grasp neither the decision as a natural effect of the motives or motive-experiences, nor myself as subject of the decision as effected by the ego as subject of the motivating experiences. The same holds in every logical inference — it is not to be overlooked that the practical inference is an essential analogue of the logical inference but not the same as it — and so everywhere that I give expression to a motivational situation in the egological attitude. When I can establish this situation in the other by empathy, I say "I understand why the other decided thus, I understand why he passed this judgment," on what grounds. All these "causalities" can be brought out fully intuitively, precisely because they are motivations. The last sentence carries the weight: a motivation is intelligible without remainder because it is given as what it is, and that intuitive completeness is what distinguishes it from a cause, which is never given in this way.

Thingly causalities too — nature-real, natural ones — are intuitively given: we see how the hammer forges the iron, how the drill bores the hole (230–231). But the seen causalities are here mere appearances of "true" nature-causalities, just as the seen thing is a mere appearance of the true, physical thing. The true nature of the physicist is a methodically necessary substruction of thinking and is to be constituted only as such; only as "mathematical" does it have its truth. By contrast it makes no sense to underlay motivation, which is to be grasped originarily and intuitively, with something non-intuitive by substructions of thinking, as a mathematical index for an infinite manifold of intuitive appearances of which the given is only one.
