If I take the spirit in one with the body as a natural object, then it too is interwoven in natural causality, which is likewise given only by way of appearance — already because the one member of the relation of dependence, the physical, is only appearance and only substructively determinable. Sensations, sensuous feelings, reproductions, associations, apperceptions, and, founded on them, the whole course of psychic life in general, its position-takings included, is objectively — naturally, inductively — dependent on the physical body with its physiological processes and physiological build, and thereby dependent on real physical nature. But the physiological processes in the sense organs, in nerve and ganglion cells, do not motivate me when they psychophysically condition the appearance of sensation-data, apprehensions, psychic experiences in my consciousness. What I do not "know," what does not confront me in my experiencing, representing, thinking, doing as represented, perceived, remembered, thought, does not "determine" me spiritually. And what is not enclosed in my experiences, be it unnoticed or implicitly intentional, does not motivate me, not even unconsciously. The asymmetry between the two causalities is now stated as an asymmetry of givenness: nature's causality is intuited only as appearance of a mathematical substruction, while motivation is intuited as itself and admits no substruction. The last two sentences give the limit of the "hidden motivation" of §56 b: unconscious motives are still contents of my experience; brain processes are not, and so cannot motivate.

#### g) Relations Between Subjects and Things Under the Viewpoints of Causality and Motivation

How does it stand when we consider subjects as subjects of intersubjective motivations and thereby find them determining others and determined by them (231–232)? Is there not causation here? First: if I do something because I hear that another has behaved thus and so, then my doing is motivated by hearing and knowing, and that is no natural causation. But I do grasp myself thereby as determined by the other human being, by the other real ego; we speak, after all, of the reciprocal "effects" of human beings on one another. It is similar to the case in which an "abominable" noise in the street disturbs me at work and I find it abominable, annoying: my mood-state is then conditioned by hearing the noise with its determinate acoustic content, by the sensuous feeling and so on. We say the noise annoys me, it effects displeasure in me, as on the other hand the sounds of glorious music effect joy and admiration. Is that not a causal relation? Does anyone doubt that vibrations of the air propagate to my ear? On the other hand we also say: since M and N are, I infer that S is; my experience is related to M and N, to the facts, which may be geometrically ideal and to which I can nonetheless ascribe no effect. The example is chosen to make the question hard — the noise really does reach my ear — and the counter-example, the ideal premise that "moves" me without effecting anything, shows that motivation cannot be a species of causation.

If we look more closely we find, as before, that relations of motivation have different sides (232). On the one side, noetically, inferring is motivated by the judging of premises, willing by a seeing, hearing, valuing. On the other side it lies in the essence of these act-motivations that relations also obtain between the act-correlates and the acts, and among the correlates themselves, which likewise have their "because" and "so." These correlates may be characterized as actual; we may have, in the process, the proto-doxically positing consciousness of actual things and states of affairs. But they enter into these because-relations as these correlates "lying in" consciousness itself: the intuited as intuited, the feigned as feigned, the judged as judged. Precisely for that reason it makes no essential difference whether actualities correspond to the correlates or not, whether they have the "sense" of actualities at all. I fear the ghost and perhaps shudder, although I know that what I see is nothing actual. The plot of the play shakes me although it is nothing actual, as I very well know. Whatever changes feelings and acts of consciousness in general may show according as I find myself determined in them by actual things or by mere phantasy-things of art, that is a matter of indifference for our question. Motivation's independence from the object's existence, established for the single subject in §55, is here extended to the noematic side of motivation generally: what motivates is always the correlate as correlate, and the theatre is the proof.

Accordingly a fundamental distinction is to be drawn (232–233). On the one side there are relations of the real subject to real objects — things, persons — which are actually real, hence real-causal effects; the objects and persons are here posited as natural, as real, as dependent on one another in their real existence and being-so. On the other side there are relations between subjects, which are not posited as causal-real objects, and intuited, judged, and so on, things "as such" — that is, between subjects and thing-noemata — relations that necessarily have their reverse side in relations between certain acts of the subjects, acts of intuition, acts of thinking; and likewise relations between subjects and other human beings and their acts, which are not actually causal relations but relations that establish themselves through empathy between the acts and motivations of the one subject and those of the other. In these the respective subject that undergoes the "influences" is motivated by its own experiences, or only by the correlates of these experiences, or by the correlates of empathy as such. The intersubjective case is thereby assimilated to the ghost: the other's act motivates me as an empathy-correlate, and whether the other is posited as a real cause is a further, separable positing.

The subject may, as it normally does — where it does not perform empathy into imagined subjects — posit the empathized subject, the fellow human being and his experiences, as actualities and as natural realities, just as it can posit itself as such a reality; and then the relation of motivation can indeed transform itself for it into a real-causal one, the motivational because into a real because (233). I am annoyed by the noise outside — the apprehension can be exactly the same as: I am annoyed by the street-song that pursues me in phantasy. In the first case the correlate is posited as actual, in the latter not. And where it is posited as actual, I can understand the situation causally; the change of attitude is barely noticeable, although it is an essential change. The real process outside acts causally on me as a reality: the falling of the hammer shakes the air, the shaking excites my organ of hearing, and in consequence the noise is awakened in me as a real ego. The barely noticeable but essential change of attitude is the whole problem of §62 in miniature: the same experience, the same annoyance, can be read as motivated or as caused, and nothing in the experience forces one reading, only the positing of its correlate as a reality.

It is analogous with the intersubjective relations of dependence (233–234). Of course the foreign personality is given to us in comprehension as related to a body, and as one with it it stands there. Is this unity then not a real psychophysical, causal unity? Looked at more closely, we find, living in comprehensive consciousness — when, say, we deal with other persons, they turn to us speaking and we turn to them, receive commands from them, fulfil them — not the naturalistic unity between body and soul, as little as the things we see there as surrounding things are things of nature. The editors note that the continuation of the text of Supplement VII begins at this point. The things surrounding us are our intuitive counterpart; they have their colors, smells and so on; they are exactly those that the given experience shows us, admittedly given imperfectly in it, but we need only step closer, look at them "from all sides," and we would get to know them "completely" — completely, that is, for our purposes. The thing-qualities that stand out ever better in this way, the "sensuous," "secondary" ones, are the qualities constitutive of the surrounding things, the only ones proper to them; no extension of experience leads here beyond such qualities. So in the sense of the naive personal and interpersonal experiential apprehension there do not lie, behind the actually intuitive things, "physical" things, nor behind the intuitive qualities other, non-intuitive, "primary" qualities of which the former would be mere "signs," mere "subjective appearances." The things of our socially common world, the world of our conversation and our praxis, have precisely the qualities with which we actually, optimally, see them. Everything here is intuitive thingliness, and no one apprehends seeing and hearing in relation to these things as real-causal processes, neither his own nor the other's. One simply looks at the things; that is a doing of the subject, but no natural-causal relation. The subject is not a natural-scientific object; it acts, but it is not a theme. The personal world's things are thus given a complete ontology of their own — secondary qualities, optimal givenness, no primary qualities behind them — and the seeing subject, being agent and not theme, cannot appear in that world as a term of a causal relation.

As for the persons who confront us in society, their bodies are of course intuitively given to us like other surrounding objects, and in one with them their personality (234–235). But we do not find there two externally interwoven things, bodies and persons; we find unitary human beings who deal with us, and the bodies are included in the human unity. In their intuitive content — in what is typical of bodiliness generally and in many particularities changing from case to case: facial expression, gesture, the spoken "word," its tone — the spiritual life of the persons expresses itself, their thinking, feeling, desiring, their doing and leaving undone. So too, already, their individual spiritual character, which of course comes ever more completely to givenness in the course of states that become intelligible to us in their connection as well. Everything here is intuitive: as the external world and the body, so the bodily-spiritual unity of the human being standing there.

I hear the other speak, see his facial play, lay these and those conscious experiences and acts into him, and let myself be determined by them thus and so (235–236). The facial play is seen facial play and is the immediate bearer of sense for the consciousness of the other — for his will, say, which in empathy is characterized as the actual will of this person and as a will addressed to me through his communication. This will, so characterized, or the empathizing consciousness of this will that posits in the manner of empathy, motivates me in my counter-will, in my submitting, and so on. There is no talk of a causal relation — say between the optically effective thing "head" and face of the other and my face-appearance, or between the other's production of voice and the excitation of my ear — and just as little of any other psychophysical relations. The other's expressions determine me, and that is already a kind of motivation, to attach to them a sense in the other's consciousness. And the expression is just the seen expression, which I, seeing, as little bring into causal relations with my seeing, my sensations, my appearances as in any simple sensuous perceiving otherwise. It is by no means as if we grasped causality only with gaps, superficially: we are simply not in the attitude to grasp nature-causality. In empathy consciousness is set in relation to consciousness, my will and the foreign will in a determinate milieu of consciousness, and in a somewhat modified way from that in individual consciousness one act here motivates another — modified because first my will and the empathy of the other's will stand in a because-relation, but then also my will and the other's will. This motivation has its necessary presuppositions as motivation, not real presuppositions as a real state of consciousness — the familiar ones, such as "I see the other's facial play." To carry nature-causality in here would be to lose the attitude. The two-step structure is the point: my will is motivated by my empathic consciousness of the other's will, and through it by the other's will itself, so that intersubjective motivation is a chain of motivations and not, at any link, a causal transmission. That is also why the naturalistic reading is not a partial view of the same relation but a change of attitude that loses it.

#### h) Body and Spirit as Comprehensive Unity; “Bespirited” Objects

The thoroughly intuitive unity that presents itself where we grasp a person as such — as a person speaking to persons or listening to their speaking, say, working together with them, watching their doing — is the unity of "expression" and "expressed," which belongs to the essence of all comprehensive unities; the note refers to Supplement VIII (236). The unity body-spirit is not the only one of this kind. When I read the "pages and lines" of this book, or read in this "book," grasp the words and sentences, physical things stand there: the book is a body, the pages are pages of sheets of paper, the lines are blackenings and physical impressions on certain places of these papers. Do I grasp that when I "see" the book, when I "read" the book, when I "see" that it is written, what is written, said, what is said? Obviously I am attuned quite differently. I do have certain "appearances"; the physical thing and the physical occurrences on it appear, stand there in space in a determinate orientation to "my" center of apprehension, before me, right, left, exactly as if in my experiencing I were directed to the corporeal. But I am not directed to it. I see the thingly insofar as it appears to me, but I "live comprehendingly in the sense." And as I do so, there stands before me the spiritual unity of the sentence and of the sentence-contexts, and these in turn have their character — the determinate stylistic peculiarity, for instance, that presses itself on me and distinguishes this book as a literary product from another of the same genre. The book is chosen as the clearest case in which what appears and what one is directed to come apart: the paper appears in full orientation, and the theme is nonetheless the sense.

Now one could say: with the physically appearing, as a first objectity, a second is connected, precisely the sense that "ensouls" the physical (237). But I ask: am I directed to a second objectity only externally linked with the first? Is not what I am directed to rather a thoroughly fused unity that does not stand beside the physical at all? It is surely so that this is no connection in which the parts are "outside one another," no linkage in which each part could also be for itself apart from the connecting form.

Take a suitably altered example: I have before me a piece of jewelry, and my interest is exclusively in a beautiful sapphire set in it (237). I regard it, I am turned to it in an experience directed at it; the rest of the jewelry appears but does not fall within the frame of my observing perception. Or I look, as a natural scientist, at an organ I am to dissect, for itself; the rest of the physical body from which I cut it out I see but do not observe. Is it so, or analogous, in the case of the attitude toward something spiritual — is it something attached to the appearing bodily in a way similar to that in which a physical part is attached to another part, which I attend to without also attending to the bodily? One recognizes that the situation is entirely different. The sapphire and the organ are foils: they show what "attending to a part while the rest appears" really is, and that reading is not that.

Certainly I can say that a physical thing has an ensoulment and has a diverse yet connected sense (237). The word, the sentence, the whole piece of writing — the drama, the treatise — has its spiritual content, its spiritual "sense," and therein a connection of physical nature appears. I can attend to this at any time; I can take my attitude so that the observing, experiencing, attending regard, the thematic intention, enters into these appearances and means the appearing spatial existence. Then it is simply "there." I can further return from there into the attitude in which the drama, the treatise or its individual sentences are my object; but now I have an object that is no longer there in space, at this place; that makes no sense in this case.

I can further, reflecting and holding the one object as object of the one attitude together with the other as that of the other, say: the existing book, the existing page of paper has a special sense, is ensouled by a meaning (237–238). The book with its leaves of paper, its binding and so on is a thing. To this thing no second thing, the sense, is attached; rather the sense penetrates the physical whole "ensouling" it in a certain way — insofar, namely, as it ensouls every word, but again not every word for itself, but connections of words that combine through the sense into sense-endowed forms, these again into higher forms, and so on. The spiritual sense is, ensouling the sensuous appearances, in a certain way fused with them, instead of being merely connected with them in a connected juxtaposition.

It is clear that this analysis, still insufficient as it is, applies first of all to all works of spirit, all works of art and all things that have a comprehensive spiritual sense, a spiritual significance — and naturally, with the necessary changes, to all things of ordinary life within the cultural sphere, the actual sphere of life (238). A drinking glass, a house, a spoon, a theater, a temple means something. And it is always something different to see the thing as a thing and the thing as a use-object, as a theater, as a temple. Here the spiritual sense sometimes belongs to a purely ideal sphere and has no relation to existence, sometimes has such an existence-relation, while yet never, in the proper sense, being something real and thingly, bound on as a second existence to the physical existence. Everywhere it holds that this is a fundamental mode of apperception, a peculiar experiencing attitude, in which what sensuously appears, the pre-given, does not become the sensuously given, perceived, experienced, but helps to constitute, in its "psychic fluid," in the unity of the different apprehension, a distinctive objectivity — and Husserl's note adds that the attitude itself does not constitute the spiritual formation; the physical-spiritual is already pre-constituted, pre-thematically pre-given. Obviously the sensuous appearance has here another "subjective function," another subjective mode, than in the case of outer thematic experience. The generalization from the book to the spoon is what turns the analysis into an account of the cultural world as such; the note guards against reading the attitude as producing sense, which is already there before the attitude finds it.

More precisely we would have to say that the cases differ — the case of the use-object, say, and that of the written work, the pictorial work and the like (238–239). In the latter the written signs are inessential, but the associated word-sounds are not, and these in turn are not posited as memory, as existing, or even as "appearing." In the case of the use-object certain sensuous existence-determinations of the object enter into the total apprehension; I look at the form of the spoon, for that belongs essentially to a spoon. Here one will say that perception with its existence-thesis is directly the substrate for the spiritual grasping. But here too it holds that the spiritual is nothing second, nothing bound on, but precisely ensouling; and the unity is not a connection of two but one, and only one is there. The physical being can be grasped for itself, through the natural attitude, as natural being, as thingly being, performing the existence-thesis, and insofar as one can take this attitude it is "contained" therein. But what now comes is not a surplus set upon it, but a spiritual being that essentially includes the sensuous and yet again does not include it as a part, as one physical thing is a part of another. In some cases we have an actual nature, an existence as substrate; in some — as already indicated — a physically unreal that has no existence. The euphony of the rhythms of a drama read on the page is not to be posited as real existence; as little as the drama is anywhere as something spatially existing, this euphony is anywhere. To the ideal spiritual unity the ideal euphony belongs. A long note, which Husserl himself calls quite insufficient, separates two claims the text runs together: first, that in denying attachment he denies a linked externality of two things which appear as parts of equal level when the regard passes from one to the other; second, that through the spiritual sense-giving of a sensuous substrate the sensuous receives, so to speak, an inner life, so that, as with a drama's physical substrate of manifold sensuous members unitarily ensouled, the spiritual sense is in this sense too not beside the physical, with a cross-reference to 243. The "contained" is the crucial hedge: the thing is in the cultural object as a possible attitude is in an actual one, not as a part in a whole, and for the ideal work not even that.

Let us now leave the domain of these partly real, partly ideal "spiritual objects," formations of "objective spirit," and consider again the spiritual living beings, these peculiar ensouled beings — human beings, we shall say, though naturally including all animals (239–240). The question above was: is the human being a connection of two realities, and do I see him as that? If I do, I grasp a corporeal existence; but I am not in this attitude when I see human beings. I see the human being, and in seeing him I also see his body. In a certain way the apprehension of the human being goes through the appearance of the body that is a living body. It does not, so to speak, stop at the body, does not direct its arrow at it, but through it — and not at a spirit connected with it either, but precisely at the human being. And the human-apprehension, the apprehension of this person there who dances and laughs merrily and chats or discusses science with me, is not the apprehension of something spiritual fastened to the body but the apprehension of something that accomplishes itself through the medium of the body-appearance, essentially includes the body-appearance in itself, and constitutes an object of which I can say: it has a bodiliness, it has a body which is a physical thing, constituted thus and so, and it has experiences and dispositions to experience. And it has peculiarities that possess both sides at once: to walk thus and so, to dance thus and so, to speak thus and so. The human being in his movements and actions, in his talking, writing and the rest, is not a mere connection, a tying-together of a thing called soul with another called body. The body is, as living body, through and through a soul-filled body. Every movement of the body is soul-filled — coming and going, standing and sitting, walking and dancing — and likewise every human accomplishment, every product; the note refers to Supplement IX. The image of the arrow that passes through the body without stopping at it is the section's answer to introjection: the person is not behind the body, nor in it, but is what the body-appearance presents when one is directed through it.

The human-apprehension is such that it passes as "sense" through the body-apprehension — not as if a temporal sequence were in question, first body-apprehension and then human-apprehension, but it is one apprehension that has the body-apprehension as its founding substrate, constituting bodiliness, for the comprehensive apprehension of sense: basically and in the main just as the wording is the "body" for the ensouling "sense" (240–241). Further, the printed page or the spoken lecture is no connected duality of wording and sense; rather each word has its sense, and perhaps parts of the word already have word-character, as already there a sense pointing ahead refers to a new sense, to new words; as words combine into word-formations, into sentences, sentences into sentence-connections, by the ensouling sense's having such a rhythm, such a weaving of sense, such a unity — a unity that has its hold, or better its bodiliness, in word-substrates — so that the whole of the lecture is through and through a unity of body and spirit, in its articulations continually a unity of body and spirit, which unity is part of a unity of a higher level, until finally the lecture itself stands there as the unity of the highest level.

Exactly so with the unity human being (241–242). The body is not an undivided physical unity, undivided from the standpoint of its "sense," of the spirit; rather the physical unity of the body standing there, changing thus and so or at rest, is manifoldly articulated, and according to circumstances now more, now less determinately. And the articulation is an articulation of sense, which means it is not one to be found within the physical attitude, as if to every physical division, every distinction of physical properties, "significance" accrued — significance as living body, an own sense, an own "spirit." Rather the apprehension of a thing as a human being — more closely, as a human being who speaks, reads, dances, is angry and rages, defends himself or attacks — is one that ensouls manifold but distinguished moments of the appearing corporeal objectivity, gives the individual moment sense, psychic content, and in turn links the already ensouled particulars, according to the demands lying in the sense, into a higher unity and finally into the unity of the human being. It is only to be noted that at any time only little of the bodily actually falls into appearance, only little appears in direct ensoulment, while very much is "supposed," can be co-apprehended, co-posited, and is co-posited in a more or less indeterminate, vague way — what is co-posited bodily and has co-posited sense. A large part can remain wholly indeterminate and yet retains this much determination: a certain bodily with a certain spiritual, a certain something that, as a horizon of experience, is to be determined more closely by experience. The articulation of the body by sense, not by physics, is the precise statement of what "expression" means in §56 h: the units of the expressive body are cut by what they express, and the horizon-structure of thing-perception applies to them unchanged.

This apperception of spirit transfers to one's own ego, which as the apperceiver of other spirits obviously need not, for itself, be apperceived in this way — as a comprehensive unity, as spirit — and which, if it is not so apperceived, functions as the non-objectivated pure ego (242–243). To the apprehension of the human being in the spiritual sense I come, in relation to myself, through comprehension of others, insofar, namely, as I comprehend them not only as central members for the rest of the surrounding world but also for my body, which is for them an object of the surrounding world. Precisely thereby I comprehend them as apprehending me similarly to the way I apprehend them, and so as apprehending me as a social human being, as a comprehensive unity of body and spirit. Therein lies an identification between the ego I find in direct inspection — the ego that has my body over against it — and the ego of the other's representation of me, the ego that the other can understand and posit in one with my body, as what is "externally" representable to him, in acts that I on my side attribute to the other. The comprehensive representation that others have, or can have, of me serves me to apprehend myself as a social "human being," and so in a quite different way from that of directly grasping inspection. Through this complexly built kind of apprehension I order myself into the association of humanity, or rather create the constitutive possibility for the unity of this "association." Only now am I properly an ego over against the other and can say "we"; only now do I first become also "I" and the other precisely another; "we" are all human beings, alike to one another, as human beings capable of entering into intercourse with one another and forming human connections. All this accomplishes itself in the spiritual attitude and without any "naturalization." But we already know that we can turn every comprehensive unity "human being" into a unity of nature, into biological and psychophysical objectivity, in which the pure spirit no longer functions as a member of a comprehensive unity but a new phenomenal unity, an objective thingliness, constitutes itself. That transfers to myself as a natural object — as one sees, a very mediated mode of representation. Through the change of attitude the spiritual ego transforms itself, in my own case too, though more mediately than in that of others, into the psychic ego of natural-scientific psychology. This is the constitutive genealogy of the social self, and its order is the reverse of the naturalistic one: I become a human being among human beings by taking over the others' comprehension of me, and only from there, by a further and doubly mediated turn, a psychophysical object. The difficulty §53 flagged in self-naturalization is thereby explained: it is the last and most indirect apperception, not the first.

The analysis just concluded is a fundamental one, Husserl says, and its scope is the whole class of spiritual objects, all unities of body and sense — not individual human beings only but human communities, all cultural formations, all individual and social works, institutions (243). The claim that body and spirit are never a combination of two things is thereby extended from the person to everything that carries sense on a physical bearer.

That extension is at once protected against a misreading (243). To deny that body and spirit are ever a connection of two things does not prevent us from ascribing to the body a bodily unity and to sense a unity of sense, so that the bodily-spiritual unity we call a state or a church contains two kinds of unity in itself: bodily unities as material-corporeal unities, wherever corporeal existence enters into the whole of the "bespirited" object, and spiritual unities. For the individual human being this means a unitary body, that is, a sense-bearing ensouled physical body, and a unitary spirit. For a state, a people, an association, we have a plurality of bodies standing in the physical relations that direct or indirect intercourse requires, and everything that belongs to that has sense; each body has its spirit, but overarching them they are joined by a common spirit that is nothing beside them but a sense or spirit spanning them — an objectity of higher level. Two unities are distinguishable within one object; two things are not. The higher-order communal objects of §49 e and §51 are here given their constitutive form: a common spirit that is a sense spanning bodies, not an entity added to them.

Ideal spiritual objects — a drama, a written work in general, a musical work, and in a certain way every other artwork — differ in that their sensuous body is not an existing one; the sensuous body of a picture is not the picture hanging on the wall (243). Husserl notes that this would not be hard to develop but lies too far from the present context. The remark separates two kinds of bespirited object by the mode of being of the bearer, and it is the germ of the later distinction between bound and free idealities.

In any case, with respect to the unity of spirit that makes up the "sense" of the body in the individual human case, Husserl announces that something remains to be noted, and the next unit supplies it (243–244).

Empathy into persons is nothing other than the apprehension that understands the sense, that grasps the body in its sense and in the unity of sense it is to carry (244). To carry out empathy is to grasp an objective spirit, to see a human being, a crowd. There is here no apprehension of the body as bearer of something psychic in the sense that the body is posited and experienced as a physical object and then something else added to it, as if it were apprehended as something in relation to or in connection with something else. It is an objectivation of a higher level that overlays the other apprehension-stratum so that the unity of one object constitutes itself, an object that in turn implies — without any connection that would presuppose separation — an object-stratum of lower and one of higher level, distinguished only afterwards. Empathy is redefined as sense-understanding, not as the transfer of psychic states, and the order of constitution is reversed relative to naturalism: the strata are found by later analysis inside a unity given first; they are not assembled into it.

The unity given in the apprehension of spiritual being can be distinguished, by a change of apprehending attitude, into body and sense (244–245). The human body appears in a perceptual appearance and stands there as actuality, its correlate, ordered into the surrounding actuality of the one who understands and who grasps with this body the human person as a fellow. Strictly, he does not posit, does not grasp in the proper sense of an actually performed thesis, the actuality of the body when he grasps the person expressing herself in it — as little as, in reading, we posit the written sign on the paper in an actual thesis of experience and make it the theme of theoretical or practical position-takings: the sign appears, but we live in the performance of the sense. So the body appears, but we perform the acts of comprehension, grasp the persons and the personal states that "express" themselves in its appearing content. As expressed they belong only to the appearing body of my surroundings; but this belonging is a peculiar relation that has nothing less than the sense of the founded natural unity human being as animal being, as zoological object, and rather precedes the constitution of every such unity. The reading analogy is exact: the bearer appears without being thematically posited. And the final clause states the section's central thesis about order: expression-belonging precedes the constitution of the psychophysical unity, so the personalistic apprehension is not derived from the naturalistic but is what the naturalistic presupposes.

Compared with the unities of word-sound and sense drawn on above, these are irreal unities, whereas the unity of body and spirit constitutes itself as a higher unity of two real unities (245). It requires its own constitutive manifolds, as shows in the demonstration of this unity in the consciousness of its explicit givenness. The analogy with reading therefore has a limit: the sign-sense unity is irreal, the body-spirit unity is real on both sides and needs a constitution of its own.

A real objectivity of experience must, if it is to come to givenness, take the two real unities body and soul together and follow their relations of dependence on real circumstances and on one another as a unity in experience (245). Instead of merely looking at the body, or, living in understanding, merely at the person, we have first to take the connection established between expression and expressed as a whole and see how this whole behaves in concordant experience. Husserl grants that even before the experiencing turn and grasp this unity of expression and expressed is conscious, in the course of the perceptive apprehensions, as a single reality, perceptively present as a formation of two levels, such that the dependence of the whole formation on circumstances essentially includes the dependence of the occurrences of the upper level on those of the lower. The passage marks where the naturalistic apprehension gets its purchase: not on the person, but on the two-level whole taken as a real unity subject to circumstances.

Indeed, a unity of realizing apperception is constantly a lived experience into which the regard of the pure ego can turn and grasp the founded reality with its states and circumstances (245). But the expression-unity is the presupposition for the constitution of the founded reality as one enclosing levels; it is not in itself already that reality. Through expression the other's person is there for the experiencing subject at all, and it must be there at all before it can enter as a level into a real unity of higher level, together with what serves as expression. Two claims are kept apart: expression makes the person present; realizing apperception makes the person a level of a psychophysical reality. The first is a condition of the second and is not the second.

Husserl then tests whether the whole real relation between body and spirit could reduce to the expression-unity (246). It is in itself quite thinkable: spiritual being would express itself in the bodily to the extent that spirit was graspable, but the psychophysical unity would be lacking, body and soul would not appear in real connection. One might object that the connection of expression and expressed is itself already apprehensible as real: if the body has the peculiarity that personal states attach in empirical regularity to its general type and in particular to certain of its occurrences, facial expression, spoken word, then these occurrences have real spiritual consequences, and conversely, if spiritual states run their course and in parallel certain expressions and gestures occur at the body, then the spiritual has real consequences at the body and is experienced causally accordingly. One need only state such propositions to see that the view cannot be carried through. A parallelistic connection of this kind creates no reality of a higher level. We would have two realities, each with its states and real properties; within limits there would be a correspondence, one could infer from parallel states to parallel states and use one as an indication of the other; but not a single new real property would arise, and there would be no talk of a causality binding body and spirit, since that would presuppose that the two realities took on for one another the function of circumstances with respect to their real states. As the situation was assumed, striking one reality would change nothing for the other; the entire manifold of its states would be the same. This is the section's first refutation of parallelism, and it is constitutive, not metaphysical: a mere correspondence founds nothing new. §63 returns to it.

In truth, in the fitting attitude the human being stands over against us as a real unity with real properties we call psychophysical, which presuppose a causality of body and soul in relation to one another (247). Through such causality a founded unity of its own kind is made possible. In the sense of the natural apperception of the human being there is such a thing as health and illness in countless forms, illness of the body having psychic disturbances and manifold experiential consequences for the soul; experience also gives the reverse causalities, for instance that the will, having the body as the field of its freedom, brings bodily occurrences in its train. Husserl declines to list all the forms of psychophysical causality, which, however much denied in philosophical argumentation after the fact, govern the plain experiential apprehension of animal being. What matters is that through them something enters the experiential apprehension that is not without further ado contained in the unity of expression and expressed. A footnote attached here, one of Husserl's later annotations, adds that the attitude itself does not constitute the spiritual formation: the physical-spiritual is already pre-constituted, pre-thematic, pre-given. The note guards the whole chapter against an idealism of attitudes — the personalistic attitude finds the bespirited object, it does not make it.

The body we apprehend as expression of spiritual life is at the same time a piece of nature, ranged into the general causal nexus, and the spiritual life we grasp through bodily expression and understand in its motivational nexuses appears, by its attachment to the body, as itself conditioned by natural processes, apprehended naturally (247). The unity of body and spirit is a double one, and correlatively a double apprehension, personalistic and naturalistic, is enclosed in the unitary apprehension of the human being. The double unity is the result the Third Chapter takes up; here it is stated as a fact about the one apperception "human being," which contains both.

### §57. Pure Ego and Personal Ego as Object of Reflexive Self-Apperception

Taken as it was found in the inspection of self (inspectio), that is, without regard to its unity with the expressing body that empathy gives us, the personal ego at first seems not to differ from the pure ego (247–248). The body is then my possession, over against me in the widest sense like everything pre-given and ego-foreign, like the things of my surroundings — though with a special subjectivity, as we saw earlier: it is in a special sense my own, organ and system of organs of the ego, organ of perception, organ of my effects into the "outer," extra-bodily surroundings. I myself am the subject of the actual "I live": I suffer and do, am affected, have my counterpart and am affected, attracted, repelled, variously motivated by it. Husserl then sharpens the point about reflection: self-perception is a reflection, a self-reflection of the pure ego, and by its essence presupposes an unreflected consciousness. The unreflected ego-life, related to pre-givennesses of every kind, to an ego-foreign world of things and goods, takes on a distinguished form, that of self-reflection or self-perception, which is thus a special mode of "I live" within the general nexus of ego-life. Spelled out: I perceive that I have perceived this or that and still do, that this or that affected me unperceived before and drew my attention, that I remain fixed on it, that a joy moved me and still moves me, that I made a resolve and hold to it. Through such reflections I know of my unreflected ego-life; they bring its structures into the focus of noticing. Everything said so far is true of the pure ego, and that is the difficulty: a personal ego has not yet come into view.

Reflection can be a continued and continuous unity of reflections: reflecting, I pass from one cogito that has become a grasped object to another and again to others, and the ego that is the subject in every cogito identifies itself essentially in the process; the manifold actions and passions of the ego are originally given as those of the one identical ego, and correlatively the manifold possessions, the affecting, the pre-given of the immanent or transcendent sphere, as possessions of the same ego (248). All these are descriptions that fit the pure ego. The identity found across reflections is the pure ego's identity as pole; it is not yet a character.

The personal ego enters with a different kind of experience (249). When in living, unreflected acts of reflecting I move in fields of reflection, the objectivated subjectivities, I experience how I "behave" under various subjective circumstances, that is, in relation to my sphere of respective pre-givennesses, my surrounding world in the widest sense; and when I enter into the interweaving of the motivations of my cogito, into the open and hidden intentionalities of motivation, I experience how I am motivated by them and am accustomed to be, what experiential peculiarity I possess as a motivation-subject of these motivating circumstances in general — in short, what kind of personal subject I am. All of this at first without accompanying conceptual fixation and without deliberating, without "reflecting" in the quite different sense of thinking and asserting. So Husserl distinguishes from the pure ego-reflection, the reflection on the pure ego that belongs essentially to every cogito, the reflective-thematic experience on the basis of the developed experiential apperception, whose intentional object is this empirical ego, the ego of empirical intentionality, as self-experience of the personal ego with reference to the experiential nexuses in which this personal ego demonstrates itself, by the acts it performs under the relevant motivating circumstances, according to its "personal peculiarities" or character-properties. The decisive word is "accustomed": the person is what shows itself in the regularity of how I let myself be motivated, and it is given in an experience with the structure of thing-experience, an apperception built up over time, not in the instantaneous reflection that gives the pure ego. Two later marginal notes register Husserl's dissatisfaction with this. One asks whether "habit" is the right title, since I have my position-takings and my way of taking positions not from mere habits but from freedom and from capacities of various kinds; the other asks whether the apperception really grows merely from repeated reflection on the motivations of my affections and actions, insists that as personal ego I am a human among humans, and states that the person is the subject of capacities, whose becoming and growth I learn to know in an experience of their own kind. §59 takes up exactly this correction.

The inner reflection performed here does not exclude but includes my apprehending myself as a human ego also in the relations in which I stand to other human beings, since as personal ego I behave toward others too, as belonging with me to my surrounding world (249–250). But if I hold to what pure and proper self-perception offers and restrict myself to the self-preservation of my personality under the circumstances of my behavior in the surrounding world, I can disregard the apprehension-stratum that enters through my presenting myself at the same time as the same one whom others apprehend empathically from outside. And even if I strike out every apperception that relates to others, and with it everything it contributes to the remaining apperception of the surrounding world and of myself, there evidently remains my ego behaving in a regulated way in my pure, natural, thingly surrounding world, and a restricted personal apperception. Into proper self-intuition, self-perception and self-memory, nothing falls from the start of the presentation of how I would look from a "there," from another's standpoint. The passage isolates a solipsistic layer of personal apperception, the person's regulated behavior toward things alone, from the intersubjective layer. A long footnote attached to the preceding paragraph balances it: the personal ego is the human ego; repeated reflection on others' like behavior under like circumstances yields an inductive apperception; I come to know myself as a personal "reality" only insofar as I apperceive myself as a human being in the human context and find occasion often enough to observe the regularity of my own behavior, so that the personal reflection I exercise is intentionally very mediated. The note then lists what remains open — that a piece of inductive apperception concerning myself grows before the experience of others, as somatological, my body as substrate of bodily capacities; how the ego as pole plays its role in this; and that an active capacity is not a habit, not an inductively constituted peculiarity, not a merely associative formation — and closes by calling the analysis of personality here very imperfect. The section's candor is part of its doctrine: the inductive account of the person is offered as a first layer with its inadequacy marked.

I, the personal ego, am for myself, after the development of the empirical ego-apperception, a pre-givenness, as much as the thing is pre-given to me once thing-apperception has developed (250–251). Just as "experience," in the sense of the deliberate observation carried further after the development of thing-apperception and of the arbitrarily ordered satisfaction of factual interest in series of experiences, teaches me to know the thing more closely — which leads to observational science — so also for the empirical ego: I deliberately "go into experience" and learn to know myself, perhaps with a purely observational interest, "more closely." Self-perception as personal self-perception and the nexus of reflective self-experiences "teach" me that my pure ego-acts run their course in a regulated way under their subjective circumstances. And I see eidetically, or can see, that in accordance with these regulated courses the "presentation" ego-person, the empirical ego-apperception, must necessarily develop and go on developing, so that when I reflect after a course of experiences, a course of cogitations of various kinds, I must find myself constituted as a personal ego. The course of experience of pure consciousness is necessarily a course of development in which the pure ego must take on the apperceptive form of the personal ego, must become the core of intentions of all kinds that would find their demonstration or fulfilment in series of experience of the kind indicated. The conclusion is eidetic and strong: personhood is not an optional overlay on the pure ego but a necessary form its life takes, because a regulated life necessarily apperceives its own regularity. What §58 then asks is whether this apperception is what makes the ego a person or only what makes it known as one.

### §58. Constitution of the Personal Ego Before Reflection

Reflecting, I always already find myself as a personal ego; but originally it constitutes itself in the genesis that pervades the flux of experience, and the great question is whether the personal ego constitutes itself on the basis of ego-reflections, quite originally on the basis of pure self-perception and self-experience (252). There are lawfulnesses like those under the title "association" that belong to the experience-stream with its whole stock, to the cogitations occurring in it as to the other experiences; so the question is whether merely by such lawfulnesses apperceptions in general, and specifically those of the personal ego behaving in a regulated way with respect to subjective circumstances, can develop, so that reflections on cogitations play no privileged role, or whether precisely they have a special and quite essential constitutive function. Must I run through my modes of behavior in reflective experience for the personal ego to be able to become conscious as their unity, or can it already be "conscious" in pre-givenness before it was originally given through such identifying and realizing series of experience, which look as reflections at the cogitations, at the behavior with respect to circumstances? What then organizes itself in the pre-reflective sphere? Certainly associations form, references forward and back develop, as with the unnoticed sensuous and thingly "backgrounds." So a stock is already there, and in subsequent reflection, in memory, I can and must find something shaped. That is the presupposition for the "explication," for the fully conscious setting-out of the "if" and "so," and for that identification of the ego with respect to its circumstances in which the ego "properly" constitutes itself as a personal-real unity. A parenthesis asks whether the same holds for thing-constitution and says that Husserl has in fact shown it in the Transcendental Logic. The answer to the dilemma is that reflection presupposes a pre-formed stock: explication makes "proper" what association has already shaped.

But even apart from the associative nexuses, the ego constituted in reflection points back to another (252). Originally I am not a unity out of associative and active experience, if experience means what it does for the thing. I am the subject of my life, and living, the subject develops; it experiences primarily not itself but constitutes natural objects, valued things, tools; as active it forms and shapes primarily not itself but things into works. The ego is originally not out of experience — in the sense of associative apperception in which unities of manifolds of the nexus constitute themselves — but out of life; it is what it is not for the ego but is itself the ego. This is the deepest correction the section makes to its own inductive account: the person is not first an object built up for the ego but the subject that builds, and its being precedes its being-for-itself.

The ego can therefore be more and other than the ego as apperceptive unity (252). It can have hidden capacities, dispositions, that have not yet come forward, not yet been apperceptively objectivated, as a thing has properties not yet drawn into the thing-apperception. We make all these distinctions in the ordinary personal consideration of human beings and accordingly in human-scientific, for instance historical, consideration, in ordinary experience: someone does not "know" himself, does not "know" what he is; he learns to know himself. Self-experience, self-apperception, constantly widens; "getting to know oneself" is one with the development of self-apperception, the constitution of the "self," and this proceeds in one with the development of the subject itself. The disanalogy with the thing is that here the object of apperception is itself developing, so that self-knowledge and self-formation run together.

What of a supposed beginning (253)? At the beginning of experience no constituted "self" is pre-given or on hand as an object; it is wholly hidden for itself and for others, at least in intuition, though others can already understand more empathically, since for them the form of subjectivity as a form constituting itself in development is experientially pre-delineated. The peculiarity of the spiritual subject is that in it the apperception "I" occurs, in which this "subject" is the "object," if not always the thematic one; in the thing no apperception "thing" occurs, but only in subjects. So one must separate "I, who am," on the subject side, from "I, who am" as object for me, that which in the existing I-am is presented, constituted, possibly meant in the specific sense: the me. What is meant is "the person" constituted for me, the ego that is conscious as a self. The distinction between the I and the me, subject side and object side of one being, is the formal result of §§57–58, and it is what allows Husserl to say both that the ego is "out of life" and that it is a pre-givenness like a thing.

Must one not also say that the sleeping ego, as against the waking, is complete immersion in the ego-material, in the hyle, undifferentiated ego-being, ego-submersion, whereas the waking ego sees the material over against itself and is now affected, does, suffers (253)? The ego posits the non-ego and behaves toward it; it constantly constitutes its counterpart, and in this process it is motivated and ever newly motivated, not arbitrarily but exercising "self-preservation." Setting aside a lower level in which "sensuous objectivity" grows as a unity "without acts," the ego develops constantly; its doing and suffering have after-effects. It practices, grows accustomed, is determined in the later by earlier behavior, the force of some motives grows; it "acquires" capacities, sets itself goals and in reaching them acquires practical capacities; it does not only do, but activities become goals, and systems of activities — to be able to play a piano piece smoothly — and corresponding capacities. Waking is defined here by the constitution of a counterpart, and the person's genesis by acquisition; both anticipate §59, and self-preservation as the non-arbitrary law of motivation anticipates the later doctrine of the ego as a substrate of habitualities.

### §59. The Ego as Subject of Capacities

The ego as unity is a system of "I can," and one must distinguish the physical "I can," bodily and bodily-mediated, from the spiritual (253).

I have power over my body; I am the one who moves this hand and can move it; I can play the piano — but it does not always work: I have unlearned it, I am out of practice; I practice my body (254). In the commonest activities I do not in general get out of practice, but after a long illness I have to learn to walk again, and quickly get back into it. I can also fall nervously ill and lose mastery over my limbs: "I cannot." In this respect I have become another. The examples show that the "I can" is a possession that can be lost, and that its loss changes who I am.

"I am bodily-practically normal" means the enduring normal lower stratum, that I can "naturally-freely" move my organs as organs of perception and as practical organs of sensuous life (254). I am spiritually normal in presenting when I can freely carry out my spatial experiences and phantasy-formations and freely run through my memories, in a typical, natural range, not without limit; I have a normal memory, a normal phantasy, a normal thinking-activity — I can draw inferences, compare, distinguish, combine, count, calculate; I can also value and weigh values, normally, like a "mature human being." On the other hand I have my peculiarity, my how of moving and doing, my individual valuations, my way of preferring, my temptations, my powers of overcoming certain groups of temptations against which I am proof, another being different in this, having other favorite motives, other temptations dangerous to him, other spheres of individual energy — but within normality, specifically the normality of youth, of age. Within this typicality there are of course special developments, conscious self-education, inner conversion, transformations through ethical goal-setting, practice. Normality is the typical range of the "I can"; individuality is the personal style within it; and the two are not on one level, since the second presupposes the first.

So the spiritual ego can be apprehended as an organism, an organism of capacities, of their development in a normal typical style, with the stages of childhood, youth, maturity, age (255). The subject "can" many things and is determined to action, according to its ability, by stimuli, by actual motives; it is active again and again according to its capacities and constantly transforms, enriches, strengthens, or weakens them by its doing. A capacity is no empty ability but a positive potentiality that in each case comes to actualization, is constantly in readiness to pass over into an activity which, as lived, points back to the belonging subjective ability, the capacity. But motivation is for consciousness something open, intelligible; the "motivated" decision is as such clear through the kind and force of the motives. Finally everything points back intelligibly to primal capacities of the subject and then to acquired capacities, sprung from earlier actuality of life. A marginal note asks whether "original character" is nothing other than a determinate motivation at the beginning, every later motivation being co-conditioned by earlier ones, answers that this would be a determinate kind of motivation and only for the beginning, and adds that the beginning must not be understood merely temporally. "Organism of capacities" replaces the bundle of habits: the person is a system of positive potentialities that its own activity reshapes, and its intelligibility is the intelligibility of motivation, not of causal law.

The personal ego constitutes itself in original genesis not only as a personality determined by drives, from the start and continually driven by original "instincts" and passively following them, but also as a higher, autonomous, freely active ego, guided in particular by rational motives, not merely drawn and unfree (256). Habits must form both for the originally instinctive behavior, so that the force of the drive of habit joins the instinctive drives, and for free behavior: yielding to a drive founds the drive to yield, habitually; likewise, letting oneself be determined by a value-motive and resisting a drive founds a tendency, a "drive," to let oneself again be determined by such a value-motive, and by value-motives in general, and to resist such a drive. Here habit and free motivation interweave; when I act freely again I do follow habit too, but I am free insofar as I follow the motive, reason, in free decision. From all this is to be distinguished the efficacy of "association" in which the personal empirical subject constitutes itself. If the personal subject means for the experience-stream a certain rule of development for the kinds of ego-behavior under subjective circumstances, and a certain rule of the manner of behaving in activities and passivities, then to this rule there corresponds a certain, so to speak, doxic habit, a certain familiarity for the respective behavior of the ego, certain tendencies of expectation or possible tendencies of expectation relating to the occurrence of that behavior in the stream of consciousness. In background consciousness this is no proper expecting but a protention directed at the future occurrence, which can become expectation when the ego's regard turns to it. But not only that: an objectivity constitutes itself, the subject of the modes of behavior; the system of such protentions and interweavings, which could be transformed into actual "if–so," into actual hypothetical and causal motivations, creates a new intentional unity, correlatively a new apperception. A marginal note adds that the habitual has its intentional relation to circumstances, that an instinctive drive is also related to circumstances but that habit carries an implicit horizon of similar memories, and asks how the expectation of yielding relates to the growing force and tendency of yielding itself. Two theses are laid side by side here without being merged. Freedom is habituated too, and is not thereby abolished, because what habit reinforces in the free case is the following of a motive; and the empirical person is constituted by a protentional familiarity with one's own behavior that can be turned into an apperceived object.

On the one side, then, we have tendencies that govern the "I do" and "I suffer," and forces that give it rules; on the other, tendencies of consciousness that characterize these acts and the ego after the fact and lend it apprehension (256). This is the distinction §58 needed: the forces that shape a life and the apperception that reads the shape off it are two things, and the person is the first before it is the second.

The whole consideration so far concerned the ego-unity that constitutes itself in the stream of life, first of all the ego that develops, co-forms, and constitutes together with all other apperceptions, above all in one with the developing thing-apperceptions (256–257). But not that alone: I am not only the subject, the ego, that can in a certain freedom regard a thing, move the eyes in seeing; I am also the subject that is accustomed to take pleasure in such and such things, habitually desires this and that, goes to eat when the time comes — subject of certain feelings and habits of feeling, habits of desire, habits of will, now passive, now active. It is clear that strata constitute themselves here in subjectivity, insofar as certain groups of ego-affections or passive ego-acts organize themselves relatively on their own and close together constitutively into an empirical unity; a closer investigation would have to set out these strata. The section ends by opening what it cannot finish, a stratification of the empirical person by habitual affective and volitional groupings; §61 names the lowest of these strata the person's "nature."

### §60. Person as Subject of Reason-Acts, as “Free Ego”

Against the general and unitary empirical subject, "person" in a specific sense is to be delimited: the subject of acts that are to be judged under the viewpoint of reason, the subject that is "self-responsible," the subject that is free and enslaved, unfree — freedom taken in a special and probably in the proper sense (257). A passive compliance in "I move" is a subjective going-on, and it is called free only insofar as it "belongs to my freedom," that is, insofar as, like every subjective going-on, it can be inhibited and released again centripetally from the ego: the subject "approves," says yes to the solicitation of the stimulus as a solicitation to yield, and practically gives its fiat. In relation to my centripetal ego-acts I have the consciousness of "I can." They are activities, and in their whole course there is not a mere running-on of happenings; the course has constantly issued from the ego-center, and as long as that is so the consciousness "I do," "I act" extends. If the ego is "carried away," "fettered" by some affection, the proper "I do" is broken through, the ego is inhibited as active, it is unfree, "moved, not moving." In the case of freedom there is, for the coming phases of the doing that lie in the immediate horizon, in relation to the horizon of unfulfilled practical intentions, the consciousness of the free "I can" and not the mere consciousness "it will come," "it will happen." Freedom is here defined before it is analyzed, by a direction: what issues from the ego-center is free, what carries the ego along is not, and even yielding is free when it passes through the ego's fiat. The horizon of the "I can" is what separates an action from a happening that merely continues.

#### a) “I Can” as Logical Possibility, as Practical Possibility and Impossibility, as Neutrality-Modification of Practical Acts, and as Original Consciousness of Ability (Subjective Force, Capacity, Resistance)

The subsection's title sets out four things the "I can" might be, and a footnote to it credits Lipps's Psychologie with the first fundamental discussion, adding that the most original concept of possession, of "I have," arises here — I have my limbs, I have power over them (258). Husserl begins from the practical sense. What I can, am able to do, know myself capable of, what stands before me in consciousness as such, is a practical possibility. Only between practical possibilities can I "decide"; only a practical possibility can — in another, theoretical sense of "can" — be a theme of my will. I can will nothing that I do not have consciously before my eyes, that does not lie in my power, in my ability; and in "I can will nothing" the "can" itself may be meant practically, since the will can be an object of will only insofar as it lies in my "power," in my sphere of power, only insofar as the performance of the thesis is itself something practically possible for me. Before the will with its active thesis of the fiat lies doing as drive-doing, for instance the involuntary "I move," the involuntary reaching for my cigar — I desire it and do it "without further ado," which admittedly is not easy to separate from the case of choice in the narrower sense. The practical "can" is thus the condition of willing, not its result, and the willing of a will is itself subject to it.

What kind of modification, then, is the "I can," "I am able," "I am capable" (258)?

In experience the "I can" and the "I cannot" separate by their phenomenological character (259). There is a doing without resistance, or a consciousness of resistance-less ability, and a doing in the overcoming of a resistance, a doing with a "counter" and a belonging consciousness of an ability that overcomes resistance. There is, always phenomenologically, a graduality of the resistance and of the force of overcoming, of the "active" force against the "inert" force of resistance. The resistance can become insurmountable; then we strike on the "it does not go," "I cannot," "I do not have the strength." Connected with this is the transferred apprehension of acting and counteracting outside the sphere of my doing and ability: things are "active" in relation to others, have "forces and counter-forces" in relation to others, offer one another resistances, and possibly the resistance one thing exercises is insurmountable, the other "cannot overcome it." The vocabulary of force in physics is here traced to a transfer from the lived polarity of doing and resistance.

The proper apprehension of resistance presupposes that what is at issue is not something merely thingly but something of the kind that falls into the sphere of my "will," into the sphere of what I have possibly already come to know as something I am able to do (259). All my ability in the physical sphere is mediated by my "bodily activity," by my bodily ability, my bodily capacity. From experience I know that my limbs move in the proper manner that distinguishes them from all other things and thing-movements, from physical mechanical movements: in the character of subjective moving, of "I move." And this is from the outset apprehensible as practically possible; indeed, Husserl formulates it generally: only what has this subjective character is a priori subject to such apprehension. Originally the "I will" occurs only here; originally a presented will can be affirmed here and only here and becomes an actual will. Here too I can meet resistance: my hand has "fallen asleep," now I cannot move it, it is temporarily paralyzed. I experience the same in the domain of the outer "consequences" of bodily movement: the hand pushes aside what stands in its way, "it goes"; sometimes it goes "with difficulty," "less so," "without resistance," and sometimes not at all, the resistance insurmountable despite all straining. The body is the original locus of the "I can," and every other practical possibility is practical only through it — a thesis §62 will make the pivot of its account of the will-body.

How then does my will take hold when I carry out a bodily activity, what does it do immediately (259)? Must I have physiological knowledge for it? Objectively and physically a material state is of course the first, though I know nothing of it or need know nothing. "But how I can set it in train," one is accustomed to say, "is a riddle." "Psychic causality is a fact, but unintelligible" — so one thinks, or one declares it mere semblance; admittedly one also says that a physio-psychic causing is a riddle. The paragraph states the received puzzle in order to dissolve it in the next.

But does this "riddle" not belong to the essence of all causation (260)? That means: it is no riddle. "Causality" belongs to the essence of thing-constitution, and its specificity lies precisely in the experiential apperception. So one must experience the thing as thing and determine the causal apperception phenomenologically more closely in examples of actual experience, and to demand something else here has no sense. The reply is a general one about causation: the intelligibility of any "because" is the intelligibility of an apperception, never of a mechanism behind it, and the will's causality is in no worse a position than the stone's.

The same holds in the domain of will-causality and of body-apperception, the apprehension of a thing with "limbs" that are not only moved but that I move and that accordingly can possibly be moved in the "I will" (260). What is the first for the will? The presupposition is the hand-apperception with its phenomenal position and so on; no presupposition is physiological investigation and knowledge. Physical and physiological understanding is something quite other than practical understanding. The one time it is a matter of knowledge, more closely of scientific knowledge, of the thing as a natural object in physical, substantial-causal nature; the other time of a practical understanding, an understanding of the practical happening, not of the process according to its physical causality — the question is after the practical ground, the "psychic cause," of the process, after its motive. The thing moves because "I" gave it a push; I stretched out my hand and pushed. But if I move my hand involuntarily? Why does it move? Because the position of the hand is uncomfortable. Or "I do not quite know why," I did not attend to it; but the ground lies in the psychic and its obscure stimuli and motivations. The practical "because" is a motivational "because," and it reaches down into obscure motivation without ceasing to be one.

Admittedly my hand is also a thing, and when I perform a subjective "I move" and am not dreaming or deceived, a physical process takes place in nature as well (261). Certainly the perception of the "I move" includes the perception of the physical movement in space, so the question of physical causality can be raised here too. But it need not be raised, and it is not to be raised in the personal attitude, in which alone the active and suffering person is posited as motivation-subject and subject of its surrounding world. A footnote attached here forestalls a shortcut: one might recur to the fact that physical nature and its causality dissolve into motivations of consciousness, but these form a closed group whose indices are the posited and theoretically determined things and natural laws, and one may not say that in the surplus over the physical we have the psychic with its psychic "causation" — three different attitudes are in play, in which nature is posited simply as theoretical theme, or as correlate of the motivations constituting it, or, as appearing nature, as the field of praxis. Husserl then turns to the lived "I can." Originally the "I move," the "I do," precedes the "I can do"; but there is also a lived "I can" detached from actual doing. I can "present" that I move my hand, which is now at rest, voluntarily or involuntarily. Can I also present that I move this table, not "through" my hand-movement, by means of it? I can of course present that it moves "mechanically," but its movement can never be my moving it except "through" bodily movement, through a push. I have power over my body, and in the physical world only through having power over my body. If I present the movement of my hand in the form "I move my hand," I present an "I do" and not a merely mechanical movement; but such a presentation is not yet an "I can." In the "I can" there evidently lies not merely a presentation but, beyond it, a thesis, which concerns not only myself but the "doing" — not the actual doing but the being-able-to-do. The last sentence is the subsection's hinge: "I can" is positional, not a mere presentation, and what it posits is an ability.

It is important to work out by examples the contrast that emerges here between possibility in the sense of merely "logical" possibility, mere possibility from intuitive presentation, and the practical possibility of ability (261).

If I fictionalize a mechanical movement or any other natural process, or a thing, whatever it may be, I can at any time so transform my consciousness of this free fiction that a possibility-thesis relating to the fictum springs from it (261–262). The presentable, or first the presented, is possible; the "object" as such, as intuitively hovering, is the substrate of the possibility-predicate — that is, the meant object is a possible one insofar as it can be intuited, and here a "can" comes in again. A centaur is a possible object; "it" is intuited, the identical of this and other quasi-perceptions I can freely perform. One must presumably say: every intuition admits a turn into an act that posits the intuited "object," as meant what, as possible, that "experiences" it as possible in originary givenness. And a possibility-positing without intuition is an intention that by its sense finds fulfilment in an intuition, or in a turn of intuition, that delivers the possibility-thesis in "proper" form. Logical possibility is here given its own phenomenology: it is the positional turn of a neutralized intuition, and it has proper and improper modes like any positing.

This possibility is the doxic, logical possibility, not the formal-logical one (262). Under this "it is possible" falls of course also "it is possible that I move the hand," when I present the hand-movement and take the sense of the possibility-thesis from this presentation, a neutrality-modification. But with that I do not have the practical "I can," although the talk of ability is applicable to that general class of cases too: a centaur can exist; the movement of a body is possible, it can move; "I move the hand" is possible, it can be that I move the hand. So everywhere: it is possible that A is, equals: that A is can be; the possibly-existent is what-can-be. But what is at issue here is not the doxic, logical possibility of being that I move the hand, that I do something. Certainly, that I move the table "immediately," doing it, cannot be; that I move the hand "immediately" can be — the one "I can" can be made intuitive, the other cannot. The distinction is pressed to its limit here: even the practical case can be stated as a logical possibility, and the difference is not in what is said but in the kind of consciousness that gives it.

But is that all, and does not the talk of being able to intuit already point into another domain (262)? A movement of my hand is not only a possibility of being.

Different kinds of neutrality-modification come into consideration (263). The neutrality-modification of a doxic consciousness, a consciousness of an objective being, is a "mere presentation"; if it is a perception or memory, an originary consciousness of present being or of remembered being, neutralization yields a neutrally modified intuition. And from every neutralized intuition a theoretical, doxic possibility can be originarily drawn, a being-possible that gives itself as a modification of being-certain, of being simply — of that which is to be drawn from a non-neutralized intuition and most originally from a "perception," from present being. In a wider sense every neutrality-modification of the doxic sphere admits a turn into a doxic consciousness of possibility, though the possibility may then no longer have the mode of "evidence," of self-givenness. The doctrine of neutrality-modification from Ideas I is here reapplied as the source of possibility-consciousness.

Likewise from every "practical" neutrality-modification a practical possibility can be drawn, and possibly drawn originarily (263). So there stand correlatively over against one another: presenting, that is, intuiting and neutralized intuiting, and being, that is, presentable being, possible being; likewise doing and quasi-doing — action and possible action, and again deed, the action-goal as the result of action, and "possible" deed, possible practical result, practical possibility. On the side of the subject there corresponds to the "I do" the "I can do," just as in the parallel case to the "I believe," "I hold for true, for existent," the "I hold for possible." On both sides I transpose myself into the neutrality-modification and draw from it possibility of being and possibility of deed.
