A footnote generalizes: these parallels must run through all the basic classes — joy and quasi-joy, a possible joy, "it could be gladdening"; pleasure and a quasi-pleasure I think myself into, from which a possible pleasingness, a possible value, can be drawn; and the actual performance of a quasi-pleasing is the analogue of an actually intuitive phantasizing, so that empty presenting does not include the being-able-really-to-present, and practical ability, for instance as being-able-to-intuit, plays its role everywhere. Practical possibility is thus given the same structure as doxic possibility: a neutralized act with a positional turn. The next paragraphs show why this is still not enough.

The intuitive presentation, the quasi-perception, that I will something, do something, decide thus and so in a given situation, choose this or that possibility, of course presupposes not only the intuitive presentation of the respective outer processes but the intuitive presentation of the value-characters and practical characters in question, and again the originarity of these characters, hence, with actual intuition, the neutrality-modification of the corresponding acts of feeling and will: I must, in the modification, value, wish, will thus and so (263). To present myself acting is to quasi-value and quasi-will, not merely to picture movements; the feeling-sphere's own originarity, established in §50, is presupposed by every intuitive "I could."

One sees, then, that much depends on the distinction treated in the Logical Investigations as that of the propriety and impropriety of doxic position-takings (264). I cannot intuitively present that two times two is five — that is, I cannot intuitively present that I judge, properly, intuitively, in evidence, that two times two is five. But I can present that I judge it to be so, namely improperly, "unclearly," "confusedly" performing the theme. Particularly with propositional matters that are still foreign to us and cannot be grasped as false in immediate or easily reachable insight, whose falsity shows itself only in long proofs, it becomes clear that such matters in the mode of improper givenness are compatible with any thesis. The LU distinction is recruited to explain a fact about self-presentation: confusion makes anything presentable, and only proper performance blocks.

The analogue holds for the whole sphere of reason-acts, the active acts properly so called, and their synthetic formations in all spheres, including feeling and will (264). I can "think" that I would value something, desire it, will it as end or as means, which on exact deliberation I could not value; that I could and would strive after something as a suitable means which on deliberation I would not and could not. The "I can" is there the one time and not the other. "I could" here says that I phantasize myself into it, perform the neutrality-modification of the doxic and the valuing acts, and find the pleasure-thesis, desire-thesis, will-thesis in question compatible with its foundation. Compatibility in the modified act is the criterion of "I could," and it is a weak one, since improperly performed acts are compatible with almost anything.

Two kinds of motivation therefore result (265). In the case of impropriety I have compatibility of the sense in the mode of "unclarity," of the improperly performed sense, which may be intuitive in its members, with any position-taking, thesis, and thetic modification whatever. On the other hand, the concrete act does occur, somehow "motivated," in the nexus of consciousness — and that holds in the neutrality-modification too. That is, when I step as it were out of my actual life and pass into a "phantasy" life, which means that a strand of "phantasy" attaches itself to my actual life in which I perform a quasi-life, this phantasy-life is a unity of life, and in it there is unity through motivation. The problem is to clarify the peculiarity of this motivation, for it is not arbitrary. Even fiction is motivated: the quasi-life I phantasize hangs together as my quasi-life, and that is the fact the next paragraphs exploit.

But we see that this consideration does not yet suffice: I can present that I commit a murder, a theft, and yet I cannot present that I would do it (265). I could present that I judged the angle-sum of a triangle to be three right angles, and yet I could not so judge. What kind of antinomy is this?

"I could do it" — that is the neutrality-modification of the doing and the practical possibility drawn from it (265). "I could not do it after all" — I lack the original consciousness of ability or power for this action, which even in a fictive action is an originary, non-neutralized consciousness; this action conflicts with the kind of my person, with my way of letting myself be motivated. A footnote refers to Beilage XI for this and the whole of subsection b. The antinomy is dissolved by the fourth sense of the title: beside logical possibility, practical possibility as neutralized doing, there is an original, non-neutralized consciousness of ability, and it belongs to a person, not to a presentation. The murder I can picture myself committing is one I cannot do, because the picturing is neutral and the ability is not — and the ability is the shape of a character. That is what subsection b analyzes.

#### b) The “I Can” Motivated in Knowledge of One’s Own Person. Self-Apperception and Self-Understanding

I know myself from experience, I know what kind of character I am: I have an ego-apperception, an empirical "self-consciousness" (266). Every developed subject is not merely a stream of consciousness with a pure ego; a centering in the form "I" has also been performed, the cogitations are acts of an ego-subject, and the ego is a unity constituted from its own position-takings, actively, and from its own habits and capacities, and thereafter outwardly apperceived, whose core is the pure ego. Hence the evidence "I am." I can deceive myself in my character, but I must posit myself with some character, and I posit myself as ego with a determinate character, apart from horizons of indeterminacy. If I now phantasize, live myself, as the one I am, into a phantasy-actuality or a neutrally given world, into the familiar world somehow transformed in phantasy, then I judge how these and those motives would work on me — more exactly, the quasi-motives of this phantasy-surroundings — how I, as the one I am, would act and could act, could judge, value, will, and could not. So I judge, or can judge, empirically, on the basis of my experiential knowledge of myself, with regard to myself, the ego constituted for me in empirical apperception as experience-ego. By analogy with earlier modes of behavior, earlier position-takings in relation to their foundations and motives, I expect later modes of behavior. These are not only inferences of expectation: intentional characters grow, just as thing-apperception grows out of experience-systems of "possible" expectations that nonetheless form a unity apperceptively. In any case every trait of the intentional object points back to earlier similar experiences; there is nothing in principle new in the apprehension of the thing, and were there, it would already be the beginning of the constitution of a new unity-stratum. The "I am" is given here a specific content: not the bare pole but a self with a character, and self-knowledge is modeled on thing-knowledge as inductive apperception — the model the next paragraph breaks.

But now: can I not think myself into motivation-situations in which I have never been, which I had never experienced, the same or similar (266)? And can I not see, find out in quasi-seeing, how I would behave, although I could behave otherwise — that is, although it would be thinkable, clearly presentable, that I decided otherwise, while as this personal ego I could not? That is the decisive point. And further: I may already have been repeatedly in similar motivation-situations, but I am not a thing that reacts the same under the same circumstances, whereas it is obvious to me that things in principle can act as the same under the same causal circumstances. Earlier I was motivated thus, now otherwise, and precisely because in the meantime I have become another. The motivation, the effective motives, may be the same, but the force of the different motives is another. For every human being, for instance, the power of sensibility in youth is quite other than in age; the sensuous foundation, especially that of the sensuous drives, is another. Age becomes deliberate, becomes egoistic; youth is rash, readily disposed to devotion in a noble surge; age is accustomed, through manifold experiences, to hold itself back and weigh consequences; the life-tempo of youth is from the start a faster one, its phantasy more mobile, but its experience less — it has not learned bad consequences, does not know the dangers, still has the fresh original joy in the new, in impressions and experiences not yet had, in adventures. The disanalogy with the thing is now explicit and is the core of the personalistic account of individuality: a person's regularity is a regularity of a subject that changes by living, so that the same motives have different force at different stages, and self-knowledge cannot be induction alone.

So the foundations, directions, and forces of motivation differ; how do I learn to know them (267)? As the one I am, by phantasizing presentification of possible situations, in which I "consider" what sensuous or spiritual stimuli would work on me, what forces they would have, how I would decide, in which direction the greater pull would go, which force would tip the balance — given the same situation. It may be that in the given case other motives would arise and work, that I feel obscure motives without bringing them to clarity as I now do in phantasy-deliberation; it may be that in actual acting I am "indisposed," have slept badly and am therefore apathetic and weak, while now I phantasize myself into a freshness that corresponds to an actual freshness as my present habitus, and conversely. But those are possibilities of equal right. I as spiritual ego can also grow stronger in the course of my development; the weak will can strengthen itself. I can then say, considering: I, as I was earlier, would not have withstood this temptation, could not have done that; now I can and would act so. But I say the latter not from experience but because in the attempt I can test my motives, and do test them. I can also strengthen the force of freedom by making fully clear to myself: if I yielded, I would have to despise myself, the subject of the yielding, and that would so strengthen the moment of disvalue that I tend to prefer that I could not do it, could not yield; my power of resistance grows with that. The passage gives the method of self-understanding proper: not observation but trial in phantasy — testing one's motives in the attempt — and it gives freedom a technique, the intensification of a motive by making its disvalue clear. Self-knowledge here changes the self it knows.

Judgment from experience and judgment on the basis of understanding the person as a motivation-subject, the subject of actual and possible motivations, and of understanding the motivation-possibilities proper to him, often combine in the form that experience instructs me which motivating "grounds" are effective given his prevailing direction of thought, his forgetfulness known to me, his habit of presenting non-intuitively, and the like (268). "He would never have acted so if he had made the true state of affairs clear to himself. He would be charitable — at bottom he has a good heart — if he made clear to himself the need of the one asking him for help." For that he is too hasty, too busy, as I know from experience. What works as a motive harbors manifold intentional implications, and here lies a source of important new motivations: to pursue the proper sense and the verification of the finding-out of "the truth itself" and to let oneself be determined by it in genuine reason. There lie the preeminent values, on which the value of all motivations and of actual deeds finally depends. There lie also sources for fundamental formal laws, which like all noetic norms are laws of motivation-validity, and to these belong again laws of motivation-force and of personal values. The highest value is represented by the person who habitually lends the highest motivational force to genuine, true, valid, free resolving. The subsection closes by turning description into axiology: understanding a person means understanding what he would do if he saw clearly, and the person of highest value is the one for whom seeing clearly has the greatest force. Reason is defined as a habit of motivation, not as a faculty.

#### c) Foreign Influences and Freedom of the Person

The development of a personality is determined by the influence of others, by the influence of foreign thoughts, of foreign, suggested feelings, of foreign commands (269). The influence determines personal development whether or not the person later knows of it, remembers it, is able to determine its degree and kind. Foreign thoughts penetrate my soul; under changing circumstances, according to my psychic situation, the stage of my development, the formation of my dispositions, they can exercise a varying effect, an enormous one or a slight one, and the same thought works differently on different persons under "the same" circumstances. Over against one another stand thoughts of my own, which "grew originarily" in my spirit or were won by me from premises — which may themselves rest on foreign influence — and adopted thoughts; likewise feelings of my own, originally sprung in me, and foreign feelings, appropriated, felt after another, inauthentic. What is foreign, "taken over" by me, more or less external, can be characterized as issuing from the foreign subject, first as a tendency issuing from him and turning to me, as a demand (Zumutung) to which I perhaps yield passively, perhaps reluctantly and yet overcome. But perhaps I appropriate it actively, and it becomes my property. It then no longer has the mere character of a demand I yield to, which determines me from outside; it has become a position-taking issuing from my ego, not a mere stimulus that tends toward this, and yet it keeps the character of the taking-over of what came from another ego, of something that has its primal institution (Urstiftung) in him. It is a case similar to one in my own egoistic sphere: primal institution and later reproduction as an actualized habitus of my own. Beside the tendencies issuing from other persons stand the demands that appear in the intentional form of indeterminate generality — those of custom, of usage, of tradition, of the spiritual milieu: "one" judges so, "one" holds the fork so, the demands of the social group, of the estate. To these too one can passively give way or actively take a position, decide for them freely. The taxonomy of influence is a taxonomy of ownership: an influence becomes mine when it becomes a position-taking of mine, and it does not thereby cease to have been instituted elsewhere. The "one" (man) of custom is named here as a distinct source of motivation, indeterminate in its subject.

The autonomy of reason, the "freedom" of the personal subject, thus consists in my not passively yielding to foreign influences but deciding out of myself; and further in my not letting myself be "drawn" by other inclinations, drives, but being freely active, and that in the manner of reason (269). Freedom is defined twice over, against the foreign and against one's own drives, and both times positively as deciding from oneself in the manner of reason.

We have therefore to distinguish between the human person, the apperceptive unity we grasp in self-perception and in the perception of others, and the person as the subject of reason-acts, whose motivations and motivation-forces come to givenness in original experience of our own and in the re-understanding experience of others (269). The regard here goes to the specifically spiritual, the free act-life. The distinction announced at the head of §60 is now grounded: the empirical person is an apperceived unity; the person in the specific sense is the subject of rational motivation, given not by apperception but in the living and re-living of motivations.

#### d) General-Typical and Individual-Typical in the Understanding of Persons

At issue is first a general-typical for the ego in affection and action, and then further a special-typical and individual-typical: the type of this human-ego, more distinctly the type in the behavior of the ego belonging to this body in the actions and affections of its life that is to be understood after the fact (270).

I can understand in detail how this ego is motivated: he reaches for the cup now because he wants to drink, and that because he is thirsty (270). This has nothing to do with his person in particular; it is something general-human. But that he suddenly sets the cup down before drinking because he sees hunger and thirst in a poor child standing nearby, and hands the cup to the child — that bespeaks his "good heart" and belongs to his personality. By its essence personality builds itself up from special characters within the general type or character "human subject," and indeed from those which as the lowest specialties make up the individual type of this human subject. Every human being has his character, we can say his life-style in affection and action, with respect to the manner of being motivated by these and those circumstances; and he not only had it hitherto: the style is something that persists at least relatively within periods of life and then in general changes again characteristically, but so that as a result of the changes a unified style shows itself again. Character is defined as style of motivation, individuated downward from the general type, and the definition allows for change without loss of identity because what persists is a style, not a set of reactions.

Accordingly one can to some extent expect how a human being will behave in a given case if one has apperceived him correctly in his personality, in his style (270–271). The expectation is in general not univocal; it has its apperceptive horizons of indeterminate determinability within a bounding intentional frame; it concerns just one of the modes of behavior that corresponds to the style. An "amiable" person, for instance, will in such and such a case reel off empty amiabilities, and his turns of phrase have a stylistic stamp; that does not mean we can guess the exact wording with the quite determinate turn of thought. If we can, we say the person is stereotyped; if we know him, we soon know the arsenal of his amiable — or, if he is a wit, witty — turns, among which we have the choice, unless we have associative cues to prefer particular ones. Prediction of persons is horizonal: a style delimits a frame within which the particular remains open, and the case where it does not is the case of a person who has become a thing.

Taken all together: the person has in the widest sense a typical character, character-properties (271). Everything a person experiences widens the frame of her pre-givennesses, can enter into memory again darkly or clearly, affect the ego, motivate actions; but even without that it determines the future stock of experience according to laws of the new formation of apperceptions and associations. The person forms herself through "experience."

The concept of experience here is certainly another than the one in which, in validity-contexts of the grounding of knowledge through experience, experience is a title for theoretically grounding acts, acts that give theoretical acts a basis of right — ego-acts of perceptual grasp of existing objects or of recollecting grasp (271). Of course every such experience also has after-effects, every active perception, memory; but no less every inactive one, and likewise every judging, valuing, willing. Everything has after-effects, but not in every respect; only within the limits of its type. The two senses of experience are kept apart — evidential ground and formative sediment — and the second is the one that makes a person.

To personal life there belongs a typicality that is a different one for each (271–272); a footnote adds that association and apperception are the principles of the typification of all psychic acts. Within certain stretches this typicality remains the same, even when the person's "experiences" — the domain of ever newly forming experience-apperceptions — grow and with them the domain of pre-givennesses changes. Everything has after-effects, but not in every respect. On the street people meet me, cars drive by; that has its apperceptive type, within which the street's bustle keeps, while what individually happens could always run otherwise than it does. All the detail I hardly attend to, but which co-delineates my horizon of experience, changes nothing in my moral character, in my aesthetic character; no motivations run from there into these spheres. And if on the other hand motivations arise that work in the sense of a transformation of "character," still a typicality reigns throughout the succession of life's stages: the typicality of the ages; and in accordance with this typicality I can say, once I have seen it, that when this person stands under these circumstances she will behave according to the type, and when the circumstances are changed, according to the type. Two claims are made about what sediments: not everything that happens to me touches my character, since motivation into the moral and aesthetic spheres is selective; and even transformation of character has a type, that of the ages.

That is an experiential apperception, but it presupposes understanding as well (272). The ego, the subject of affections and actions, is thereby related to its pre-givennesses and is not taken merely as the subject of the individual affections and actions. Here the general apperception of the human being, or of the person, essentially co-operates: the general typicality of bodiliness is the presupposition of empathy, and what is empathized is an ego-analogue. That is already something typical — the general structure "ego, pre-givenness, affection, and so on," the ego of a life together with its life. Understanding is thus not added to typification but presupposed by it: even the most general type, "an ego with a life," is an understood structure, not an observed regularity.

To the general-typical it also belongs that human beings in general are experientially determined in their behavior by earlier behavior (272). Disappointments one has experienced with people make one distrustful; repeated disappointments of fine hopes embitter.

As kinds of things change in experience generally in familiar ways and are judged accordingly in future, so with human beings (272). We learn objects by their kind, and in the given case the behavior of an object is intelligible to us when it follows the general rule of its kind. We move in the domain of intuitive experience when we seek understanding. We construct the development of a human being when we reconstruct his course of life and make it intuitive in such a way that the whole becoming of the person, especially with respect to his manner of letting himself be motivated as a subject, together with the determinate actions and passions that belong to him, becomes experientially comprehensible — experientially, that is, it runs as it generally goes in human life, the subject's acts and their motivations occurring in an empirically intelligible way. That is "knowledge of human nature," "knowledge of souls." The lowest level of understanding persons is a typology, and Husserl names it with the ordinary words for worldly wisdom to mark that it is not yet the understanding of an individual.

I enter into relation with various ego-subjects and learn to know typical moments in pre-givennesses, actions, and so on, and I apprehend them according to these types — not as if I first had the types in abstracto, any more than I first have the type "tree" in abstracto when I apprehend a tree as a tree, but in manifold experience the type stamps itself out, or stamps itself into us, and determines an apperceptive form and then a stratum abstractable from the actual apprehension (273). The human being behaves ever again differently, has ever again another actual surrounding world, another field of living and hidden pre-givennesses; but he is the same not only as a bodily but also as a spiritual type, he has his empirical peculiarities and is, as a spiritual type, an intelligible unity. Types are apperceptive, not conceptual; that is what lets a person be recognized as the same across wholly different surroundings.

I understand another's thinking and acting according to the usual of my own modes of behavior and motivations, but not all judgment of the other comes about only by the so to speak outer style of his life abstracted from experience, where I would not need to penetrate into the interior of the motivations or present them fully alive (273). I learn to see into the other's interior, I learn to know the person herself inwardly: the motivation-subject that springs forth as soon as I present the other ego as so motivated. The turn from the outer style to the inner motivation-subject is the turn from typology to understanding proper, and the next four paragraphs give its phenomenology.

But how is it when from some glances, postures, utterances the character-kind of a person suddenly lights up for us, when we "look as if into an abyss," when the "soul" of the person suddenly "opens itself" to us, we "see into wondrous depths," and so on (273)? What kind of "understanding" is that? Husserl announces that the following is to be answered.

First, it is saying too much that empirical understanding is the same as gaining full intuition of experiential nexuses (273–274). Nexuses of outer nature too light up all of a sudden before one actually lays out clearly and distinctly in intuition how the relations lie; that comes only afterwards. Likewise historical nexuses that flash up, or logical nexuses — all before the explication, the actual subsequent establishing of the nexuses. We speak here of "intuition," a word that very often means precisely the opposite of intuition in the phenomenological sense: a presentiment, a foreseeing without seeing, an obscure, that is, symbolic, often ungraspably empty pre-grasping; the actual nexus is a goal grasped in advance, an empty intention, but one so determinate that we can follow the determinately directed tendency and in fulfilment gain a chain of actual intuitions — simple experiential intuitions or logical evidences. To see a person does not yet mean to know him. To see a person is, as we found, something other than to see a material thing: every thing is of a kind, and once one knows the kind one is done; the human being, however, has an individual kind, each a different one. In general he is a human being, but his characterological kind, his personality, is a unity constituted in his course of life as subject of position-takings, a unity of manifold motivations on manifold presuppositions, and insofar as one knows analogous lines from experience of various people, one can "intuitively" grasp the special and peculiar complication in question and the unity constituting itself here, and have in it a guiding thread for fulfilling the intentions by laying out the actual nexuses in intuition. So this "intuition," in Husserl's view, must not be confused with actual intuition; it is a matter of the success of a more closely determining apperception, which like every apperception offers a guiding thread for confirming, in the progress of experience, intentional nexuses that are often extremely complex. The famous "insight into character" is demoted and preserved at once: it is an empty but determinate anticipation, not a seeing, and its value is that it can be fulfilled.

But the subject is not a mere unity of experience, although experience and the general type play an essential role, and it is important that this be stressed and made clear (274). I transpose myself into the other subject: through empathy I grasp what motivates him and how strongly, with what force. And inwardly I learn to understand how, since these motives determine him so forcefully, he behaves and would behave, what he can and cannot. Many inner correlations I can understand by immersing myself in him in this way. His ego is thereby grasped: it is precisely the identical ego of such motivations, so directed and of such force. The other's identity is given, on this account, as the identity of a force-field of motives — the same result §64 will state for individuation.

I gain these motivations by transposing myself into his situation, his level of education, his youthful development, and so on, and in the transposing I must go along with them; I do not only feel my way into his thinking, feeling, doing, but must follow him in them (275). His motives become my quasi-motives, which in the mode of intuitively fulfilling empathy motivate insightfully. I go along with his temptations, I go along with his fallacies; in the "along with" lies an inner co-experiencing of motivating factors that carry their necessity in themselves. Admittedly, unresolved and insoluble residues remain: the original disposition of character, which however I can also make clear and intelligible to myself analogically. The account of understanding is completed: to understand a person is to be quasi-motivated as he is, and the necessity understood is the necessity of motivation, felt from within; what cannot be so shared, the original disposition, is reached by analogy from one's own.

I am predominantly phlegmatic, but occasionally I am stimulated to cheerfulness and liveliness, it wells up, perhaps after the enjoyment of stimulants; it has physical grounds in a modification of my bodiliness (275). Analogously, that person there is lastingly and predominantly disposed to cheerfulness: he is from the start somewhat as I am after wine, and is so habitually. In this sense I understand other things too. I occasionally have "brilliant ideas," occasionally scientific thinking goes easily for me, I have wide horizons — or at least I imagine so. By this analogy I present to myself, in quantitative and possibly also qualitative intensification, for which I may again have intuitive bases, the genius. The residue that cannot be re-motivated — temperament, talent — is understood by scaling one's own states, and the section ends on the modesty of the method: the genius is intelligible to the phlegmatic only as an intensification of his own good days.

### §61. The Spiritual Ego and Its Foundation

The undergrounds on which the intelligibly motivated spiritual life of the other builds itself, and in accordance with which it runs its individually typical course, thus present themselves as "variations" of my own (276). I also hit upon these undergrounds when I want to understand a human being's development: I then have to describe, stage by stage, what the surrounding world was in which he grew up, and how the surrounding things and people, as they appeared to him and as he regarded them, motivate him. In doing so I come upon a factual stock that is unintelligible in itself. This child has an original joy in sounds, that one does not; the one inclines to quick temper, the other to patience. Nature-causal factors enter too: as a result of a bad fall the person becomes a cripple, and that has consequences for his spiritual life, since certain groups of motivation drop away from then on. The real-causal working out of the consequences is of no interest here; but medical knowledge can serve to lay in, in the right way, psychic effects that come into question for the subjective development, and so to bring them to bear on the clarification of the subjective motivations and development. The physical serves there as an indicator of what is to be laid in. The limit of understanding is stated with the explanatory method that respects it: where motivation runs out, a fact is recorded, and medicine is used not to explain the person but to tell the interpreter what to lay into him.

If we take the personal ego, then, in its developmental context, we find two levels, which may on occasion separate — the lower level, say, as "pure" animality — a double "subjectivity": the higher is the specifically spiritual, the stratum of the active intellect (intellectus agens), of the free ego as the ego of free acts, among them all proper acts of reason, the positively but also the negatively rational acts (276). To it belongs also the unfree ego, unfreedom understood in the sense that holds for an actual ego: I let myself be dragged down by sensibility. This specifically spiritual ego, the subject of spiritual acts, the personality, finds itself dependent on a dark underground of character dispositions, original and hidden dispositions, and on the other side dependent on nature.

We come here again upon the old distinction, which presses itself on us so early, between reason and sensibility (276). Sensibility too has its rules, its rules of understanding for concordance and discordance; it is a stratum of hidden reason, at least as far as the constitution of nature reaches, since all the complicated relations of the if–then, all causalities, can become guiding threads for theoretical and so spiritual explications, for clarifications in the form: "in the concordance of experience the doxa of perception confirms itself, in discordance the posited being or being-so is cancelled." The Kantian pairing is taken up with a phenomenological correction: sensibility is not the merely given but a "hidden reason," because the syntheses that constitute nature in it are the very ones reason explicates when it theorizes.

In the sensuous sphere, the sphere of the underground taken in the widest sense, we have associations, perseverations, determining tendencies and the like (277). That "makes" the constitution of nature, but it continues after nature is there for the spirits: through all the life of spirit runs the "blind" efficacy of associations, drives, feelings as stimuli and determining grounds of drives, tendencies surfacing in the dark, which determine the further course of consciousness according to "blind" rules.

To these lawfulnesses correspond habitual modes of behavior of the subject, acquired peculiarities — the habit, say, of drinking one's glass in the evening (277). It is to be asked whether these are properties of his "individuality" or whether that does not rather lie on the side of his proper activities. Admittedly there is a sense in which one speaks of individuality as the total style and habitus of the subject, which as a harmonizing unity runs through all modes of behavior, all activities and passivities, and to which the whole psychic underground constantly contributes. Two senses of individuality are thereby separated: the character of the free ego's position-takings, and the total style to which the underground also contributes; §64 will locate absolute individuation in the first.

The context of experience belonging to the human being is not a mere bundle of experiences, or a mere stream of consciousness in which experiences float along; rather all experiencing is the experiencing of an ego, which does not itself flow as its experiences do (277). And there is constantly an underground which it has pre-given, a manifold being-related to it, or being-driven; a performing of specific acts and, in one with it, a constant enrichment of the stream of experience that is accomplished from the ego itself: its egoic governing becomes eo ipso experience. But this underground of pre-givenness points back to the other underground, which we have called psychic, of firmly regulated habitus. Husserl's note refers to Supplement XII §§1–3. The "bundle" is Hume's, and the answer is not a substance but a non-flowing ego whose acts, once performed, enter the flow as its own contributions — the mechanism of §29's "deposits" seen from the personal side.

But the ego is, in one with this, a truly unitary person in a still higher sense when it has a certain pervasive unitary style in the way it decides, judging and willing, in the way it esteems aesthetically, but also in the way "ideas" turn up for it — he is, say, a person who has good ideas in mathematical thinking — in the way likenesses present themselves to it, the way its involuntary phantasy works, but also in the manner of its apperception in perception and the peculiarity of its "memory-activity," a man with a good memory (278). He distinguishes easily or with difficulty, reacts faster or slower than another in involuntary associating. The human being has in this a manifoldly determined general type, and each particular human being a particular individual type. It is a matter, on the one hand, of the coming and going of experiences as such, on the other of the ego's being a "position-taking" subject: a subject of will, an acting subject, a thinking subject too. Should we add: a representing, perceiving, remembering, imagining subject? In a certain way, yes. The subject has objects over against it; it is "representing," and that is the basis for its "behavior" toward the objects.

We must distinguish intentionality, first, as that in accordance with which objects are conscious, mere consciousness, representing, and second, as that which makes up the behavior of the acts toward what is represented, the position-takings (278). We distinguish, then, object-consciousness and position-taking, behavior toward the object. Subjectivity in its peculiarity shows itself in the manner of object-consciousness as in the manner of its position-takings. As for the first point, we have to distinguish the case in which the object is there as an object of attending, with its various attentional levels, from the case in which the object is there, conscious "for itself," set off, delimited, apperceived, but unnoticed. The distinction is the one Ideas I drew between the "objectivating" ground and the acts built on it, here applied to the person: individuality shows in how one is conscious of things as well as in how one takes a stand toward them.

The turning of attention is also a "behavior," but not a position-taking; it is rather the presupposition of position-taking (278). In this too lies the "peculiarity" of the subject — what fetters its attention and how: the one leaps easily from object to object, from theme to theme, the other stays long attached to its object.

As for the constitution in consciousness of the object before the attentive turning-toward and the specific position-takings, we are referred back to constitutions in consciousness of earlier objects and earlier attentions and perhaps earlier position-takings, referred back to the sensation-data and the pointings forward and back attached to them (279). Ultimately we come upon the "dark," "hidden" representations and complexes of representation. But insofar as attention plays a role in this constitution of transcendent unities and manifolds, we have there too, implicitly, a behaving ego, and on the other side ultimately a background that lies before all behavior, or rather is presupposed by all behavior.

According to what has been said, the peculiarity of the ego-subject as the habitual peculiarity of its behaving separates itself from the peculiarity in the self-weaving of the backgrounds (279). There is, as it were, a root-soil in dark depths.

The thing-world in which the spirits live is an objective world constituted out of the subjective surrounding worlds, and it is the objectively determinable surrounding world of the spirits (279). As an intersubjectively obtaining rule of possible appearances, and already of possible sensation-complexes of the single spirits, it points back to a lower level of all spiritual existence. Every spirit has a "nature-side." That is precisely the underground of subjectivity: its having sensations consciously, its having reproductions of sensations, its associations, its forming of apperceptions, and indeed of the lowest ones, which constitute unities of experience. To the nature-side belongs immediately the lower life of feeling, the life of drive, and probably also the function of attention, which is a specific ego-function, as is the general function of turning-toward; it forms the bridge to specific ego-being and ego-life. The lower level is the site of the constitution of a world of appearances, or of objects of appearance, the world of the mechanical, of dead lawfulness — all of it mere findings. The nature-side of the spirit is thus not the body but the passive stratum of the spirit's own life, and attention is placed on the boundary, half nature and half ego.

The spirits are the subjects that perform cogitations which stand on this underground and are interwoven into more comprehensive contexts in which motivations in the higher sense — motivations of position-takings by position-takings, proper reason-motivations — hold sway (280). In the experiences of the lower level a sensuous "soul" announces itself, insofar, namely, as dispositions of representation, habitual properties, announce themselves in them which do not concern the attending, grasping, position-taking ego itself with respect to its position-takings. Here belongs the domain of association-psychology. The sensuous, lower soul is one with the subject of position-taking; both form a single empirical unity, from which only the unity of the personal subject, the position-taking ego, sets itself off. This soul is "mine," it "belongs" to my ego-subject and is inseparably one with it. One will have to say that it belongs to the person as a founding underground.

This soul is here not an objective, natural reality but a "spirit-soul": soul in this sense is not defined as a real unity with relation to circumstances of objective nature, hence not psychophysically, or at least need not be so defined (280). There is an immanent lawfulness of the formation of dispositions as a substrate for the position-taking subject. On the other side this regulation hangs together with the psychophysical one, or is apperceivable as natural, so that both are considered in psychology. The "spirit-soul" is the concept that makes §62 possible: a soul defined by an immanent lawfulness of habit-formation, which can be apperceived as nature but does not have to be, and which psychology takes in both ways.

Spirit is not an abstract ego of position-taking acts but the full personality, the ego-human being — I who take positions, who think, value, act, accomplish works (280). To me there then belongs an underground of experiences and an underground of nature, "my nature," which announces itself in the drive-mechanism of the experiences. This nature is the lower psychic, but it reaches into the sphere of position-takings as well: the position-taking ego is dependent on the substrate, since I, in order to experience motivations in my position-takings, must have the motivating experiences, and these stand in associative connection and under rules of associative dispositions. But the position-takings themselves also stand under inductive rules: with every position-taking there grow "tendencies" to equal position-takings under similar circumstances. The section's last sentence returns the argument to §56 b: even the free ego acquires habits of freedom, so that the spirit's dependence on its nature-side goes all the way up without becoming a causal determination.

## Third Chapter: The Ontological Priority of the Spiritual World Over the Naturalistic

### §62. Interpenetration of Personalistic and Naturalistic Attitude

By apperceiving the "underground" of the spirit as its "nature-side," we come to a point where the two attitudes that we separated from one another — the naturalistic and the personalistic, or the natural-scientific and the human-scientific — and correlatively the two kinds of reality, nature and spirit, enter into relation with each other (281). The editors' note refers to Supplements XIII and XIV for the chapter as a whole.

The running-off of the stream of experience stands under the law that has its index in the reality soul, in its dependence on the natural body (281). The reality body-thing expresses an intersubjective regulation, a regulation reaching beyond all consciousness of the association body-soul. The reality soul expresses a second regulation, and a determinate one related to the first: the soul is dependent on the body. Insofar as the spirit is a unity that relates itself to its surrounding world, and the surrounding world consists at the lowest level of appearances that each person constitutes for itself and then, in community, constitutes as communal, components of the regulations of the somatic, the soul and nature enter into the regulation whose index is the spirit. But here, so to speak, another cut is made through absolute consciousness, and a distinctive regulation running within it is designated. The spirit in its spiritual acts is dependent on the soul insofar as the stream of experience lets the acts well up out of itself — the ego, that is, performs them on the ground of the rest of the stream; so the spiritual ego depends on the soul, and the soul on the body: it is, then, conditioned by nature; but it does not on that account stand in a relation of causality to nature. It has an underground that is conditionally dependent; it has, as spirit, a soul, a complex of natural dispositions which as such are conditioned by physical nature and dependent on it. The image of two cuts through one absolute consciousness is the key to the chapter: body, soul and spirit are three indices of regulation in the same stream, and the dependence among them is a relation among regulations, not a causal chain among things.

The spirit in its freedom moves the body, and thereby accomplishes an effect in the spiritual world (282). But the works are, as things, at the same time things in the natural world, just as the body is at the same time an object of the spiritual world — already by being a bearer of sense for comprehension — and moreover a thing in nature. It is not only an appearance for me but is "ensouled" for me; it is, in the manner of consciousness, the organ of my original free movements; from it as given there issue at any time tendencies to movement, to which I can yield or which I resist, and yielding to them I move the hand, the foot, or move the whole body. And all the tendencies issuing from external things, wherever external things become stimuli to perception — the things of appearance, which alone are stimuli in the phenomenological sense — are mediated by the body and by bodily tendencies, tendencies to movements apperceived as movements of the eyes and so as movements of "accommodation" in the widest sense. Wherever external things of my sphere of appearance function as practical stimuli, wherever tendencies grow, directed at me, to move the things, to take them in hand, to alter them, there my body and the tendencies relating to it mediate: grasping, seizing, lifting, pushing, bracing oneself, striking. Mere tendencies to movement, or as accomplishments free movements, there combine with, and hand in hand with them as a new dimension, accomplishments of force, tensions of force.

The body thus plays a comprehensive phenomenological role in the spiritual (283). The purely spiritual lies in all active acts, which are partly actions, partly passions. The egoic, the subjectively spiritual — and the note reminds us that what is had, like appearances, is also subjective, but as field, medium, possession, not as passion or action belonging to egoity as its life — has a special "connection" with its own body; admittedly this connection obtains primarily with respect to special data, movement-sensations and tendencies radiating from bodily sensations toward courses of movement-sensation, which, apperceived bodily, enter into everything bodily. With this hangs together, then, the "expression" that permits foreign bodies to be interpreted, to a wide extent, as bodies for a spiritual life. The body is not only, for me as my body, something specially subjective insofar as it mediates my perceptions and my effects issuing into the thing-world; apprehended by the other it gains a significance, a spiritual significance, insofar as it expresses something spiritual and not only indicates sensibility.

Always to be noted: every ego is in itself ego, a point of identity on which "stimuli" act and from which acts issue, which is active or suffers, turns toward or away, follows inclinations or resists them — the ego of the intentionalities, which even when these are unperformed has a direction issuing from the ego, into which the ego then possibly enters actively in the manner of the performing ego (283). And it is further to be noted that the subjective "domains" to which it relates belong essentially to the ego.

The spirit as connected with its body "belongs" to nature (283). But despite this assignment, this being bound to it, it is not itself nature. The spirit "acts into" nature, and yet it exercises upon nature no causality in the sense of nature. Causality is the relation of a reality to its correlative circumstance-realities. The reality of the spirit, however, is related not to real circumstances lying within nature but to real circumstances that consist in the "surrounding world" and in other spirits; and that is not nature. It is likewise, on the other side, with physical things: they have their real circumstances in one another and further in bodies and souls, but not in spirits. The definition of causality as relation of a reality to its circumstance-realities, from §§31–33, does the work: since spirit's circumstances are its surrounding world and other spirits, and nature's circumstances never include spirits, neither can be a cause of the other by definition.

We must therefore posit a peculiar relation between spirit and physical nature, a relation between two kinds of reality, a relation of conditionedness and yet not of causality in the genuine sense (284). It is likewise with the relation between spirit and soul, or between spirit and body as an aesthesiological unity and not as a physical thing. The body in this aesthesiological sense belongs to the presupposed surrounding world of every personal subject and is the field of its will. That is a spiritual and a causal relation: I perform my "fiat," and the hand moves "because" I will. The body, as a body freely movable by me, is a spiritual reality; to the idea of its reality belongs the relation to the ego as subject of free movement. And likewise conversely: the ego is an individuality, as such performing acts alongside other acts, accomplishing bodily and then further accomplishments. But in the same way my representing, my imagining, remembering and so forth belong to my spiritual field, and with them the forming of new apprehensions. The soul is presupposed, like the body, but it is at the same time surrounding world, determining for the spirit. Here "causal" is used in the spiritual sense: the "because" of the fiat is a motivational because, and it is called causal only within the homogeneous spiritual sphere in which the will-body is a reality of the surrounding world.

We must probably say here that the body is a two-sided reality as living body, that is, when we leave aside that it is a thing and so determinable as physical nature (284). There is constituted, first, the aesthesiological body: as sensing it is dependent on the material body, though here one must again distinguish the material body as appearance and member of the personal surrounding world from the physical body. There is constituted, second, the will-body, the freely movable one: it is something identical also with respect to the various possible movements that the spirit, freely active, performs with it, and that yields a reality-stratum of its own.

The body as living body has accordingly a double face, first of all within intuition (284). It is a reality with respect to nature as the intuitive world of things and at the same time a reality with respect to the spirit; it is thus a double reality, to which two directions of real circumstances belong. Here the aesthesiological stratum is the substrate for the stratum "freely movable." The movable is already presupposed as aesthesiological, but the lower stratum is one-sidedly detachable. An immovable body is thinkable as a limit case, as merely sensing, though it is to be asked whether the immovable does not then mean the zero of moving, as a paralyzed body — and that is surely so.

Likewise the soul is a reality of double face: as bodily conditioned it is physically conditioned, dependent on the physical body, and as an identical reality has its real circumstances in the physis; as spiritually conditioned it stands in a connection of reality with the spirit (284–285). So we have two poles, physical nature and spirit, and between them body and soul. And it emerges that body and soul are "nature in the second sense" properly only on the side turned toward physical nature. In appearance they belong to the spiritual surrounding world; but appearance means precisely appearance of a physical nature, and that establishes a relation to the physical world. This holds at first only for the physical substrate, but as soon as it becomes physical, body and soul too receive a physically determined transcendence. The whole topology of the book is drawn in one figure: two poles with two intermediaries, each of which faces both ways, and the naturalistic apprehension of the intermediaries is the apprehension of their one face.

On the other side the appearing body — and in it the sensation-determined stratum, that of the sense-qualities — and the soul belong to the spiritual surrounding world and receive in it such a relation to the body and to other things that they take on the character of spiritual realities: the person acts on the body by moving it, and the body acts on other things of the surrounding world, the person acting through the body on these things as things of the surrounding world (285). The free moving of my body, and mediately of other things, is an acting on nature insofar as the body-thing of the surrounding world is at the same time determinable as a natural-scientific thing. The effect of the spirit on the body and of the body on other things accomplishes itself as spiritual in the spiritual surrounding world; but by virtue of the correspondence that holds sway here, changes also take place in nature in the physical sense.

We speak of two "faces," two reality-sides of body and soul; but it must be noted how this is rightly to be understood (285–286). The body as a thing in the surrounding world is the experienced, intuitive body-thing, and that is an appearance of the physical body. The latter, and the whole of physical nature, is no concern of the surrounding world, at least of the primary surrounding world; for we can also say that it is secondary surrounding world. As the valued thing becomes a value-object and now in turn, as an objective value, is an object of the surrounding world, so the physical nature theoretically determined on the ground of the "appearances" is a secondary object of the surrounding world, whose primary object is precisely the appearance. The body as thing is the substrate for the aesthesiological body, and thereby we have the substrate for the will-body, the freely movable one, and so the body stands in causality to the spirit: there we are in a homogeneous sphere. By contrast, because the body of the surrounding world is at the same time an appearance of nature in the physical sense, the body has a second face: it is the point of conversion (Umschlagstelle) of spiritual causality into nature-causality. The physical body is thereby given the same status as an objective value: a secondary object of the surrounding world, founded on a primary one, and the living body is the single place where the two orders of causality touch without either becoming the other.

It is likewise with the soul, which has its share in nature insofar as it is related to the body (286). Physical, material thing, body, soul constitute themselves intuitively, and so on the one side as non-spiritual, but on the other as something that is there for the spirit: as "thing" of the lowest level for the constitution of merely physical thinghood, further as "spirit-soul," spiritual substrate for the constitution of the soul. These intuitively constituted unities have a reality-side for the spirit; they are circumstances of the real unity spirit, and it too is a circumstance for them.

The identity of the thing is, admittedly, as a reality of appearance not related to the spirit as a real circumstance; but the identity of the body is, since it receives its own volitional reality-stratum as freely movable; and likewise the soul, as dependent on the body but also taking up psychic occurrences through voluntary movement of the body and otherwise directly influenced by the spirit (286).

If we then pass over to "objective nature," which excludes the spirit as a circumstance because we leave the whole human-scientific attitude, we have nothing of the behavior of the subject; we have merely things, aesthesiological dependencies, psychological dependencies and the corresponding objective realities (286).

If we pass from one attitude into the other, the spirit produces something in its attitude, and by virtue of the parallelism dependent changes in the objective world correspond to it (286–287). But that they are dependent must be established objectively; I can and must convince myself whether an actual movement of nature corresponds "in reality" to the bodily movement. From the standpoint of objective knowledge of nature — where in the human-scientific attitude it is said "the person moves her body" — an act of "fiat" occurs in the body-soul context, and as a state of consciousness, with its corresponding appearances; and all of this is a state in the body-soul reality. But the spirit is here no reality; it does not exist here as spirit at all; only the cogito as a state exists, the ego in it, and so on. The parallelism is affirmed as a correspondence to be verified case by case, never as an identity; and the price of the naturalistic attitude is named without softening: in it the spirit does not exist, only its acts as psychic states.

The difference between this apprehension and the spiritual attitude stands out clearly when we now theoretically posit the ego-side itself, the person and her possessions — where, admittedly, ego-subjects that posit are again there, over against the subjects that have become theoretical objects (288). We posit other subjects, and ourselves in reflection, theoretically by positing them and ourselves exclusively as subjects of experiences, as states of our or their subjectivity, and as subjects of surrounding things and persons, which are here taken exactly as they are there as "surrounding" for the subject. Then these things are not natural-scientific objects — apart from the case where the theoretically posited subjects are researchers of nature, and so are thought as related to the nature "investigated" by them, "determining" it, searching for its objective predicates. But when the nature-researching subjects are objects, the investigated nature is put in brackets. Apart from this case the things are correlates of the given experiences; they are the things that we see, grasp, touch, and as we and as others see and grasp them. If the others see them differently, the differently seen are, precisely as seen by them, their correlate-things, and if they are ghosts, then ghosts. If, to say a word more about the excluded case, the theoretically posited subjects are posited as investigating psychophysical and physical nature, then of course this comes to be posited: that they perform the nature-investigating acts, that their appearances are in a certain way constitutive for "objective nature," that they recognize dependencies between objective possessions of theirs, sensations, acts and the things of nature. But then objective nature is bracketed; it is now not itself theoretically posited but theoretically posited by the theoretically posited ego. Here, in particular, the theoretical object human being included in the theoretical positing of nature — the zoological, physiological, psychological object — is other than the theoretical object human personality. The natural object human being is not a subject, a person, but to every such object there corresponds a person; so we can also say that each "implies" a person, an ego-subject, which however is never a piece of nature, something contained as a reality in nature, but something that expresses itself in the surrounding-object "human body" — the human body being here merely the correlate of the positing of the subject, taken purely as that, something of which we know that it announces a nature which is objectively investigable but which now does not undergo this theoretical positing. The apprehension may be there, but it is not the point of passage for a theoretical positing. The bracketing of nature within the human-scientific attitude is the operation the whole chapter has been preparing: the human sciences can take even the natural scientist as their object, provided his nature is taken as his correlate, and the two theoretical objects "human being" and "human personality" never coincide.

In the same way the subject can be apprehended simultaneously as nature; but this apprehension, which points back to an apprehending subject that is not in turn apprehended as nature, is not the point of passage for a theoretical positing; so posited is the subject simply, which has behind it only the pure subject (288). A long note appended here is meaning-bearing. According to our presentations, it says, the concepts I–we are relative: the ego demands the you, the we, the "other," and further demands the relation to a world of things, so that I, we and the world belong together, the world as common surrounding world bearing the stamp of subjectivity. All science belongs in this context; it is an intersubjective activity, a research in the attitude "we and reality," which seeks objective validity and as objective science makes statements about the world simply, statements in which nothing is said of the we but only of the objective, first and lowest of physical nature. But in doing so we find ourselves constantly, and the natural scientist finds himself even where he investigates nature, living as a person in the personal world, his life-world (Lebenswelt): only that he is theoretically directed exclusively at physical or zoological nature. The apprehension as nature thus subordinates itself to the personal apprehension. Researching, I can be directed at mere things, but also at persons in relation to things — either at persons as spiritually determined by things, motivated by them as experienced and valued, or at persons with respect to their real dependence on things as natures, the things being then apprehended doubly, as correlate of the subject's relation and as correlate of natural-scientific knowledge. The persons are in the one case theoretically posited as they are given, as persons in the association of persons, in the other posited as natures, dependent on the nature of the body. This note contains what may be the earliest use of "life-world" in Husserl's published work, and already in its mature sense: the personal world in which the scientist lives while theorizing, to which his theoretical apprehension is subordinate.

### §63. Psychophysical Parallelism and Interaction

We now take into consideration what we call physical nature and, further, objective nature in general, the correlate of natural science, which is for its part an interpersonal cultural accomplishment in the association of persons and so presupposes it, just as human science is such a cultural accomplishment (288–289). The one absolute consciousness, a plurality of communicating monads and pure egos, is thus by essence so constituted that it has a parallel unity of regulation — a parallel regulation, and yet not a mere double regulation that orders the same thing twice without changing anything in the matters.

The one regulation is the natural-scientific, the other the human-scientific (289). Both complement and interpenetrate one another. But they would be merely two sides of one and the same thing, expressing the same thing in both sides, only if all the connections that appear under the title of human-scientific fact also turned up in natural science, merely in another apprehension. That is not the case. The parallelism the section examines is thus denied at the outset in its strong form: the two regulations do not order the same content.

The appearing thing is a unity of spatio-temporal causality; one can say it is what it effects in space, and its states are states of force (289).

The personal individual is likewise a unity: the human being is one and the same in relation to the various circumstances according to which his states change (289). That says, in "absolute consideration," that dependencies obtain between the human body and other things — both of which lead, in intersubjective consideration, to certain contexts of appearance — which are not physical. If, namely, in a consciousness standing in an intersubjective context of understanding, things are to be able to appear as concordant actualities, then sensation-data must belong to the appearances, and if these are to be able to be there, bodies must be able to appear and must have their physical actuality. This regulation therefore concerns groups of appearances and is intersubjective. Sensation-data can occur only if "in objective actuality" there are sense organs, nervous systems; to this correspond possible apprehensions of sensuous data and the corresponding intersubjective regulations. Can it now have any sense to assume that the possibility of every sort of apprehension, and of all consciousness whatever, is dependent on the body and its bodily-objective occurrences — that apperceptions, position-takings of belief, of will and so on are dependent on the body in the same sense as sensation-data? If we think of monadic subjects and their streams of consciousness, or rather think of the thinkable minimum of self-consciousness, a monadic consciousness would be thinkable that had no "world" given at all, hence without the corresponding regulations in the occurrence of sensations, without motivated possibilities of thingly apprehension. What is then needed for an ego-consciousness in the ordinary sense to occur? A human consciousness, of course, requires an appearing body and an intersubjective body — an intersubjective understanding. The question of parallelism is here set up as a question about the reach of a regulation whose existence for sensations is not in doubt: whether it can be extended from the hyletic to the noetic.

Let us presuppose already a plurality of subjects communicating intersubjectively, and so a certain "objectively" constituted world with "objective" things, bodies, real and objective spirits (290). A certain relation of sensations to the given objective body is thereby already presupposed. Suppose it to be as it actually is: my body a system of sense organs related to the central organ C, on which the occurrence of sensations and sensuous phantasms depends.

The question is then whether not only these sensuous contents but also, in the same or a similar sense, all apprehensions and higher functions of consciousness can be dependent on C — whether and to what extent such a dependence is thinkable (290). Dependence means, for sensations: a certain bodily state, or rather a certain form of bodily states — with regard to the metabolism which cancels the individual identity of the elements of the same organ, the same nerves, ganglia, but maintains a certain form — has a certain sensation, in a determinate stream of consciousness bound to the body in question, as its univocal and objective consequence. Can the like hold for all components and moments occurring in the stream of consciousness?

The consciousness of the world constitutes itself in appearances, more closely in appearances of the body (290–291). Sensations occur in certain apprehensions, in certain intersubjective regulations, and to these belongs in particular the regulation corresponding to the objective actuality of those hidden bodily organs we call the central organ C, nerve tracts, sensory nerves. And now the occurrence of every sensation E in my consciousness B, or in a determinate individual consciousness B of the human being M, is to be dependent on that part of this regulation which is called a determinate state of my C, the C of the individual M. Insofar as the sensations enter into other experiences of consciousness as constitutive moments, these would all be dependent on C and its states.

The same holds for phantasms (291). If now all sensations and phantasms within B are dependent in this way on the designated rule of apprehended and apprehensible sensations, on this moreover intersubjective group of possible perceptions and so of possible theoretical judgments, why should not all experiences within a monadic consciousness B be able to depend on just such a group C, intersubjective when taken as a whole, and so for every other consciousness? Why should that be a difficulty, if psychophysical causation is simply conceived as a certain relation of functional regulations, as we have done here and must do? The candor of the question is the point: once causation has been reduced to a correlation of regulations, nothing in the concept of causation itself blocks a universal parallelism, and the objection must come from elsewhere.

The main point to be weighed here is whether the essence of consciousness, which expresses itself a priori in essential laws, raises an objection to such a universal regulation (291). The essence of consciousness in general does make claims, demands. Can the matter, for instance, be so understood that the brain-states, the states of C, precede the corresponding experiences objectively in time, or must brain-state and its companion in consciousness be simultaneous on grounds of principle, in the absolute sense of simultaneity? Is a parallelism not thereby given eo ipso? Namely thus: to every experience in my consciousness B there corresponds a certain state in my C, a certain organic state; on the other side, to all and everything that makes up C there correspond real occurrences of a certain kind in every subject, and so in mine too certain real possibilities of perception — if not of this C itself, then of something else that stands in natural-scientific connection with it.

The opposite conception would be that such a universal regulation does not obtain or cannot obtain; that rather only the sensuous data in B have determinate states of C belonging to them, but not the noetic in the wider sense of consciousness, whether in general or within certain limits (292). And that again can mean either that this noetic is contingent, occurs lawlessly, is not univocally determined in advance; or that it is univocally determined but stands in no functional dependence on, or parallel relation to, the physical body and so to physical being in general. If univocality obtains, then the consciousness in question, apart from its hyletic stock, can be determined by rules of inner dependence that prescribe what, given certain states already present and given sensation-data occurring in them in these and those psychophysical dependencies, must further be present and occur in consciousness.

Empathy too would have to be drawn in, by virtue of which, in the individual consciousness B with its psychophysically determined stock of sensations, a simple interpretation onto foreign psychic life takes place, not prescribed by psychophysical laws — foreign psychic life that, given as existing, now motivatingly determines the further psychic life of B: an "acting-into" of one subjectivity upon the other, mediated by comprehension (292). A direct acting-into is excluded; all acting-into presupposes that B has within itself consciousness of the foreign psychic life, and this consciousness is what is immediately "effective" within B. But this effect of soul on soul, of subject on subject, is constantly mediated psychophysically in a wholly different way; psychophysical causality always plays along, but with the succor of peculiar, purely psychic laws of nature. These would be recognizable only in intersubjective experience, self-experience included — through knowledge of the general manner in which experiences of consciousness constitute individualities in the "monads." These general empirical cognitions would have their connection with the eidetic general laws that belong to consciousness in general and to the constitution of individuality in consciousness in general. The passage sketches a psychology that is neither psychophysics nor phenomenology: empirical laws of how monads constitute individualities, bound to eidetic laws above and to psychophysical causality below.

These are possibilities weighed in general (293). Into the frame of such considerations falls also the following important problem: in fact bodies occur scattered in nature, for so we regard nature; but the possibility would have to be thought through of what would belong to it, or whether and to what extent the possibility would obtain, that every physical thing in nature is a body.

The decisive question is now to what extent the essential in consciousness assigns limits to admissible possibilities (293). It is an absolutely indubitable truth that there are essential laws of consciousness. Is a thoroughgoing psychophysical parallelism compatible with them — one that prescribes for every state of consciousness B, in lawful fashion, a dependence on C or a parallel in it?

The following misgiving presses itself upon us, whose cogency we must consider (294). The changes of C are factual changes; they stand under laws of nature that could also be otherwise. Suppose now that all experiences of consciousness, just as they are, in their whole content of parts and moments, depend on C and are so long as C exists. If now, as belonging to the a priori essence of consciousness, certain necessities of succession obtain — as, for instance, the modes of retention belonging to the constitution of time are a priori tied, in necessary successions, to any impressions whatever — then these connections of succession could not be conditioned by C and the successions of its objective states. Only what the essential connections leave open can be empirically conditioned. The sensation, say, could be conditioned, but not what attaches to it by way of retentions; or perhaps more exactly, a content of the sensation, and likewise, within the prescribed form of the retentional succession, a content left open by it, differences of clarity and distinctness and the like, in which respect it is questionable and to be determined by experience how far it is psychophysically conditioned. And in any case we can say: if a priori a modification of consciousness is excluded, if a priori a law of incompatibility obtains by which B′ and B″ are incompatible in one consciousness whatever, then what is bound up with B′, which we think as dependent on C′, is no longer determined purely by a C″ bound up with C′, or by any play of C-states at all; an absolutely rigid lawfulness obtains that has no parallel in the empirical lawfulness of C. This is the refutation: the time-consciousness of the 1905 lectures supplies necessities of succession that no contingent natural law can underwrite, and wherever an a priori necessity governs consciousness, the parallel must fail, because the brain's laws are contingent and consciousness's are not.

It is also to be pointed out that every experience has its background, its surroundings in coexistence, just as it has its surroundings in sinking down into the past, whereby it constitutes itself in sinking down as a unity of the living past (294). These are a priori relations; it is so and cannot be otherwise. Accordingly we have many phenomena which it would be nonsense to reduce to causal dependence on C-states. A note adds a further consideration Husserl himself calls questionable: it would be thinkable that there were no bodies at all and no dependencies of consciousness on material occurrences in constituted nature, hence no empirical soul either, while absolute consciousness remained as something that simply cannot be struck out; it would then have in itself a principle of factual unity, its own rule according to which it ran off with its own content; connected with a body it may become dependent, but it keeps its own principle of unity, so that it becomes dependent together with its own a priori and individual essence, which means that what is in it is conditioned but cannot be a mere epiphenomenon of another.

With such grounds one can, it seems to me, radically refute parallelism, and the refutation has a wholly different style from the usual refutations, which operate with skewed concepts of causality and substance and with traditional prejudices of many kinds and steer straight for interaction — as if the question parallelism or interaction were the radical and complete one (294). With the rejection of parallelism nothing at all is yet decided for interaction.

That is not even the next thing; rather it matters first to determine how far the dependence of B on C reaches (295). Doubtless as far as the sensuous substrate of consciousness. Certainly the higher, properly noetic consciousness becomes co-dependent on C insofar as it is founded by the hyletic. Certainly not only the sensuous sensations in the narrower sense but also the sensuous feelings and drive-experiences are determined by the body. Certainly a good piece of individuality belongs here too, namely the sensuous dispositions with their individual habitus. How far all that goes can of course be decided only empirically, and if possible by experimental psychology. In particular, whether and to what extent the peculiarity, the rhythm of higher consciousness — both according to what is generally human but not essentially lawful, and according to what runs within the human type, the type of the species, and the individual type — is determined by empirical-psychological rules of its own, or whether these regularities in the typical and the individual are sufficiently grounded in the physical organization with mere recourse to the essential laws: that cannot in any way be settled a priori. The a priori argument having done its work, the empirical question is left open with an unusual generosity: even the typical and individual rhythm of higher consciousness might be physiologically grounded, so long as it is not essentially lawful.
