Now to the question of interaction, and in particular of the effect of consciousness, or rather of the soul, on the body (295). One may of course not operate with mythical concepts of effecting. What "effecting" means in the physical world is sufficiently clear: we are led back to lawfully regulated contexts of dependence among physical occurrences, and since physical things are what they are as unities of appearances, to dependencies of certain intersubjective regulations of consciousness of one and the same type on one another. When we speak of "effects" with respect to the dependencies of sensations, sensuous feelings and so on, that is a type of dependence of an entirely different kind: the occurrence of sensations in a monad is dependent on a physical body in its natural surroundings, and so on a certain subjective and further intersubjective regulation of appearances, and not merely of sensations. It is certain that every existence of sensations points back to something bodily, and since sensations and experiences in general have no objective temporal existence except through relation to the intersubjective body, one is already led to the conclusion that the relation between sensation and bodiliness must be thought as simultaneity — that the sensation must be assigned the objective time of the objective brain-process in question.

To speak of a reverse dependence makes no sense unless we assume a causality of its own, an inner empirical lawfulness of the soul in the production of sensations, which could first run off in itself and lead to a sensation to which the dependent state of C could then attach — as, say, in the voluntary production of a hallucination, but also in the voluntary production of sensations that order themselves into certain thing-appearances, namely in the voluntary movement of bodily members (296). It is questionable whether one can ever decide empirically whether an empirical succession obtains here or not, or whether the objective time-point of the brain-excitation belonging to the movement of the hand must not count as the identical time-point of the sensation. Everything here depends on how the time-point of a determinate state of consciousness is to be defined, and so on the question of the sense of a temporal determination of an experience of consciousness. Interaction is thus neither affirmed nor denied but shown to hinge on a prior problem: what it means to assign an objective time to an experience at all, given that experiences have objective time only through the body.

For the rest, what was already set out elsewhere comes into consideration, with a reference to 266ff. (296–297). If I can enter into understanding with an individual, see through him entirely, then I know how he will let himself be motivated, but no differently from the way I know it in my own case. Of course I say many things about what I would do in a given case, because I do not make the circumstances fully clear to myself and so leave much out of account. I also cannot say with full determinacy what I would do under future circumstances of this or that kind, because by then much will have run off in my consciousness that extends motivatingly into the subsequent time and so comes into force with the rest. The future doubtless cannot be firmly predicted, only hypothetically, with the supplementation of an intermediate piece. The past, by contrast, can be understood in clear memory, and every motivational connection in it brought to understanding. To every course of consciousness belongs an individuality that can be understood, "felt after," as one to which just these and no other modes of motivation belong; in this each differs from every other. On the other hand that does not mean univocal necessity of the sequence. Everywhere nature-causality plays along, the bodily and what is determined by bodiliness in consciousness; these determinacies need by no means be univocal. C can be a necessary but not a sufficient precondition; if one adds it, the connection is, as one will say, a necessary one. The section ends by relocating the question of determinism from causation to understanding: a person is fully intelligible in retrospect and only hypothetically predictable, and the brain is granted the status of a necessary condition that completes a motivational necessity rather than replacing it.

### §64. Relativity of Nature, Absoluteness of Spirit

From the foregoing considerations the limit of possible naturalization emerges: the spirit can be grasped as dependent on nature and itself naturalized, but only up to a certain degree (297). A univocal determination of spirit through merely natural dependencies, a reduction to something like physical nature, something analogous in its whole mode of determination with the goal of natural-scientific univocal determination, is unthinkable. Subjects cannot be exhausted in being nature, since then what gives nature its sense would be lacking. Nature is a field of thoroughgoing relativities and can be so because these are always relative to an absolute, which thus carries all the relativities: the spirit. The section's title is argued in two sentences, and the argument is transcendental in form: nature's relativity is intelligible only as relativity to something, and what it is relative to cannot itself be one of the relative things.

The spirit is determined by its surrounding world and also has a natural regulation, insofar as, set in relation to the nature that constitutes itself in relation to the personal world, it shows dependencies of many kinds (297). But that does not prevent its being absolute, irrelative. If we strike all spirits out of the world, there is no more nature. But if we strike out nature, the "true," objective-intersubjective existence, something still remains: the spirit as individual spirit; only the possibility of sociality is lost, the possibility of a comprehension that presupposes a certain intersubjectivity of the body. We then no longer have the individual spirit as a person in the narrower, social sense, related to a material and thereby also to a personal world. But with all the enormous impoverishment of "personal" life we still have an ego with its life of consciousness, and in that too it has its individuality, its way of judging, valuing, letting itself be motivated in its position-takings. The thought experiment is Ideas I §49's annihilation of the world, restated for the person: what survives is not a bare residuum but an individual ego with a style, though not a social person.

In the course of consciousness of the spirit its unity, its individuality, in any case announces itself (298). If I want to understand it, I must follow this course, must reconstruct it; if I want to understand it in a certain respect, I must see out the corresponding connections in its course of consciousness. The understanding is thoroughly intuitive; this objectivity, the "individual," comes here to givenness in its own proper being.

I re-live the foreign spiritual life and with it the foreign spiritual world, the foreign spiritual objectivity, and understand it in its individual or spiritual significance: the political situation, the spiritual epoch, the contemporary literature (298).

It must be noted here that individuality in the spiritual sense is something wholly other than natural individuality (298). The thing has its individual essence as what is here and now; but this what is itself a "universal." That is to say, every thing is an exemplar of a generality, and so already the thing at the level of mere, concordantly thought "sensuous" experience; each is to be thought as repeated arbitrarily often. Likewise physical objectivity is what it is, and in every determination, as existing under mathematically formulable laws and in mathematically formulable determinations.

The thing as real is dependent on real circumstances; it is what it is in the context of real nature, which is constituted in itself as thoroughly homogeneous (298).

The purely objective consideration, which pursues the objective sense of thinghood, requires that things be dependent on one another with respect to their states, that they reciprocally prescribe something to one another in their real existence, and indeed with respect to their content of being, their causal states (298).

Is a thing, then, which is under all circumstances one thing, an identical of properties, really in itself something fixed and rigid with respect to its real properties — an identical, the identical subject of identical properties, while what changes in it is only the states and circumstances (299)? Is the opinion this: according to the circumstances into which it is brought, or into which it can ideally be thought, it has other actual states, but in advance, a priori, it is prescribed by its own essence how it can behave and then also will behave? But does every thing — or, what says the same here, does any thing — have such an essence of its own at all? Or is the thing, so to speak, always on the march; is it not to be grasped at all in this pure objectivity, but rather, by virtue of its relation to subjectivity, in principle only something relatively identical, something that does not have its essence in advance, or have it as graspable once and for all, but has an open essence that can take on new properties again and again according to the constitutive circumstances of givenness? But there is the problem of specifying more exactly the sense of this openness, and specifically for the "objectivity" of natural science. The question is put against the substance-ontology of §§15–17: the thing was there defined by a fixed schema of causal properties, and here that fixity is queried in the name of the thing's relation to the subjectivity that constitutes it.

Does the "infinity" of the world, instead of a transfinite infinity — as if the world were a finished being in itself, an all-encompassing thing or closed collective of things that nonetheless contained an infinity of things — not rather mean an "openness" (299)? But what is to be meant by that?

If we think nature, as we can, altered in manifold ways, a nature is always thinkable too in which there were several equal things, and things of any content whatever that is only representable (299). Several things completely equal in properties and causal states are thinkable, both in coexistence and in succession: the one here, the other there, the one now, the other then. It is also thinkable that a thing periodically returns to identically the same state. What distinguishes two equal things is the real-causal context, which presupposes the here and now. And with that we are necessarily referred back to an individual subjectivity, whether single or intersubjective, in relation to which alone determinacy of the positing of place and time constitutes itself. No thing has its individuality in itself. The Leibnizian identity of indiscernibles is denied for things and, in the next unit, affirmed for spirits: things are individuated by a here and now that is the subject's, so their individuality is borrowed.

The spirit, however, is an experiencing, position-taking, motivated spirit; every spirit has its way of motivation, it has, unlike the thing, its motivation in itself (299–300). It is not individuality first through being at a determinate place in the world. Absolute individuation belongs already to the pure ego of the given cogitatio, which is itself something absolutely individual in itself. But the ego is not an empty pole; it is the bearer of its habituality, and therein lies that it has its individual history. The pure ego of §§22–24, which there was a bare pole, is here given habituality and a history — the doctrine that the Cartesian Meditations will call the ego as substrate of habitualities.

The experiences in the flow of consciousness have their absolutely own essence; they carry their individuation in themselves (300). Can experiences be equal — can they be absolutely equal in one flow of consciousness, differing merely by thisness? And can two experiences differ merely in that the one belongs to this, the other to that consciousness, monad? An experience now and "the same" experience afterwards, "merely repeated" — can that be the same in its whole essential stock? In the now, consciousness has an originary stock of experience and a horizon of the past, which is represented in the now in the form of an experiential horizon of "primary memory," of retention in its continuous transformations into one another. Is this medium of experience indifferent for the originarily occurring experience, say a new sensation-datum? If not, we already have a difference in it, for afterwards this medium is another. But does it not remain the case, one might object, that every experience together with its horizon, in its full concretion, is thinkable as repeated? Certainly, we answer: I think it repeated; but I necessarily think it as the same individual, it is thought as one and the same, as something that occurs once.

We can also frame the problem thus: is a separation to be made anywhere between the full, concrete essence and individual existence (300)? Or conversely: is this difference not to be made a priori and necessarily everywhere, saying that in principle full equality of individuals in the sphere of experience is quite possible, every experience being "idealiter" an essential stock that has its thisness, which is no quality? But is not haecceity too a universal, insofar as every experience has its haecceity in itself? But it is not to be asked at all what distinguishes one this from another this purely as this, and again what both have "in common." That already confuses quality and this; so too when we speak of the "essence of thisness." Thisness is a form. The scholastic term is used to block a scholastic move: haecceity is not a shared property, and asking what two thisnesses have in common is a category mistake.

What distinguishes this "something" from another "something" as "something" (301)? "Nothing," insofar as there are no qualities, no stocks of content, that make the difference. The form of the this is no whatness and in this sense no essence; it is universal in the sense of the form.

Consciousness has an essence of its own, a flowing one that cannot be exactly determined; but as an idea an "exact" essence is to be assigned to it, and with the positing it receives its determinate this (301).

Objective thinghood determines itself physically, but as a this it determines itself only in relation to consciousness and the subject of consciousness (301). All determination points back to a here and now and thereby to some subject or contexts of subjects.

The only originally individual is consciousness, concretely with its ego (301). Everything else individual is something appearing and has the principle of its individuation in actual and possible appearing, which for its part points back to an individual consciousness.

Absolute individuation enters into the personal ego (301). The surrounding world of the ego gains its individuation essentially through relation to the ego that has experience of it and exchanges its experience with other individuals; for each, some thing has the here and now in the correlate of intuition. The surrounding world constitutes an ego, or an intersubjectivity, for itself, and if the ego lets itself be determined by its counterpart in the surrounding world, or for its part actively and perhaps formatively determines that counterpart, the counterpart still has the secondary individuation of the counterpart, while in the ego itself lies the originary, absolute individuation. The same spirit cannot be twice, cannot even return to the same total state and show its individuation merely in standing with the same content in different contexts.

Spirits are precisely not unities of appearances but unities of absolute contexts of consciousness, more exactly ego-unities (302). And appearances are correlates of contexts of consciousness that have their absolute being. And if appearances are intersubjectively constituted, we are led back precisely to a plurality of persons who can communicate with one another. A correlate as such has its hold in the persons and their experiences, and their absolute being precedes the relative being of the appearances. All individuation of the latter hangs on the absolute individuation of the former, all natural existence on the existence of absolute spirits. The ontological priority named in the chapter's title is here stated as a priority of individuation and of being at once, and it is the priority of persons, not of a transcendental consciousness in general — the difference that separates this section's "absolute" from that of Ideas I.

But how does it stand with the soul and the human being as nature (302)? Is that too merely an exemplar of a universal? To this one will have to answer: insofar as the soul is naturalized spirit and specific individuality belongs to the spirit, no. On the other side one will have to say: everything by which the soul determines itself as a natural reality is exemplary and general; individuality does not lie in what is here nature. Nature is the X, and in principle nothing other than an X that determines itself through general determinations. The spirit, however, is no X but what is given itself in spiritual experience. The book closes on the contrast that the whole Third Section has prepared: nature, from the physical thing to the naturalized soul, is an empty subject of general determinations, an X; the spirit is the one thing that is given as itself, in its own individuality, and that is why it can be understood and nature can only be determined.

-----

## Supplements (Beilagen)

The volume contains fourteen supplements (Beilagen I–XIV) drawn from various manuscript sources, dating from 1912 to approximately 1928. These supplements amplify, deepen, and in some cases provide alternative treatments of themes from the main text. They include: an attempt at a stepwise description of constitution (Beilage I); the ego as pole and the ego of habitualities (Beilage II); localization of ringing in the ear (Beilage III); a draft introduction to the “Constitution of the Spiritual World” (Beilage IV); the pre-givennesses of spirit in spiritual life (Beilage V); the inspectio sui — “I do” and “I have” (Beilage VI); the ego and its counterpart (Beilage VII); on the unity of “body” and “spirit” (Beilage VIII); spiritual products (Beilage IX); the personal ego and surrounding world, the levels of constitution of objective reality, pure ego and personal ego (Beilage X); the inductively-naturally apprehended human being and the free personality (Beilage XI); supplements to the third section of the second book, divided into two major parts — (I) the person, the spirit and its psychical foundation, and (II) subjectivity as soul and as spirit in natural-scientific and human-scientific attitude (Beilage XII); “personal subjectivity” as theoretical theme (Beilage XIII); and the human-scientific attitude with natural science placed within it, covering the different kinds of intuitive causality, abstract-scientific research, natural science within human science, and the concept of objectivity (Beilage XIV).

These supplements extend and deepen the analyses of the main text, particularly regarding the constitution of the person, the distinction between naturalistic and personalistic (human-scientific) attitudes, the relationship between soul and spirit, the role of the body in spiritual life, and the ontological priority of spirit over nature. They represent materials spanning the full period of Husserl’s work on these problems from 1912 to 1928, and provide crucial additional perspectives on the central themes of the *Ideen II*.
